are given by

\displaystyle P_{n}(\tau)=\sum_{N=1}^{n-1}p_{N}\tau^{2N},

(4.12)

\displaystyle p_{N}=\frac{a}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\frac{2a}{\varepsilon b}\right)^{k}\sum_{\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{N}\}\in M_{k,N}}\frac{(m_{1}+\ldots+m_{N})!}{m_{1}!\cdot\ldots\cdot m_{N}!}\prod_{l=1}^{N}(b_{2l+1,0})^{m_{l}},

(4.13)

where the summation set
M_{k,N}
consists of the sets of numbers
m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
,
i=1,2,\ldots,N
, that
solve the system

\begin{gathered}m_{1}+\ldots+m_{i}+\ldots+m_{N}=k,\\
m_{1}+\ldots+im_{i}+\ldots+Nm_{N}=N.\end{gathered}

(4.14)

Proof.

If
s_{0}^{\infty}=0
, then the relation (
3.1
) implies the equation for
s_{0}^{0}
given in the
list (
4.2
).
Equation (
2.4
) is equivalent to the condition
g_{11}=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\pi a}g_{21}
; but,
equations (
2.3
) and (
2.6
), together with the assumption
a\neq\mathrm{i}k
,

k\in\mathbb{Z}
, exclude the possibility
g_{11}=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{\pi a}g_{21}
, so one arrives at the second equation in the
list (
4.2
).
Note that, because of the aforementioned condition on
g_{11}
and equation (
2.6
),

g_{11}g_{21}\neq 0
.
Choose
g_{21}\neq 0
and
s_{1}^{\infty}g_{21}^{2}
as the parameters defining the solution

(u(\tau),\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)})
. Substituting
g_{11}=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}g_{21}
into
equation (
2.6
) and dividing both sides of the resulting equation by
g_{21}
, one gets a linear
equation with respect to
g_{12}
and
g_{22}
. The second linear equation with respect to these co-ordinates is
obtained analogously via equation (
2.5
): using the formula
s_{0}^{0}=2\mathrm{i}\cosh(\pi a)
, the left-hand
side of equation (
2.5
) can be factorised as two linear forms with respect to
g_{12}
and
g_{22}
,
wherein one of these forms coincides with the left-hand side of the linear equation with respect to
g_{12}
and

g_{22}
already derived using equation (
2.6
) and, therefore, can be removed with the help of
this linear equation.
Thus, solving the obtained linear system (its discriminant is non-vanishing provided
a\neq\mathrm{i}k
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
),
one arrives at the last two equations in the list (
4.2
).

For
s_{0}^{\infty}=0
, it follows that
\varrho=n+1\pm\mathrm{i}a/2
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}

(cf. equation (
3.1
)). As a consequence of the symmetry discussed in Remark
3.4
,
we can, without loss of generality, assume that

\varrho=n+1+\mathrm{i}a/2
.
23
23

23

Note that, because of this choice for
\varrho
,
the reflection symmetry
\varrho\to 1-\varrho
in the asymptotics (
4.3
) and
(
4.4
) (cf. equations (
4.5
) and (
4.6
)) is lost.

Also, note that
\varrho\neq 1/2
because
a\notin\mathrm{i}\mathbb{Z}
.

We now proceed to the proof of item
(1)
of the theorem, that is, assume
\mathrm{Im}\,a>0
and

n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
.
Substituting
\varrho=n+1+\mathrm{i}a/2
and the expression for
g_{11}
in the list (
4.2
)
into equation (
3.10
) for
w_{2}
, one verifies equation (
4.6
) for
\hat{w}_{2}
. The derivation of
equation (
4.5
) for
\hat{w}_{1}
is not as straightforward, because substituting the same expressions
for
\varrho
and
g_{11}
into equation (
3.9
) for
w_{1}
gives rise to the appearance of the term

\Gamma(-n)
as the value of the right-most
\Gamma
-function, which, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, is its
valuation at the pole
-n
.
At the same time, though, the right-most
(parenthetical) term in equation (
3.9
), which consists of the linear combination of the monodromy data,
vanishes for the monodromy data (
4.2
); thus, we get an indeterminate
expression. This indeterminacy can be resolved in several ways:

(i)
the simplest way is to use Theorem
3.1
′
.
The key point here is to note that the coefficients
w_{3}
and
w_{4}
are finite for the monodromy
data (
4.2
); therefore, for this set of monodromy data and
\mathrm{Im}\,a>0
, one
can use the asymptotics (
3.13
) and (
3.14
) without any modifications. These
asymptotics resemble (as functions of
\tau
) those stated in equations (
4.3
) and
(
4.4
); however, the coefficients
w_{3}
and
w_{4}
, calculated for the monodromy
data (
4.2
), look different than the coefficients
\hat{w}_{1}
and
\hat{w}_{2}
presented
in equations (
4.5
) and (
4.6
). To get the exact correspondence, one has to use the
relations (
3.17
), which have an algebraic nature and hold for any parametrization(s) of the
coefficients
w_{k}
,
k=1,2,3,4
, in terms of the monodromy data;

(ii)
a modification of the approach suggested in item
(i)
, but using
Theorem
3.1
in lieu of Theorem
3.1
′
.
As discussed above, the parameter
w_{2}
in the asymptotics being calculated for the
monodromy data (
4.2
) coincides with
\hat{w}_{2}
given in equation (
4.6
),
while the formula for the parameter
w_{1}
for the monodromy data (
4.2
) does not give
rise to a definite result. To find the value of

w_{1}
for the said monodromy data, which is denoted as
\hat{w}_{1}
in equation (
4.5
), one can use,
again, the results of Proposition
3.1
, where any one of the
relations (
3.17
) can be employed for this purpose; and

(iii)
a direct resolution of the indeterminacy problem that provides an alternative
proof of equation (
4.5
). The standard method for
resolving such indeterminacies is to consider a proper limiting procedure, which we now proceed to outline.
Define a small parameter
\delta
via the equation
\varrho=1+n+\mathrm{i}a/2-\delta
. Substituting this expression for

\varrho
into the argument of the right-most
\Gamma
-function in equation (
3.9
), we get

\Gamma(-n+\delta)=\frac{(-1)^{n}\pi}{\Gamma(n+1-\delta)\sin(\pi\delta)}.

(4.15)

We have to take a limit along a curve on the monodromy manifold; therefore, we have to find infinitesimal

\mathcal{O}(\delta)
-corrections to the monodromy data at the point (
4.2
)
parametrized by
s_{1}^{\infty}
and
g_{21}
. Before doing so, however, we consider the right-most (parenthetical)
term in equation (
3.9
), where we denote by
\kappa
an infinitesimal correction to the parameter
g_{11}
,
namely,
g_{11}=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}g_{21}+\kappa
; then, after a straightforward calculation, we find that

g_{11}\mathrm{e}^{\pi\mathrm{i}/4}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\varrho}+g_{21}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}/4}\mathrm{e}^{\pi\mathrm{i}\varrho}=\kappa\mathrm{e}^{\pi\mathrm{i}/4+\pi a/2+\pi\mathrm{i}(n+1)}-2\pi\mathrm{i}\delta g_{21}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}/4-\pi a/2-\pi\mathrm{i}(n+1)}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2}).

(4.16)

To find the
\delta
-dependence of
\kappa
, we have to use
equations (
2.2
)–(
2.6
) defining the monodromy manifold and
equation (
3.1
) for
\varrho
. First, we find the perturbation of
the Stokes multipliers:

s_{0}^{0}=2\mathrm{i}\cosh(\pi a)-4\pi\delta\sinh(\pi a)+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2}),\qquad s_{0}^{\infty}=4\pi\mathrm{i}\sinh(\pi a)\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}\delta/s_{1}^{\infty}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2}).

(4.17)

With the help of equations (
4.17
), one finds

\kappa=\frac{4\pi\mathrm{i}\delta\sinh(\pi a)\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}g_{21}^{2}g_{22}}{1+2\mathrm{i}\sinh(\pi a)g_{21}g_{22}}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2}).

(4.18)

Substituting into equation (
4.18
) the formula for
g_{22}
given in
the list (
4.2
), one gets

\kappa=-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}\delta}{s_{1}^{\infty}g_{21}}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi a}+2\pi\delta g_{21}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2}).

(4.19)

Substituting the expression for
\kappa
given in equation (
4.19
) into
equation (
4.16
), we observe that the terms without
s_{1}^{\infty}
cancel! Taking this into
account together with equation (
4.15
),
we simplify equation (
3.9
), and, denoting by
\hat{w}_{1}
the special value of
w_{1}
corresponding to the
monodromy data (
4.2
), arrive at equation (
4.5
).

We now turn our attention to the proof of item
(2)
of the theorem. Here, we rely upon our basic result
as formulated in Appendix B of
[
17
]
and the local expansion studied in
Appendix
A
below.

Note that the case
0<\mathrm{Im}\,a<1
has already been considered in item
(1)
of the theorem;
but, the leading terms of the corresponding asymptotics look different, and
the correction term in item
(2)
is more precise than the one in item
(1)
.
So, our goal is to prove that the leading terms coincide, and to justify the correction term stated in
item
(2)
.

The case under consideration corresponds to
n=0
in the formula for the parameter
\varrho
given in
item
(1)
, so that
\varrho=1+\mathrm{i}a/2
; this formula implies that
\mathrm{Re}\,\varrho\in(1/2,1)
, which
means that
\tau^{1-2\varrho}>\tau^{-1+2\varrho}
.
Taking the last fact into account, we compare the asymptotics (
4.3
) and
(
4.4
) with the asymptotics (
4.8
) and
(
4.9
), respectively. Comparing these formulae, we find that the following relations hold:

\sigma=4(1-\varrho)
and
(1-2\varrho)^{2}\hat{w}_{2}/\hat{w}_{1}=b_{1,-1}
.
Both formulae can be validated with the help of equations (
4.5
)–(
4.7
).

The term proportional to
\tau
in the asymptotics (
4.3
) and the
\mathcal{O}(1)
term in
the asymptotics (
4.4
), which are explicitly written in the
asymptotics (
4.8
) and (
4.9
), respectively, are concealed in the
correction terms. To justify the correction term in the
asymptotics (
4.8
), we refer to the expansion (
A.1
), wherein, due to
the last relation in the list of equations (
A.2
) and the fact that
\sigma=-2\mathrm{i}a
, one has
to set
b_{1,1}=0
. This relation
does not hold for the solutions in item
(1)
with
n\geqslant 1
. Since
\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma>0
, the

\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{3}\big)
correction term in equation (
4.8
) can be omitted; in fact,
the largest correction term, i.e.,
\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{3-2\sigma})
, in the expansion (
A.1
)
is hidden in the denominator of the leading term (cf. the function
A_{0}(x)
in
Appendix
A.2
). For the function

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
, we also have a more precise formula for the correction term than the corresponding one
in item
(1)
: the leading term of this formula is special case of the asymptotic formula (B.5) in Theorem B.1
of
[
17
]
. The error corrections for
\varphi(\tau)
are obtained by substituting the
expansion (
A.1
) into equation (
2.1
) and integrating the resulting expansion.

Turning to the case
-1<\mathrm{Im}\,a\leqslant 0
, we find it convenient to refer to the description of the
asymptotics in terms of the parameter
\rho
. Since both
\rho
and
\varrho
solve the same
equation (
3.1
), we can present
\rho
as
\rho=1+n+\mathrm{i}a/2
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The integer
n=-1
because
|\mathrm{Re}\,\rho|<1/2
. Comparing the asymptotics given in Theorem B.1 of

[
17
]
with the expansion (
A.1
) and taking into account the symmetry of this
expansion with respect to the transformation
\sigma\to-\sigma
, we put
\sigma=-4\rho
and once again obtain the
relation
\sigma=-2\mathrm{i}a
. Then, according to the last equation in the list (
A.2
), one finds
that
b_{1,1}b_{1,-1}=0
. Equations (B.4) and (B.6)–(B.8) in Appendix B of
[
17
]
show that

b_{1,-1}
is given by equation (
4.7
), so that
b_{1,1}=0
. The asymptotic formula for

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
is a special case of the asymptotics (B.5) in Appendix B of
[
17
]
.
The corrections written in equation (
4.8
)
are based on the local expansion (
A.1
), where we have taken into account that
b_{1,1}=0
, and
therefore
b_{3,k}=0
for
k=1,2
. We now consider the derivation of these corrections more carefully.

If
\mathrm{Im}\,a=0
, then the first two explicitly written
\mathcal{O}(\tau)
terms represent the leading term of
asymptotics, while the correction is of the order
\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{3}\big)
. In this case, the denominator of
the first fraction in the asymptotics (
4.8
) can be omitted because its contribution is of the order

\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{3}\big)
.

If
-1<\mathrm{Im}\,a<0
, then
\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma<0
; this case, however, is more complicated.
The problem here is related with the error estimate, which is presented as the factor

\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{\delta}\big)\big)
that multiplies the leading term of asymptotics of the function

u(\tau)
(cf. equation (45) in Theorem 3.4 of
[
15
]

or equation (B.5) in Theorem B.1 of
[
17
]
), where the parameter
\delta>0
is not specified. In this case,
the
\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{1-\sigma}\big)
term of the asymptotics, which contains the monodromy parameters, may be
vying with the
\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{1+\delta}\big)
term (depending on the values of
\delta
and

|\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma|
),
so that it is not at all apparent as to whether or not the leading term of asymptotics contains the monodromy
parameters or they are hidden in the correction term. In fact, the
\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{1-\sigma}\big)
term
continues to contribute to the leading term of asymptotics, and its parametrization via the monodromy data
given in
[
15
,
17
]
is correct; however, this requires a separate justification.
There are three approaches for establishing this result: (i) to perform calculations similar to those in Section 5
of
[
15
]
for correspondingly modified assumptions on the coefficients of the
associated Fuchs-Garnier pair;
24
24

24

See the comments and corrections to
this calculation given in Appendix A of
[
19
]
.
(ii) use of Bäcklund transformations; or
(iii) analytic continuation with
respect to the parameter
a
. The calculational scheme of the proof delineated in item (i) requires lengthy
calculations, together with a fairly large array of auxiliary constructions parallel to those carried out
in
[
15
]
. In the
present proof (see below), we refer to the methodology of item (ii), while the proof proposed in item (iii) is
outlined in Remark
4.1
. As a matter of fact, we have already used a proof based
on Bäcklund transformations (cf. item (ii) above) in
[
18
]
to find asymptotics of
u(\tau)

for
a=-\mathrm{i}/2
: the underlying idea of this proof works without modification for the more general
situation
-1<\mathrm{Im}\,a<0
.

The proof consists of the following steps: first, note that if
0<\mathrm{Im}\,a<1
, then
-1<\mathrm{Im}(a-\mathrm{i})<0
;
second, one verifies that the monodromy data (
4.1
), (
4.2
)
are invariant under the action of Bäcklund transformations (cf. equations (
2.11
) and (
2.12
));
and third, apply the Bäcklund transformations (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) to the asymptotics (
4.8
) and
(
4.9
) of the functions
u(\tau)
and
\varphi(\tau)
, respectively, for

\mathrm{Im}\,a\in(0,1)
. These asymptotics are differentiable, so that, after elementary calculations and a
renotation for the monodromy variables, one arrives at the asymptotics (
4.8
) and
(
4.9
). Note that, if
\mathrm{Im}\,a\in(-1,0)
, then
\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma<0
, so that
the denominator in the first term of the asymptotics (
4.8
) can be neglected since its
contribution for small enough values of
\tau
is smaller than the
\mathcal{O}(\tau^{3})
correction term.
The corrections for the asymptotics of the function
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
are obtained by integrating
equation (
2.1
) with the help of the expansion (
A.1
).

The proof for the asymptotics presented in item
(3)
of the theorem for
n-1<-\mathrm{Im}\,a<n
is also based
on the application of Bäcklund transformations. It is very similar to the proof of item
(3)
in
Theorem
4.2
below; therefore, the reader familiar with the proof for the asymptotics
stated in item
(3)
of Theorem
4.2
should not expect to encounter any complications
with the proof of item
(3)
of Theorem
4.1
.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

In this remark, we outline another proof for the asymptotics of the solution stated in item
(2)
of
Theorem
4.1
for
\mathrm{Im}\,a\in(-1,0)
. This proof is based on the analytic
continuation of the solution with respect to the parameter
a
.

In the complex
a
-plane, we denote by
\mathcal{D}
the strip
|\mathrm{Im}\,a|\leqslant 1
punctured at
a=0
;
in fact, we will work with the compactified strip

\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}:=\left\{a\in\mathbb{C}:|\mathrm{Im}\,a|\leqslant 1-\epsilon,\,|a|\geqslant\epsilon,\,\epsilon\in(0,1/2)\right\}.

Then, for any fixed parameter
s_{1}^{\infty}g_{21}^{2}
, we define, with the help of the convergent
series (
A.1
), where
b_{1,-1}
and
\sigma
are given in equations (
4.7
),
the function
u_{a}(\tau)
.
The compactified domain
\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
is necessary in order to guarantee that all functions

u_{a}(\tau)
(considered as functions of
\tau
) for
a\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
have a non-empty common domain
of definition in some cut (along the negative real semi-axis) neighbourhood of
\tau=0
.
Note that the functions
u_{a}(\tau)
are single-valued for
a\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
because the coefficients of
the expansion (
A.1
) are single-valued in
\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
. We denote by

u_{a}^{\pm}(\tau)
the functions
u_{a}(\tau)
for
\pm\mathrm{Im}\,a>0
.
As explained in the proof of Theorem
4.1
, the expansion (
A.1
) is
different for the functions
u_{a}^{\pm}(\tau)
, but, for
\mathrm{Im}\,a=0
, these expansions coincide. According to
the Principle of Analytic Continuation, the functions
u_{a}^{\pm}(\tau)
are analytic continuations of one another;
however, for the function
u_{a}^{+}(\tau)
, we proved that it corresponds to the monodromy data
(
4.1
), (
4.2
); therefore, the same conclusion follows for
the function
u_{a}^{-}(\tau)
.

\blacksquare

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
(u(\tau),\varphi(\tau))
be a solution of the system (
1.1
), (
2.1
)
corresponding to the monodromy data
(a,s_{0}^{0},s_{0}^{\infty},s_{1}^{\infty},g_{11},g_{12},g_{21},g_{22})
.
Suppose that

a\neq\mathrm{i}k,\quad k\in\mathbb{Z},\qquad s_{1}^{\infty}=0,\quad\mathrm{and}\quad s_{0}^{\infty}\neq 0;

(4.20)

then,
g_{12}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
, and the remaining monodromy data are given by the following equations:

s_{0}^{0}=2\mathrm{i}\cosh(\pi a),\quad g_{11}=\frac{s_{0}^{\infty}g_{12}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}-\mathrm{i}}{2\sinh(\pi a)g_{12}},\quad g_{21}=-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\pi a}+\mathrm{i}s_{0}^{\infty}g_{12}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi a}}{2\sinh(\pi a)g_{12}},\quad g_{22}=-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}g_{12}.

(4.21)

(1)

Assume that

\mathrm{Im}\,a<0
, and define—uniquely—numbers
\varrho\in\mathbb{C}
, with
\mathrm{Re}\,\varrho\in(0,1)
, and

n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
such that
\varrho=1+n-\mathrm{i}a/2
, i.e.,

\lfloor{\mathrm{Im}\,a/2}\rfloor=-n-1
and
\mathrm{Re}\,\varrho=\{\mathrm{Im}\,a/2\}
, where
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
and

\{\cdot\}
denote, respectively, the floor and the fractional part of the real number; then,

\displaystyle u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon(1-2\varrho)^{2}\hat{w}_{3}\hat{w}_{4}\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{4\mathrm{Re}(\varrho)}\big)+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{4(1-\mathrm{Re}(\varrho))}\big)\big)}{\tau\big(\hat{w}_{3}\tau^{1-2\varrho}+\hat{w}_{4}\tau^{-1+2\varrho}\big)^{2}},

(4.22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi a}{2}}\frac{\hat{w}_{3}\hat{w}_{4}}{2\pi}\big(2\tau^{2}\big)^{\mathrm{i}a}\big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{4\mathrm{Re}(\varrho)}\big)+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{4(1-\mathrm{Re}(\varrho))}\big)\big),

(4.23)

where

\displaystyle\hat{w}_{3}=

\displaystyle\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(\varepsilon b)\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}/2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}-\varrho}\frac{2\pi}{n!}\frac{\Gamma(2\varrho)}{\Gamma(2-2\varrho)}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\pi\mathrm{i}/4+\pi a/2}}{s_{0}^{\infty}g_{12}},

(4.24)

\displaystyle\hat{w}_{4}=

\displaystyle\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(\varepsilon b)\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}/2}\right)^{\varrho-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\pi\mathrm{i}(1/4-\varrho)}\frac{\Gamma(2-2\varrho)}{\Gamma(2\varrho)}\Gamma(2\varrho-n-1)\,2\sinh(\pi a)\,g_{12};

(4.25)

(2)

Assume that

-1<\mathrm{Im}\,a<1
, and define

\sigma=-2\mathrm{i}a,\quad b_{1,1}=-\mathrm{i}\left(\frac{\varepsilon b}{2}\right)^{1-\mathrm{i}a}\frac{\pi\mathrm{e}^{-3\pi a/2}}{\sinh(\pi a)}\frac{s_{0}^{\infty}g_{12}^{2}}{\big(\Gamma(1-\mathrm{i}a)\big)^{3}};

(4.26)

then,

\displaystyle u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}

\displaystyle\;\frac{\varepsilon b_{1,1}\tau^{1+\sigma}}{\left(1+\frac{4b_{1,1}\tau^{2+\sigma}}{(\sigma+2)^{2}}\right)^{2}}-\frac{b\tau}{2a}+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{3+\sigma}\big)+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{3}\big),

(4.27)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}

\displaystyle\;-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\pi a}}{2\pi ag_{12}^{2}}\left(\!\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{3\pi a}{2}}\Gamma(1+\mathrm{i}a)s_{0}^{\infty}g_{12}^{2}\left(2\tau^{2}\right)^{-\mathrm{i}a}-\mathrm{i}\big(\Gamma(1-\mathrm{i}a)\big)^{2}\left(\frac{4}{\varepsilon b}\right)^{-\mathrm{i}a}\right)

\displaystyle\times\left(1+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2}\big)+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2-2\mathrm{i}a}\big)\right).

(4.28)

(3)

Assume that

n-1<\mathrm{Im}\,a<n
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, or
\mathrm{Im}\,a=(n-1)
and
\mathrm{Re}\,a\neq 0
.
Let
\sigma
and
b_{1,1}
be defined by equation (
4.26
), in particular,

2(n-1)\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma<2n
;
then,

\displaystyle\varepsilon u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}

\displaystyle\,\sum_{k=1}^{n}b_{2k-1,0}\tau^{2k-1}+b_{1,1}\tau^{1+\sigma}+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2n+1}\big),

(4.29)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{\pi a}\big(\Gamma(1-\mathrm{i}a)\big)^{2}}{2\pi ag_{12}^{2}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon b}{4}\right)^{\mathrm{i}a}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\left(P_{n}(\tau)+\frac{4a^{2}}{\varepsilon b}b_{1,1}\frac{\tau^{\sigma}}{\sigma}+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2n}\big)\right)\right),

(4.30)

where the coefficients
b_{2k-1,0}
,
k=1,\ldots,n
, are defined in
Appendix
A.1
,
25
25

25

See the expansion
(
A.1
) and footnote
22
.

and the polynomials
P_{n}(\tau)
are given in equations (
4.12
)–(
4.14
).

Proof.

The direct proof of this theorem is very similar to the proof of Theorem
4.1
; moreover,
there is a symmetry between the cases considered in these theorems, namely, the transformation

\begin{gathered}a\to-a,\quad s_{0}^{0}\to s_{0}^{0},\quad s_{0}^{\infty}\to-s_{1}^{\infty},\quad s_{1}^{\infty}\to-s_{0}^{\infty},\\
g_{11}\to g_{12},\quad g_{12}\to-g_{11},\quad g_{21}\to g_{22},\quad g_{22}\to-g_{21},\end{gathered}

(4.31)

maps the monodromy data satisfying the conditions (
4.1
) and
(
4.2
) to the monodromy data satisfying the
conditions (
4.20
) and (
4.21
).
The asymptotic results of Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
can be
derived from one another with the help of the symmetry (
4.31
);
therefore, for the proof of the asymptotic results presented in items
(1)
and
(2)
of this theorem,
we refer to the corresponding proof of Theorem
4.1
. Here, we prove the asymptotics given
in item
(3)
, which also completes the proof of item
(3)
of Theorem
4.1
.

There are two schemes for the proof of the asymptotics presented in item
(3)
; the first proof is based on
Bäcklund transformations, whilst the second is based on analytic continuation: here, we consider a proof that
uses Bäcklund transformations, and the second proof is outlined in Remark
4.2
below.

Firstly, note that the action of the Bäcklund transformations on the monodromy manifold
(cf. equations (
2.11
) and (
2.12
)) preserves the conditions (
4.20
) and
(
4.21
).

Secondly, the solutions corresponding to
n=1
in item
(3)
for
\mathrm{Im}\,a\in[0,1)
coincide with the
solutions considered in item
(2)
(cf. equations (
4.27
) and
(
4.28
)) for
\mathrm{Im}\,a\in[0,1)
.
For the solutions corresponding to
\mathrm{Im}\,a\in[0,1)
,
\sigma>0
, so that we can expand the denominator of
the first term in the asymptotics (
4.27
) into a Taylor series with respect to

\tau^{2+\sigma}
, and deduce that the correction provided by this expansion is smaller than

\mathcal{O}(\tau^{3+\sigma})
; thus, the contribution to the error resulting from the denominator can be neglected,
and one arrives at the asymptotics (
4.29
) for
n=1
.
To verify the asymptotics (
4.30
), one has to expand the exponential function with

P_{1}(\tau)=0
and one non-trivial explicit term, plus the corrections, and then multiply this expansion by the first
coefficient; then, after a straightforward calculation, one finds that the formula obtained coincides with the
asymptotics (
4.28
).

Having in mind an induction-based proof for item
(3)
, denote, for
n=1
,

(u_{0}(\tau),\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi_{0}(\tau)})
the corresponding pair of
functions considered in the previous paragraph, and conclude that the base of the mathematical induction is
established.

To make the inductive step, we define the sequence of functions
u_{k}(\tau)
and
\varphi_{k}(\tau)
for
k\in\mathbb{N}

by successively applying
k
Bäcklund transformations (
2.7
) and (
2.8
), respectively, to the
pair of functions
u(\tau)=u_{0}(\tau)
and
\varphi(\tau)=\varphi_{0}(\tau)
. In this context, we define
a_{0}:=a
,
so that
a_{k}
, the parameter of formal monodromy corresponding to the functions
u_{k}(\tau)
and
\varphi_{k}(\tau)
,
satisfies the recurrence relation
a_{k}=a_{k-1}+\mathrm{i}
(cf. equation (
2.11
)). The corresponding parameter

\sigma_{k}
(cf. equation (
4.26
)) is not bounded and varies with
k
, namely,
\sigma_{k}=\sigma_{k-1}+2
,
with
\sigma_{0}=\sigma\geqslant 0
.

Our induction hypothesis is that all the coefficients of the terms
\tau^{k-l\sigma_{n-1}}
,
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
, in
the expansion (
A.1
) for
u_{n-1}(\tau)
vanish, and that the asymptotics stated in
item
(3)
are valid for the functions
u_{n-1}(\tau)
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi_{n-1}(\tau)}
.

To take the inductive step, consider the Bäcklund transformations (
2.7
) and (
2.8
), in which
we put
u_{+}(\tau)=u_{n}(\tau)
,
u(\tau)=u_{n-1}(\tau)
and
\varphi_{+}(\tau)=\varphi_{n}(\tau)
,

\varphi(\tau)=\varphi_{n-1}(\tau)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, respectively, and observe that these transformations are
covariant mappings of the expansion (
A.1
), with
a=a_{n-1}
and
\sigma=\sigma_{n-1}
, to an
expansion of the same form, but with
a=a_{n}
and
\sigma=\sigma_{n}
. Actually,
substituting the expansion (
A.1
) for the function
u_{n-1}(\tau)
into the formula for the
Bäcklund transformation (
2.7
) and re-expanding this expression as
\tau\to 0
, one sees that the resulting
expansion contains terms with positive powers of
\tau
and
\tau^{\sigma}
, and the largest term of the expansion
is
b_{1,0}(n)\tau
, where the coefficient
b_{1,0}(n)=b\,(a_{n-1}-\mathrm{i})b_{3,0}(n-1)/(4b_{1,0}^{2}(n-1))
. Here and
below, we use the notation
b_{i,k}(m)
for
m=n-1
or
m=n
to denote the terms of the
expansion (
A.1
) for the functions
u_{m}(\tau)
.
Using the second equation in the list (
A.2
) and equation (
A.5
) with
a=a_{n-1}

and
\sigma=\sigma_{n-1}=-2\mathrm{i}a_{n-1}
, one proves that
b_{1,0}(n)=2a_{n}b/\sigma_{n}^{2}
, which coincides with the
second equation in the list (
A.2
) for
a=a_{n}
and
\sigma=\sigma_{n}=-2\mathrm{i}a_{n}
. Now, we
have to check that the coefficient of the term
\tau^{1+\sigma_{n-1}}
vanishes. This fact is equivalent to the
relation

b_{3,1}(n-1)=\frac{2\,(2\mathrm{i}a_{n-1}+2)\,b_{1,1}(n-1)\,b_{3,0}(n-1)}{(2\mathrm{i}a_{n-1}+2+\sigma_{n-1})\,b_{1,0}(n-1)},

which can be verified with the help of equations (
A.2
), (
A.3
)
(for
k=1
), and (
A.5
). Thus, the largest term in powers of
\tau
that contains the parameter

\sigma_{n-1}
is of order
\tau^{3+\sigma_{n-1}}
: we denote this term as
b_{1,1}(n)\,\tau^{1+\sigma_{n}}
. The two terms

b_{1,0}(n)\tau
and
b_{1,1}(n)\tau^{1+\sigma_{n}}
, together with the fact that the
expansion (
A.1
) contains only

\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{k+l\sigma_{n}}\big)
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, terms, completely define
this expansion via substitution into the degenerate third Painlevé equation (
1.1
) with
a=a_{n}
.

As long as the form of the small-
\tau
expansion for the function
u_{n}(\tau)
is established, we have to prove that

b_{1,1}(n)
is given by equation (
4.26
) with monodromy data corresponding to the
n
th iteration
of
u_{0}(\tau)
by the Bäcklund transformations. We can certainly continue to use equation (
2.7
) and study
the largest powers of
\tau
containing
\sigma_{n-1}
, but to do so, it is convenient to multiply both sides by

u_{n-1}^{2}(\tau)
; then, we find that the
\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{1+\sigma_{n-1}}\big)
terms cancel identically.
The
\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{3+\sigma_{n-1}}\big)
terms also cancel, but to see this, one has to prove the relation

-\frac{\mathrm{i}b}{8}b_{3,1}(n-1)=b_{1,0}(n-1)\,b_{1,0}(n)\,b_{1,1}(n-1),\qquad\varepsilon=1,

which can be done with the help of equations (
A.3
) for
k=1
, and the second equation in the
list (
A.2
) for
a=a_{n-1}
and
a=a_{n}
. In order to actually establish the induction
hypothesis, one has to equate the
\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{5+\sigma_{n-1}}\big)
terms to zero, which is possible
with the help of equation (
A.6
) for
b_{5,1}(n-1)
. There is, however, an easier way to find the
corresponding recurrence relation, namely, to use the inverse Bäcklund transformation or either one of
equations (
2.13
) or (
2.14
).

Consider equation (
2.14
), say, and recall that
v_{n}(\tau)=u_{n}(\tau)/\tau
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
;
substitute into this equation the expansion (
A.1
) for
u_{n}(\tau)
and equate coefficients
of like powers of
\tau
on both sides of the resulting equation.
On the right-hand side of this equation, there is a term of the order
\tau^{\sigma_{n}-2}=\tau^{\sigma_{n-1}}
,
with coefficient equal to
\tfrac{\varepsilon b}{2}a_{n}\,b_{1,1}(n)
, while on the left-hand side of this equation,
there is also a term of the order
\tau^{\sigma_{n-1}}
, but with coefficient equal to

b_{1,0}^{2}(n)\,b_{1,1}(n-1)
; so, equating these coefficients, we arrive at the following recurrence relation:

b_{1,1}(n)=\frac{2b_{1,0}^{2}(n)}{\varepsilon b\,a_{n}}b_{1,1}(n-1)=\frac{\varepsilon b}{2a_{n}^{3}}b_{1,1}(n-1).

(4.32)

Now, according to the induction hypothesis,
b_{1,1}(n-1)
is given by equation (
4.26
) with
a=a_{n-1}
.
Equation (
4.32
) implies that
b_{1,1}(n)
is given by the same equation (
4.26
) but
with
a=a_{n}
. In verifying this fact, it is imperative to take into account the change of the monodromy data under the
Bäcklund transformations, that is,
s_{0}^{\infty}\to s_{0}^{\infty}
and
g_{12}^{2}\rightarrow-g_{12}^{2}
.

The corresponding expansion for
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
(cf. equation (
4.28
)) is obtained
with the help of equation (
2.1
), and the multiplicative constant is verified via
equation (
2.8
).
∎

Remark 4.2
.

It is instructive to provide an alternative proof for the expansion (
4.27
) without having to
resort to Bäcklund transformations. The first observation is that, for the monodromy
data (
4.21
), the coefficient
b_{1,-1}=0
(see
Appendix
A.1
, equation (
A.11
), and the text subsequent to
equation (
A.11
)).
The second observation is that, in this case, the expansion (
A.1
) contains powers of
\tau

with exponents having positive real part, which, after a rearrangement of terms, manifests as the asymptotic
expansion valid for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{C}
with
\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma\geqslant 0

(the restriction
|\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma|<2
is not imposed).
The expansion is convergent in some neighbourhood of
\tau=0
with a branch cut along the negative real semi-axis;
in particular, these solutions do not have poles in some neighbourhood of the origin.
The expansion (
4.27
) defines, therefore, the analytic continuation of the
solution for all values of the monodromy parameters for which the coefficients of the expansion are defined.
Thus, the coefficient
b_{1,1}
is given by the same formula for all
a\neq\mathrm{i}k
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

\blacksquare

Remark 4.3
.

Since the definition of the polynomials
P_{n}=P_{n}(\tau)
appearing in the asymptotics of the function

\varphi(\tau)
(cf. equations (
4.11
) and (
4.30
)) are cumbersome
(cf. equations (
4.12
)–(
4.14
)), we present explicit expressions for the first four polynomials:

\displaystyle P_{1}=0,\quad P_{2}=\frac{4a^{2}}{\varepsilon b}b_{3,0}\frac{\tau^{2}}{2},\quad P_{3}=P_{2}+\frac{4a^{2}}{\varepsilon b}\left(b_{5,0}+\frac{2a}{\varepsilon b}b_{3,0}^{2}\right)\frac{\tau^{4}}{4},

\displaystyle P_{4}=P_{3}+\frac{4a^{2}}{\varepsilon b}\left(b_{7,0}+\frac{4a}{\varepsilon b}b_{3,0}b_{5,0}+\frac{4a^{2}}{b^{2}}b_{3,0}^{2}\right)\frac{\tau^{6}}{6}.

Note that
\deg\,P_{n}(\tau)=2(n-1)
.

\blacksquare

Remark 4.4
.

Assume that the reader has an asymptotic expansion of the type

u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{\sim}\frac{p}{\tau\big(q_{1}\tau^{\alpha}+q_{2}\tau^{-\alpha}\big)^{2}},\quad\alpha,p,q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\},\quad|\mathrm{Re}\,\alpha|<1,

(4.33)

and would like to get the monodromy parametrization for the asymptotics (
4.33
).
How does one distinguish between the parametrizations given in Theorems
3.1

(
3.1
′
),

4.1
, and
4.2
?

In order to choose which of these theorems is suitable for parametrizing the asymptotics (
4.33
)
via the monodromy data, the reader should complete the following steps:
(1) set
\alpha=1-2\varrho_{1}
and
-\alpha=1-2\varrho_{2}
to obtain two possible values,
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
,
for the parameter
\varrho
so that
\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2}=1
,
0<\mathrm{Re}\,\varrho_{k}<1
,
k=1,2
, and

\varrho_{1}\neq\varrho_{2}\neq 1/2
;
(2) normalize the asymptotics (
4.33
), that is, multiply both the numerator and the denominator of
the asymptotics (
4.33
) by
\lambda^{2}
and choose
\lambda^{2}
such that
\tilde{q}_{1}\tilde{q}_{2}=1
,
where
\tilde{q}_{k}=q_{k}\lambda
,
k=1,2
;
(3) solve the equation
(1-2\varrho)^{2}=\varepsilon p\lambda^{2}
, and denote the roots as
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
,
where, clearly,
\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2}=1
,
\varrho_{1}\neq\varrho_{2}\neq 1/2
, and, if the asymptotics (
4.33
)
is correct, then the roots obtained in steps (1) and (2) coincide;
(4) find
a
by transforming the degenerate third Painlevé equation under investigation into the form of
equation (
1.1
);
(5) if
\mathrm{Im}\,a=0
, then, as follows from equation (
3.1
),
s_{0}^{\infty}s_{1}^{\infty}\neq 0
,
so that one has to use, for any one of the roots
\varrho_{k}
,
k=1,2
,
14

either Theorem
3.1
or Theorem
3.1
′
;
(6) if
\mathrm{Im}\,a>0
, then, check whether any of the roots
\varrho_{1}
or
\varrho_{2}
satisfy the conditions

\mathrm{Re}\,\varrho=1-\left\{\mathrm{Im}\,a/2\right\},\qquad\mathrm{Im}\,\varrho=\mathrm{Re}\,a/2,

(4.34)

and, in the event that none of the roots satisfy the relations (
4.34
), then, again,
for either one of the roots, the parametrizations of Theorems
3.1
or

3.1
′
are valid, whereas if one of
the roots does, in fact, satisfy the relations (
4.34
), then all the conditions enumerated
in item
(1)
of Theorem
4.1
are satisfied and one is in a position to use, for
this root, the monodromy parametrization for the asymptotics (
4.33
) given in item
(1)
of
Theorem
4.1
with the other root being inapplicable for the construction of
the asymptotics; and
(7) if
\mathrm{Im}\,a<0
, then, check whether any of the roots
\varrho_{1}
or
\varrho_{2}
satisfy the conditions

\mathrm{Re}\,\varrho=\left\{\mathrm{Im}\,a/2\right\},\qquad\mathrm{Im}\,\varrho=-\mathrm{Re}\,a/2,

(4.35)

and, in the event that none of the roots satisfy the relations (
4.35
), then, again, for
either one of the roots, the parametrizations of Theorems
3.1
or

3.1
′
are valid, whereas if one of the roots does, in fact,
satisfy the relations (
4.35
), then all the conditions enumerated in item
(1)
of
Theorem
4.2
are satisfied and one is in a position to use, for this root,
the monodromy parametrization for the asymptotics (
4.33
) given in item
(1)
of
Theorem
4.2
with the other root being inapplicable for the construction of the
asymptotics.

We conclude this remark with a brief explanation of how one should proceed in order to find the monodromy
parametrization of the asymptotics (
4.33
) once the identification of the proper theorem has been made.
Consider, say, Theorem
3.1
. Depending on the root which is chosen in
the procedure delineated above, one finds a relation of the form
\tilde{q}_{1}^{2}=w_{1}/w_{2}
for
\varrho_{1}
and

\tilde{q}_{2}^{2}=w_{2}/w_{1}
for
\varrho_{2}
. Either one of these equations have, depending on the values of the parameters

\varrho_{k}
and
\tilde{q}_{k}
,
k=1,2
, three types of solutions:
(1)
g_{11}=Cg_{21}\neq 0
, where
C=C(\varrho_{k},\tilde{q}_{k})
;
(2)
g_{11}=0
and
g_{21}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
; and
(3)
g_{21}=0
and
g_{11}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.
For the sake of example, consider case (1), and recall equation (
2.3
). The Stokes multiplier

s_{0}^{0}
that appears in equation (
2.3
) can be calculated via equation (
3.1
)
provided the root
\varrho_{k}
is chosen. The first and second terms of equation (
2.3
)
can be re-written, respectively, as
g_{21}g_{22}=\tfrac{1}{C}g_{11}g_{22}

and
g_{11}g_{12}=Cg_{21}g_{12}=C(g_{11}g_{22}-1)
, where, in the derivation of the last equation, we have taken
equation (
2.6
) into account. Consequently, one obtains a linear equation for the determination
of
g_{11}g_{22}
; the latter product is a key parameter defining the asymptotics at the point at infinity
(see Appendix C of
[
17
]
). Another parameter that is necessary for constructing the large-
\tau

asymptotics of
u(\tau)
is
g_{11}g_{12}
(see equations (C.29) and (C.31) in
[
17
]
), which has been
addressed above.

\blacksquare

5
Special Solutions with Logarithmic Behaviour as
\tau\to 0
:
\rho=0
and
\varrho=1/2

The values
\rho=0
and
\varrho=1/2
for the respective branching parameters were excluded from
the formulations of the theorems in Sections
3
–
6

because these, and only these, values correspond to solutions of equation (
1.1
)
that exhibit logarithmic behaviour. All solutions of equation (
1.1
) for
a\neq\mathrm{i}k
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
possessing logarithmic behaviour as
\tau\to 0
are members of two (
\rho=0
and
\varrho=1/2
) one-parameter families
of solutions:
the asymptotics for the family corresponding to
\rho=0
, with the restriction
|\mathrm{Im}\,a|<1
,
was obtained in
[
15
]
.
In the recent paper
[
19
]
, we: (i) rewrote the result of
[
15
]
in more convenient
form (in terms of simplified notation); (ii) obtained the corresponding asymptotics for the function

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
; and (iii) distinguished the special case
a=0
. Here, this result is extended to all

a\notin\mathrm{i}2\mathbb{Z}
, and a refined estimate for the error-correction term is obtained.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
(u(\tau),\varphi(\tau))
be a solution of the system (
1.1
), (
2.1
) corresponding to the
monodromy data
(a,s^{0}_{0},s^{\infty}_{0},s^{\infty}_{1},g_{11},g_{12},g_{21},g_{22})
. Suppose that

a\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{i}2\mathbb{Z},\qquad s_{0}^{0}=2\mathrm{i};

(5.1)

then,

\displaystyle(g_{11}-\mathrm{i}g_{21})(g_{12}-\mathrm{i}g_{22})=\mathrm{i}(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a})\neq 0,

(5.2)

\displaystyle(g_{11}-\mathrm{i}g_{21})^{2}=\mathrm{i}s_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}\neq 0,

\displaystyle(g_{12}-\mathrm{i}g_{22})^{2}=-\mathrm{i}s_{1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\pi a}\neq 0.

Define

c:=4\gamma+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{a}+\psi(-\mathrm{i}a/2)-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}+\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\,(g_{12}+\mathrm{i}g_{22})}{g_{12}-\mathrm{i}g_{22}}+\ln(\varepsilon b/2);

(5.3)

then,

\displaystyle u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}

\displaystyle-ab\tau\left(\ln\tau+\frac{1}{2}(c-\mathrm{i}/a)\right)\left(\ln\tau+\frac{1}{2}(c+\mathrm{i}/a)\right)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2}\ln^{2}\tau\big)\right)

(5.4)

\displaystyle\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}

\displaystyle-ab\tau\!\left(\ln^{2}\tau+c\ln\tau+\frac{1}{4}\!\left(c^{2}+\frac{1}{a^{2}}\right)\!\right)\!\left(1+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2}\ln^{2}\tau\big)\right),

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi}{2}(a+\mathrm{i})}}{\pi a}(g_{12}\!-\!\mathrm{i}g_{22})^{2}\left(\Gamma\big(1\!-\!\tfrac{\mathrm{i}a}{2}\big)\right)^{2}(2\tau^{2})^{\mathrm{i}a}\!\left(\frac{\ln\tau\!+\!\frac{1}{2}(c\!+\!\mathrm{i}/a)}{\ln\tau\!+\!\frac{1}{2}(c\!-\!\mathrm{i}/a)}\right)\!\left(1\!+\!\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2}\big)\right),

(5.6)

where
\psi(z)\!:=\!\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\ln\Gamma(z)}{\mathrm{d}z}
is the digamma function, and

\gamma\!=\!-\psi(1)\!=\!0.577215664901532860606512\dotsc
is the Euler-Mascheroni constant.

Proof.

Substituting
s_{0}^{0}=2\mathrm{i}
into equation (
2.3
) and using equation (
2.6
),
we obtain equation (
5.2
); then, the first condition of (
5.1
)
implies the inequality in (
5.2
). The two subsequent equalities/inequalities that include
the Stokes multipliers
s_{0}^{\infty}
and
s_{1}^{\infty}
are derived similarly, but, instead of using
equation (
2.3
), one makes use of equations (
2.4
)
and (
2.5
), respectively. Thus, the parameter
c
is correctly defined by
equation (
5.3
).

For
|\mathrm{Im}\,a|<1
and
a\neq 0
, the asymptotics (
5.5
) and
(
5.6
) are proved in
[
19
]

(see Theorem 3.1 and Remark 3.1 in
[
19
]
); however, in the asymptotic
formulae (
5.5
) and (
5.6
), more precise error estimates for
the correction terms are obtained by employing the complete local asymptotic expansion given in
Appendix
B.1
(see Remark
B.1
).

The restriction for the parameter of formal monodromy
a
remains, however: the extension of the
asymptotics (
5.4
)–(
5.6
) to all values of

a\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{i}2\mathbb{Z}
is done below (see Lemma
5.1
) using
a bootstrap-type argument as in Section
3

(cf. Theorem
3.2
), but, in the present case, with the
help of Theorems
5.2
and
5.2
′
,
which will be proved below. For the proofs of these theorems, we use the
asymptotics (
5.4
)–(
5.6
), with the updated error-correction term(s),
but in which the parameter
a
is still subject to the restrictions
|\mathrm{Im}\,a|<1
and
a\neq 0
.
∎

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
(u(\tau),\varphi(\tau))

), (

(5.7)

then,

\displaystyle(g_{11}+\mathrm{i}g_{21})(g_{12}+\mathrm{i}g_{22})=-\mathrm{i}(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a})\neq 0,

(5.8)

\displaystyle(g_{11}+\mathrm{i}g_{21})^{2}=\mathrm{i}s_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}\neq 0,

then,

\displaystyle u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}

\displaystyle-\frac{\varepsilon\left(1+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2}\ln^{2}\tau\big)\right)}{4\tau\big(\ln\tau+c_{-}/2\big)^{2}},

(5.10)

\displaystyle-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a/2}\big(2\tau^{2}\big)^{\mathrm{i}a}\exp\left(-2\mathrm{i}\varepsilon b\tau^{2}\left(\left(\ln\tau+\frac{c_{-}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\right)+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{4}\ln^{4}\tau\big)\right)}{\left(\Gamma\left(1/2+\mathrm{i}a/2\right)(g_{11}+\mathrm{i}g_{21})\right)^{2}}.

(5.11)

Proof.

),
we obtain equation (

); then, the first

in (

).

The asymptotics (

.
