Lemma 5.1
.

Completion of the proofs of Theorems
5.1
,
5.2
,
and
5.2
′
. These theorems
are valid for all values of the parameter
a
stated therein.

Proof.

The results presented in Theorems
5.2
and

5.2
′
are obtained for the parameter of formal monodromy,
a
,
belonging to the disjoint strips
-2<\mathrm{Im}\,a<0
and
0<\mathrm{Im}\,a<2
, respectively, of the complex plane.
The formulae for the respective asymptotics are similar, but the coefficients seem to be different, which is not
surprising, since, by construction, the parameter
a
belongs to different strips.
In fact, if we assume that
\mathrm{Im}\,a\in(-2,2)
and
a\neq\pm\mathrm{i}
, then both results coincide. To prove this for
the asymptotics of the function
u(\tau)
, consider the difference
c_{-}-c_{+}
(cf. equations (
5.9
)
and (
5.12
)):

\begin{gathered}c_{-}-c_{+}=\psi(1/2+\mathrm{i}a/2)-\psi(1/2-\mathrm{i}a/2)+\pi\mathrm{i}+\pi\mathrm{i}\left(\frac{g_{11}-\mathrm{i}g_{21}}{g_{11}+\mathrm{i}g_{21}}-\frac{g_{12}-\mathrm{i}g_{22}}{g_{12}+\mathrm{i}g_{22}}\right)\\
=\pi\mathrm{i}\left(\tanh(\pi a/2)+1+\frac{2\mathrm{i}(g_{11}g_{22}-g_{12}g_{21}}{-\mathrm{i}(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a})}\right)=\pi\mathrm{i}\left(\frac{2\mathrm{e}^{\pi a/2}}{\mathrm{e}^{\pi a/2}+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a/2}}-\frac{2}{1+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}}\right)=0.\end{gathered}

(5.15)

In the calculation (
5.15
), the identity
\psi(1/2+z)-\psi(1/2-z)=\pi\tan(\pi z)
and
equations (
5.8
) and (
2.6
) were used.

To confirm the coincidence of the asymptotics (
5.11
) and (
5.14
)
for the function
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
, we must, in addition, verify that the corresponding multiplicative
constants are equal; this is done by considering their ratio:

\begin{gathered}\left(\frac{-2\pi\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a/2}}{\Gamma^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\mathrm{i}a}{2}\right)(g_{11}+\mathrm{i}g_{21})^{2}}\right):\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\pi a/2}}{2\pi}\Gamma^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\mathrm{i}a}{2}\right)(g_{12}+\mathrm{i}g_{22})^{2}\right)\\
=-\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a/2}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\mathrm{i}a}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\mathrm{i}a}{2}\right)(g_{11}+\mathrm{i}g_{21})(g_{12}+\mathrm{i}g_{22})}\right)^{2}=-\left(\frac{\cosh(\pi a/2)\,2\pi\,\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a/2}}{\pi(-\mathrm{i}\,(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}))}\right)^{2}=1.\end{gathered}

The line
\mathrm{Im}\,a=0
requires separate consideration. Firstly, note that the proof presented above does not
sense the presence of this line, that is, the leading terms of asymptotics of the functions
u(\tau)
and

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
,
which can be defined by any one of the pair of formulae (
5.10
) and
(
5.11
)
or (
5.13
) and (
5.14
), and denoted henceforth as
u_{as}(\tau)
and

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi_{as}(\tau)}
, respectively, are analytic functions of the monodromy data and,
in particular, the formal monodromy parameter
a
in the strip
|\mathrm{Im}\,a|<1
. As a solution of
the differential equation (
1.1
), the function
u(\tau)
is also an analytic function of
a
; correspondingly,

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
is also an analytic function of
a
.
Secondly, note that the difference
u(\tau)-u_{as}(\tau):=E(\tau)
is an analytic function of
a
, since it is
the difference of the analytic functions. Its small-
\tau
asymptotics is studied in
Appendix
C.1
, where it is proved that the asymptotics of
E(\tau)
does not
have any singularities for any values of
a
, so that the asymptotics of the function
u(\tau)
stated in
Theorems
5.2
and
5.2
′
is true in the
entire strip
\mathrm{Im}\,a\in(-2,2)
punctured at
a=\pm\mathrm{i}
. This fact implies the validity of the same statement
for the asymptotics of the function
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
.

We are now in a position to employ the bootstrap argument. In the previous paragraph, the validity of each of the
Theorems
5.2
and
5.2
′

was extended to the strip
\mathrm{Im}\,a\in(-2,2)
punctured at the two points
a=\pm\mathrm{i}
.
One applies to the solution and the corresponding asymptotics stated in
Theorems
5.2
and
5.2
′

the inverses of the Bäcklund transformations that were used to obtain these asymptotic results;
then, one arrives at the asymptotics formulated in Theorem
5.1
, but now with the validity of
these results extended to the wider strip
\mathrm{Im}\,a\in(-3,3)=(-3,1)\cup(-1,3)
punctured at the three points

a=-2\mathrm{i},0,2\mathrm{i}
.

At the next stage of the bootstrap argument, we apply the Bäcklund transformations (
2.9
) and
(
2.10
) and arrive at the asymptotics stated in Theorem
5.2
, and apply the
Bäcklund transformations (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) to obtain the results stated in
Theorem
5.2
′
, but now with the validity of both theorems
extended to the strip
\mathrm{Im}\,a\in(-4,4)=(-4,2)\cup(-2,4)
punctured at the four points

a=\pm 3\mathrm{i},\pm\mathrm{i}
.

Finally, an inductive argument completes the proof that the asymptotics stated in Theorem
5.1

are valid for
a\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{i}2\mathbb{Z}
, and the results of Theorems
5.2
and

5.2
′
hold for

a\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\mathrm{i}(2m-1),m\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

From the qualitative point of view, the asymptotic behaviours of the solutions described in
Theorems
5.2
and
5.2
′
, which
correspond to the case
c_{+}=c_{-}=:\tilde{c}_{-1,3}=0
, do not exibit any special lineaments when compared to the
case for non-vanishing values of this parameter. A study of the complete asymptotic expansion
(see Appendix
C
), however, shows that solutions of equation (
1.1
)
corresponding to
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}=0
are the only ones for which the
levels
(see
Appendix
C.1
for the definition of levels) of the complete asymptotic
expansions are represented by truncated logarithmic series; in Corollary
5.1
below, we specify
such solutions in terms of the monodromy data.

\blacksquare

Corollary 5.1
.

Let the pair of functions
(u(\tau),\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)})
correspond to the monodromy data specified in
Theorem
5.2
;
then, the parameter
c_{-}=c_{+}=0
in the asymptotic formulae
(
5.10
), (
5.11
) and (
5.13
),
(
5.14
) iff the monodromy data satisfy,
in addition
, one of the following three
conditions:

(1)

G_{+}G_{-}\neq 0
,

\displaystyle g_{11}g_{22}=-\frac{G_{+}G_{-}}{4(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a})},\quad g_{11}g_{12}=\frac{(2+\mathrm{i}G_{-})G_{-}}{4(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a})},\quad g_{22}g_{21}=\frac{(2-\mathrm{i}G_{+})G_{+}}{4(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a})},

(5.16)

\displaystyle g_{11}(g_{12}+\mathrm{i}g_{22})=\frac{G_{-}}{2},\qquad g_{22}(g_{11}+\mathrm{i}g_{21})=\frac{\mathrm{i}G_{+}}{2},

(5.17)

(2)

G_{-}=0,\quad G_{+}=2\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}
,

g_{11}=0,\qquad g_{22}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\},\qquad g_{12}g_{21}=-1,\qquad g_{22}g_{21}=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a},

(5.18)

(3)

G_{+}=0,\quad G_{-}=-2\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}
,

g_{22}=0,\qquad g_{11}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\},\qquad g_{12}g_{21}=-1,\qquad g_{11}g_{12}=-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a},

(5.19)

where

G_{\pm}=\frac{1}{\pi}\big(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}\big)\Big(\ln(\varepsilon b/2)+4\gamma+\frac{1}{2}\big(\psi(1/2+\mathrm{i}a/2)+\psi(1/2-\mathrm{i}a/2)\big)\Big)\pm\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}.

(5.20)

Proof.

We outline the proof for item
(1)
. The derivations of the formulae in items
(2)
and
(3)

are much simpler, and are thus left to the interested reader.

Assume that
c_{-}=c_{+}=0
, then
c_{-}+c_{+}=0
, consequently, adding equations (
5.9
) and
(
5.12
), taking the two fractions consisting of the monodromy data
g_{ij}
to a common denominator,
and substituting the identity (
5.8
) for the common denominator, one shows that the
resulting equation can be written as

g_{11}g_{12}+g_{21}g_{22}=(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a})\Psi,\quad\Psi=\frac{1}{\pi}\Big(4\gamma+\ln(\varepsilon b/2)+\frac{1}{2}\big(\psi(1/2+\mathrm{i}a/2)+\psi(1/2-\mathrm{i}a/2)\Big).

(5.21)

Since
s_{0}^{0}=-2\mathrm{i}
, it follows from equation (
2.3
) that

g_{21}g_{22}-g_{11}g_{12}=\mathrm{i}(2g_{11}g_{22}+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a});

(5.22)

thus, adding and subtracting equations (
5.21
) and (
5.22
), one arrives at the
system

\begin{gathered}2g_{21}g_{22}=(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a})\Psi+\mathrm{i}(2g_{11}g_{22}+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}),\\
2g_{12}g_{11}=(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a})\Psi-\mathrm{i}(2g_{11}g_{22}+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}).\end{gathered}

(5.23)

Multiplying equations (
5.23
) and taking equation (
2.6
) into account, one gets

4g_{11}g_{22}(g_{11}g_{22}-1)=(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a})^{2}\Psi^{2}+(2g_{11}g_{22}+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a})^{2}.

(5.24)

Expanding the right-most (parenthetical) term in equation (
5.24
), cancelling the quadratic
terms
4(g_{11}g_{22})^{2}
, and introducing
G_{\pm}
as in equation (
5.20
), we obtain the first
equation in the list (
5.16
); the remaining two equations in the list (
5.16
)
are obtained upon substituting the first equation into the expressions on the right-hand sides of the
system (
5.23
).
The equations in (
5.17
) are obtained by taking linear combinations of the
equations in (
5.16
). Conversely, substitute equations (
5.16
) into, say,
the definition of
c_{-}
(cf. equation (
5.9
)) to prove that
c_{-}=0
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

In Corollary
5.1
, the quantities
G_{\pm}
are functions of the formal monodromy,

a
, and the scaling parameter
\varepsilon b
; therefore, if
a\neq\mathrm{i}(2m-1)
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
one, and only one, solution
u(\tau)
with the asymptotics given in Theorem
5.2
for
c_{-}=0

exists. One of the monodromy parameters, either
g_{11}\neq 0
or
g_{22}\neq 0
, defines the
“constant of integration” of the function
\varphi(\tau)
in equation (
2.1
), so that the function

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
is also unique modulo this multiplicative
\tau
-independent parameter.

\blacksquare

Remark 5.4
.

Items
(2)
and
(3)
of Corollary
5.1
correspond to the case when one of the monodromy
parameters, either
g_{11}
or
g_{22}
, vanishes. According to Theorem
5.2
,
such special values for
g_{11}
or
g_{22}
give rise to
small-
\tau
asymptotics that are qualitatively similar to the small-
\tau
asymptotics of solutions with
monodromy data described in item
(1)
of Corollary
5.1
. The large-
\tau
asymptotics
of solutions with monodromy data given in items
(2)
and
(3)
of Corollary
5.1
,
however, are more interesting, because they are the only solutions that have the small-
\tau

logarithmic behaviour presented in Theorem
5.2
having truncated asymptotics as

\tau\to+\infty
.
In this context, the following intriguing question manifests:
do there exist, for any scaling
\varepsilon b
, values of the formal monodromy parameter
a
that solve the equation

G_{\pm}=0
, and, if so, how many solutions exist? We haven’t yet studied this question. For
\varepsilon b=2
,
numerical studies using
Maple
give a series (infinite?) of solutions for each equation
G_{\pm}=0
,
namely,

a=a^{1}_{\pm}=0.2381378288\ldots\mp\mathrm{i}0.6358442252\ldots
,
a=a^{2}_{\pm}=0.1144878083\ldots\mp\mathrm{i}1.714583576\ldots
,

a=a^{3}_{\pm}=0.09349464758\ldots\mp\mathrm{i}2.744016682\ldots
, etc. If the monodromy parameter
a
is a solution of
the equation
G_{\pm}=0
, then, for this parameter value, there exists a unique solution
u(\tau)
of
equation (
1.1
) and a corresponding function
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
which is uniquely defined modulo a
multiplicative
\tau
-independent non-vanishing parameter that is defined in terms of
g_{12}
or
g_{21}
.

\blacksquare

6
Solutions with Poles Accumulating at the Origin:
\mathrm{Re}(\varrho)=1/2

The reader may have noted that, although the respective denominators of the asymptotic formulae for
u(\tau)

given in equations (
3.7
) and (
3.13
) vanish for an infinite sequence of points

\tau=\tau_{p}\to 0
,
p\in\mathbb{N}
, for
\varrho=1/2+\mathrm{i}\varkappa
,
\varkappa\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, such
values of the branching parameter were not excluded from the formulations of
Theorems
3.1
and
3.1
′
; more precisely, since

w_{k}\tau^{-2\mathrm{i}\varkappa}+w_{k+1}\tau^{2\mathrm{i}\varkappa}=2\sqrt{w_{k}}\,\sqrt{w_{k+1}}\cos\Big(2\varkappa\ln\tau+\mathrm{i}\ln\big(\sqrt{w_{k}}/\sqrt{w_{k+1}}\big)\Big),\qquad k=1,3,

the asymptotic formulae have second-order poles at the points

\tau_{p}=\exp\left(-\frac{\pi p}{2|\varkappa|}+\frac{\pi}{4\varkappa}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4\varkappa}\ln\frac{w_{k}}{w_{k+1}}\right),\quad k=1,3.

(6.1)

Note that the value of
k
(equal to
1
or
3
) is not important because of the second identity in
(
3.17
), and the choice of the branch of the
\ln
-function in equation (
6.1
)
is also not essential because its selection is a mere redefinition (shift) of
p
, which has the sense
of a parameter tending to
+\infty
. Hereafter, we assume that the branch of the
\ln
-function is fixed.

Note that all the points
\tau_{p}
belong to the ray in
\mathbb{C}
that is defined by the complex number
\tau_{0}
;
therefore, if
\arg\tau_{0}=0
and the notation
\tau\to 0^{+}
is understood in the standard sense, then the asymptotics
of the function
u(\tau)
is considered for real positive values of
\tau
approaching the origin, and we encounter
a problem related with an infinite number of poles of the asymptotics located along the way as the origin is
approached. One can surmise that, somewhere in a neighbourhood of the poles of the asymptotics, are located poles
of
u(\tau)
; if, however, we understand
\tau\to 0^{+}
in the standard way, then we are restrained from going around
the poles
\tau_{p}
, so that we stop at the first pole, and the sense of such asymptotics is unclear.

In fact, such a “problem” with the asymptotic formulae occurs for all the Painlevé equations, and has a standard
solution. In our case, for example, the notation
\tau\to 0^{+}
means that we take
\arg\,\tau=0
on the positive real
semi-axis, and approach the origin in a wider domain
\mathcal{S}\in\mathbb{C}
.
For the regular singular point of
u(\tau)
at
\tau=0
, the domain
\mathcal{S}

is a full neighbourhood of the origin cut along the negative real semi-axis; however, in this case, we must also
take into account those points
\tau_{p}
for which
\arg\tau_{p}
is arbitrary, which implies that the definition
of
\mathcal{S}
should be supplemented by deleting from it an infinite number of discs centred at the points
\tau_{p}
.
Below, we complete the definition of the
discs, and note here that, due to the Painlevé property of the function
u(\tau)
, one can take an arbitrary path
to the origin in the multiply-connected domain
\mathcal{S}
along which the
asymptotics of the function
u(\tau)
is considered, since both
u(\tau)
and its asymptotics are uniquely defined
in
\mathcal{S}
.

We now proceed with the definition of the discs.
The points
\tau_{p}
are located on the ray with the origin at
\tau=0
; therefore, the distance between the
neighbouring points
\tau_{p}
is
|\tau_{p+1}-\tau_{p}|=J|\tau_{p}|
, where

J=1-\exp\left(-\pi/(2|\varkappa|)\right)
.
Next, we consider the discs
\mathcal{D}_{p}
centred at
\tau_{p}
with radius
R_{p}=J|\tau_{p}|^{1+\delta_{d}}
,
where
\delta_{d}\in[0,2]
is the same for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
and will be specified later.
Clearly, for
\delta_{d}\in(0,2]
and small enough
\tau
,

\mathcal{D}_{p}\cap\mathcal{D}_{p^{\prime}}=\varnothing

\forall

p\neq p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
. To ensure this
property for
\delta_{d}=0
, we have to reduce the coefficient
J
to, say,
J/3
, because the connectedness of the
intersection of the domain
\mathcal{S}
with any small enough neighbourhood of the origin is important.
The formal definition of the domain
\mathcal{S}
reads:

\mathcal{S}:=\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}:\;|\arg\tau|<\pi\}\setminus\underset{\scriptscriptstyle p\in\mathbb{N}}{\cup}\mathcal{D}_{p},\quad\mathcal{D}_{p}:=\left\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}:\;|\tau-\tau_{p}|<R_{p}=J|\tau_{p}|^{1+\delta_{d}}\right\},

(6.2)

with
\delta_{d}
and
J
defined above.

Remark 6.1
.

We are now ready to clarify the notation
\tau\to 0^{+}
(cf. Remark
3.2
) appearing
in the asymptotic formulae of solutions that have sequences of poles accumulating at the origin; for such solutions,
the notation
\tau\to 0^{+}
is equivalent to
\tau\in\mathcal{S}
and
\tau\to 0
: for brevity, we write

\mathcal{S}\ni\tau\to 0
.

\blacksquare

Our calculations of the monodromy data in
[
15
,
19
]
uphold this modification
of the sense of the notation
\tau\to 0^{+}
because they deal with estimates of functions with power-like
behaviour. This fact implies that Theorems
3.1
and
3.1
′

also sustain this modification. The error estimates in these theorems remain unchanged as the origin is approached
“far” from the boundaries of the discs
\mathcal{D}_{p}
, or, when
\delta_{d}=0
; it is clear, however, that the
error of the approximations increases as the points
\tau_{p}
are approached, that is, when the parameter

\delta_{d}>0
.
Here, we formulate the special case of Theorems
3.1
and

3.1
′
for
\varrho=1/2+\mathrm{i}\varkappa
that is applicable to situations related
with the existence of sequences of poles accumulating at
\tau=0
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
(u(\tau),\varphi(\tau))
be a solution of the system (
1.1
), (
2.1
)
corresponding to the monodromy data
(a,s_{0}^{0},s_{0}^{\infty},s_{1}^{\infty},g_{11},g_{12},g_{21},g_{22})
.
Suppose that

s_{0}^{0}=-2\mathrm{i}\cosh(2\pi\varkappa),\quad\varkappa\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},\qquad s_{0}^{\infty}s_{1}^{\infty}\neq 0.

(6.3)

The conditions (
6.3
) imply that

a\neq 2\varkappa+\mathrm{i}(2k-1),\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z},

(6.4)

g_{11}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}/4}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\pi\varkappa}+g_{21}\mathrm{e}^{\pi\mathrm{i}/4}\mathrm{e}^{\mp\pi\varkappa}\neq 0,\quad g_{12}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}/4}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\pi\varkappa}+g_{22}\mathrm{e}^{\pi\mathrm{i}/4}\mathrm{e}^{\mp\pi\varkappa}\neq 0,

(6.5)

where either the upper signs or the lower signs, respectively, are taken.

Define

\widehat{\mathscr{A}}(\varkappa):=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi\varkappa}{2}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon b}{2}\right)^{-\mathrm{i}\varkappa}\frac{\Gamma(1+2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}{\Gamma(1-2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}\,\Gamma\!\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\mathrm{i}a}{2}-\mathrm{i}\varkappa\right)\left(g_{11}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\mathrm{e}^{\pi\varkappa}+g_{21}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\varkappa}\right),

(6.6)

and assume that
\mathcal{S}
is defined by (
6.2
) with
\delta_{d}\in[0,2)
;

then,

\displaystyle u(\tau)\underset{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}\ni\tau\to 0}{=}

\displaystyle\,\frac{4\varepsilon\varkappa^{2}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}(\varkappa)\widehat{\mathscr{A}}(-\varkappa)\big(1+\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2-\delta_{d}})\big)}{\tau\big(\widehat{\mathscr{A}}(\varkappa)\tau^{-2\mathrm{i}\varkappa}-\widehat{\mathscr{A}}(-\varkappa)\tau^{2\mathrm{i}\varkappa}\big)^{2}},

(6.7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}\underset{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}\ni\tau\to 0}{=}

\displaystyle\,\frac{2\pi\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi a}{2}}}{\widehat{\mathscr{A}}(\varkappa)\widehat{\mathscr{A}}(-\varkappa)}\left(2\tau^{2}\right)^{\mathrm{i}a}\big(1+\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2-\delta_{d}})\big).

(6.8)

Proof.

This theorem is a reformulation of Theorem
3.1
for

\varrho=1/2+\mathrm{i}\varkappa
, where we use the relations
w_{1}=\widehat{\mathscr{A}}(\varkappa)
and

w_{2}=-\widehat{\mathscr{A}}(-\varkappa)
, which are valid for this value of
\varrho
;
moreover, the restriction
-2<\mathrm{Im}\,a<0
is removed due to Theorem
3.2
.
The error estimate, however, requires further commentary: if
\delta_{d}=0
, which means that the discs
\mathcal{D}_{p}

are large enough and
\tau
is far from the pole
\tau_{p}
of the leading term of asymptotics, then the
error estimate is the same as in Theorem
3.1
, but the situation changes when we consider discs
with smaller radii
R_{p}=\mathcal{O}\big(|\tau_{p}|^{1+\delta_{d}}\big)
as
\tau_{p}\to 0
and

\tau\to\partial\mathcal{D}_{p}
.
To evaluate the error of the approximation of the function
u(\tau)
by its leading term of asymptotics, we have
to invoke the first correction term
yA_{1}(x)/\tau
(see Appendix
A.2
and
equation (
6.18
) below), which is of the order

\tau\tau_{p}^{3}/(\tau-\tau_{p})^{3}
for
\tau
near
\partial\mathcal{D}_{p}
,
while the leading term in this domain is of the order
\tau_{p}^{2}/(\tau(\tau-\tau_{p})^{2})
; thus, the error of the
approximation differs from the leading term by the factor

\tau^{2}\tau_{p}/(\tau-\tau_{p})=\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2-\delta_{d}}\big)
. The error estimate for the function

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
is obtained via equation (
2.1
) by integrating the corresponding asymptotics
of
u(\tau)
.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

As a consequence of Theorem
3.2
, the conditions of Theorems
3.1

and
3.1
′
coincide; subsequently, we can obtain
Theorem
6.1
with the help of Theorem
3.1
′
.
This leads to seemingly different, yet equivalent, asymptotics for the functions
u(\tau)
and

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
: we formulate these equivalent results in
Theorem
6.1
′
.

\blacksquare

Theorem 6.1
.

′

Let
(u(\tau),\varphi(\tau))
be a solution of the system (
1.1
), (
2.1
)
corresponding to the monodromy data
(a,s_{0}^{0},s_{0}^{\infty},s_{1}^{\infty},g_{11},g_{12},g_{21},g_{22})
.
Suppose that the conditions (
6.3
)–(
6.5
)
of Theorem
6.1
are satisfied.

Define

\widehat{\mathscr{B}}(\varkappa):=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi\varkappa}{2}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon b}{2}\right)^{-\mathrm{i}\varkappa}\frac{\Gamma(1+2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}{\Gamma(1-2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}\,\Gamma\!\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\mathrm{i}a}{2}-\mathrm{i}\varkappa\right)\left(g_{12}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\mathrm{e}^{\pi\varkappa}+g_{22}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\varkappa}\right),

(6.9)

and assume that
\mathcal{S}
is defined by (
6.2
) with
\delta_{d}\in[0,2);

then,

\displaystyle u(\tau)\underset{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}\ni\tau\to 0}{=}

\displaystyle\;\frac{4\varepsilon\varkappa^{2}\widehat{\mathscr{B}}(\varkappa)\widehat{\mathscr{B}}(-\varkappa)\big(1+\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2-\delta_{d}})\big)}{\tau\big(\widehat{\mathscr{B}}(\varkappa)\tau^{-2\mathrm{i}\varkappa}-\widehat{\mathscr{B}}(-\varkappa)\tau^{2\mathrm{i}\varkappa}\big)^{2}},

(6.10)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}\underset{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}\ni\tau\to 0}{=}

\displaystyle\;\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi a}{2}}\widehat{\mathscr{B}}(\varkappa)\widehat{\mathscr{B}}(-\varkappa)}{2\pi}\left(2\tau^{2}\right)^{\mathrm{i}a}\big(1+\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2-\delta_{d}})\big).

(6.11)

Proof.

The results of this theorem are a reformulation of those in
Theorem
3.1
′
for
\varrho=1/2+\mathrm{i}\varkappa
, where, under this
substitution,
w_{3}=\widehat{\mathscr{B}}(\varkappa)
and
w_{4}=-\widehat{\mathscr{B}}(-\varkappa)
; moreover,
the restriction
0<\mathrm{Im}\,a<2
is removed due to Theorem
3.2
. The justification
for the error estimates is literally the same as that given in the proof of Theorem
6.1
.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

The asymptotics of the functions
u(\tau)
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
given in
Theorems
6.1
and
6.1
′
coincide:
this is a consequence of the relations (
3.17
); in particular,
equations (
6.7
) and (
6.10
) imply that
equation (
6.1
) for the poles of the leading term of asymptotics can be simplified as

\tau_{p}=\exp\left(-\frac{\pi p}{2|\varkappa|}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4\varkappa}\ln\frac{\widehat{\mathscr{A}}(-\varkappa)}{\widehat{\mathscr{A}}(\varkappa)}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{\pi p}{2|\varkappa|}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4\varkappa}\ln\frac{\widehat{\mathscr{B}}(-\varkappa)}{\widehat{\mathscr{B}}(\varkappa)}\right).

(6.12)

\blacksquare

Corollary 6.1
.

Let
(u(\tau),\varphi(\tau))
be a solution of the system (
1.1
), (
2.1
)
corresponding to the monodromy data
(a,s_{0}^{0},s_{0}^{\infty},s_{1}^{\infty},g_{11},g_{12},g_{21},g_{22})
.
Suppose that the conditions (
6.3
)–(
6.5
)
of Theorem
6.1
are satisfied.

For
\epsilon>0
, define

\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}:=\{\tau\in\mathcal{S}:|\tau|<\epsilon\}.

Then, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
u(\tau)
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
have neither zeros
nor poles in
\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

The absence of poles is apparent because finite-valued asymptotics at a point means that an approximated
function has a finite value at such a point.

The proof for the zeros proceeds by contradiction. If such an
\epsilon>0
does not exist, then there exists
a sequence of zeros accumulating at the origin, which contradicts the asymptotics (
6.7
)
for the function
u(\tau)
. Actually, in the case of zeros
\tau^{0}_{k}\to 0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we find, upon
substituting
u(\tau^{0}_{k})=0
into equation (
6.7
), that
0=\mathcal{O}(1/\tau^{0}_{k})
as

k\to\infty
, so that the approximation of
u(\tau)
in a neighbourhood of
\tau^{0}_{k}

becomes worse as
\tau\to 0
, which is a contradiction.

According to equation (
2.1
), the function

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
has first-order zeros or poles only at the zeros of the function
u(\tau)
, depending
on the expansion (
6.14
) or (
6.15
), respectively.
∎

It follows from Theorem
6.1
and Corollary
6.1
that those zeros or poles
accumulating at the origin, if any, of the function
u(\tau)
can be located only in the discs
\mathcal{D}_{p}

for large enough
p
. As noted in the last sentence of the proof of Corollary
6.1
,
the zeros and poles of the function
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
are located at the zeros of the corresponding function

u(\tau)
, and, therefore, do not require further attention. Our main goal in this section is to establish
the following theorem.
27
27

27

In the proof of
Theorem
6.2
and in all constructions up to, and including, the
inequality (
6.21
), we set
\varepsilon=+1
in equation (
1.1
); all
statements remains valid for
\varepsilon=-1
. To get the
\varepsilon
-dependent variant of the aforementioned
constructions, one has to make the changes
u(\tau)\to\varepsilon u(\tau)
,

u_{as}(\tau)\to\varepsilon u_{as}(\tau)
, and
b\to\varepsilon b
.

Theorem 6.2
.

For large enough
p\in\mathbb{N}
, each disc
\mathcal{D}_{p}
contains one, and only one, pole and no zeros of
the solution
u(\tau)
corresponding to the monodromy data defined in Theorem
6.1
.

Proof.

It is straightforward to establish that if
\tau_{\hat{p}}\in\mathbb{C}
is a pole of some solution
u(\tau)
,
then
\tau_{\hat{p}}\neq 0
, it is a second-order pole, and the corresponding Laurent-series expansion of

u(\tau)
at
\tau_{\hat{p}}
is

u(\tau)=-\frac{\tau_{\hat{p}}}{4(\tau-\tau_{\hat{p}})^{2}}+u_{0}-\frac{u_{0}}{\tau_{\hat{p}}}(\tau-\tau_{\hat{p}})+\frac{2ab\tau_{\hat{p}}-24\tau_{\hat{p}}u_{0}^{2}+9u_{0}}{10\tau_{\hat{p}}^{2}}(\tau-\tau_{\hat{p}})^{2}+\mathcal{O}\big((\tau-\tau_{\hat{p}})^{3}\big),

(6.13)

where
u_{0}
is a complex parameter. Thus, both the function
u(\tau)
and its leading term of asymptotics only have
poles of the second order.

Since our asymptotics are obtained with the help of the Bäcklund transformations (
2.7
) and (
2.9
),
it is clear that the second-order poles are the images of the first-order zeros of the solutions
that are used as the “seed solutions” for these transformations: we now consider this statement more precisely.

The direct substitution of the general form of the Taylor-series expansion for
u(\tau)
at its zero,

\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
,
into equation (
1.1
) shows that all zeros are of the first order, and two possible expansions can be found:

u(\tau)=+\mathrm{i}b(\tau-\tau_{0})-\frac{(2a-\mathrm{i})b}{2\tau_{0}}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{2}\!+u_{3}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{3}\!+\frac{4b^{2}\!+(\mathrm{i}a-1)u_{3}}{2\tau_{0}}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{4}\!+\mathcal{O}\big((\tau-\tau_{0})^{5}\big),

(6.14)

u(\tau)=-\mathrm{i}b(\tau-\tau_{0})-\frac{(2a+\mathrm{i})b}{2\tau_{0}}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{2}\!+u_{3}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{3}\!+\frac{4b^{2}\!-(\mathrm{i}a+1)u_{3}}{2\tau_{0}}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{4}\!+\mathcal{O}\big((\tau-\tau_{0})^{5}\big),

(6.15)

where
u_{3}
is a complex parameter. Hence, there are two types of zeros, and their expansions
differ by “formal conjugation”, i.e., the change
\mathrm{i}\to-\mathrm{i}
in all terms. Denote by
\tau_{0}^{\pm}
,
respectively, the zeros of
u(\tau)
such that the first coefficient of the Taylor-series expansion of
u(\tau)

at these zeros is equal to
\pm\mathrm{i}b
.

Now, with the above information, we can check that the transformation (
2.7
) (
u\to u_{+}
) sends the

\tau_{0}^{+}
zeros to the poles
\tau_{p}=\tau_{0}^{+}
, the
\tau_{0}^{-}
zeros to holomorphic points, and the poles
\tau_{p}

to the zeros
\tau_{0}^{-}=\tau_{p}
, whilst the transformation (
2.9
) (
u\to u_{-}
) sends the
\tau_{0}^{+}
zeros to
holomorphic points, the
\tau_{0}^{-}
zeros to the poles
\tau_{p}=\tau_{0}^{-}
, and the poles
\tau_{p}
to the zeros

\tau_{0}^{+}=\tau_{p}
.

The zeros of
u(\tau)
require, in fact, an analysis similar to the poles, because, in the asymptotic study of the
degenerate third Painlevé equation via isomondromy deformations, the coefficients of the associated linear matrix
ODE are parametrized by the functions
u(\tau)
and
1/u(\tau)
. Therefore, in Section 4 of
[
19
]
,
we distinguished and studied the solutions of equation (
1.1
) which have sequences of zeros accumulating at

\tau=0
: these solutions are characterized by the values of the branching parameter
\rho=\mathrm{i}\varkappa
,

\varkappa\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. The monodromy data of these solutions are defined in Theorem B.1
of
[
17
]
. These monodromy data contain the restrictions
g_{11}g_{22}\neq 0
and
|\mathrm{Im}\,a|<1
,
which are removed in
[
19
]
and in Theorem
3.2
, respectively.
The result obtained in
[
19
]
states that there are two sequences of zeros of
u(\tau)

accumulating at
\tau=0
: one sequence corresponds to the expansion (
6.14
), whilst the other
corresponds to the expansion (
6.15
). The location of the members of these sequences is described
in terms of the location of the zeros of the leading term of asymptotics of these solutions, namely, the zeros
of the leading term of asymptotics are surrounded by
\mathcal{D}_{p}
-like discs, and Theorem 4.2 of

[
19
]
states that, for small enough
\tau
, each disc contains one, and only one,
zero and no poles of our solution. Equipped with this information regarding the zeros accumulating at the origin,
and the fact that the solutions that possess such sequences of zeros are the “seed solutions” of the
Bäcklund transformation(s) for the solutions studied in this section, we employ a one-to-one correspondence
argument between poles and zeros of the solutions related via the Bäcklund transformations, and arrive at
Theorem
6.2
formulated above.

As explained above, the discs containing the poles are precisely the same discs containing the zeros in
Theorem 4.2 of
[
19
]
; in particular, the parameter
\delta_{d}<2
coincides for both discs.
In
[
19
]
, we stated that the largest possible value of
\delta_{d}
could be
2
, but it
necessitates increasing the value of the
J
-like parameter in the definition of the radius of the discs,
depending on the monodromy data of the solution
u(\tau)
.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

In the case of the zeros, the estimate for
\delta_{d}
is easy enough to obtain. Denote by
\tau_{0}
and

\tau_{\hat{0}}
, respectively, the zeros of
u(\tau)
and
u_{as}(\tau)
,
28
28

28

We denote by

u_{as}(\tau)
the level-one terms of the expansion (
A.1
) that coincide with the leading
term of asymptotics obtained in Theorem B.1 of
[
17
]
.
which belong to an open disc of radius

R_{d}=\mathcal{O}\big(\tau_{\hat{0}}^{1+\delta_{d}}\big)
centred at
\tau_{\hat{0}}
;
then, the expansion (
A.1
) can be presented in the form

u(\tau)-u_{as}(\tau)=\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{3}\big)
. Thus, we find that

u(\tau_{\hat{0}})=\mathcal{O}\big(\tau_{\hat{0}}^{3}\big)
. The expansions (
6.14
) and
(
6.15
) imply that

\tau_{\hat{0}}-\tau_{0}=\mathcal{O}\big(\tau_{\hat{0}}^{3}\big)
as
\tau_{\hat{0}}\to 0
. Writing

3=1+\delta_{d}
, it follows that
\delta_{d}=2
; however, we consider an open disc, so that, in fact,
\delta_{d}<2
.
The proof that each such disc, for small enough
\tau_{\hat{0}}
, actually contains one, and only one, zero of

u(\tau)
is given in
[
19
]
.

\blacksquare

Remark 6.5

(
Direct Proof of Theorem
6.2
).

A direct proof that
\delta_{d}<2
in the case of the poles is more involved, because one cannot simply
substitute poles of the solution or its asymptotics into the expansion (
A.17
)
(see Appendix
A.2
), because it would lead to a contradiction. In the case
of the poles, therefore, the estimate
\delta_{d}<2
, without reference to the corresponding estimate for the zeros,
manifests differently.

We now consider a direct proof that the disc
\mathcal{D}_{p}
contains, for large enough
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
one, and only one, pole of the solution
u(\tau)
, together with the corresponding estimate for
\delta_{d}
.
Consider the same integral that was used in the proof of Lemma 4.1 in
[
19
]
, and calculate
it using the Residue Theorem:

I:=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\varointctrclockwise\limits_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{p}}\tau\big(u(\tau)-u_{as}(\tau)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\tau=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\tau_{\hat{p}_{k}}+\frac{1}{4}\tau_{p}=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\tau_{\hat{p}_{k}}-\tau_{p})-(n-1)\frac{1}{4}\tau_{p},

(6.16)

where
\tau_{\hat{p}_{k}}
are the proposed poles, if any,
29
29

29

The number of poles,
n
, can be
equal to
0
, in which case, the sum vanishes.
of the solution
u(\tau)
, and
\tau_{p}
is the pole of the
leading term of asymptotics
u_{as}(\tau)
(cf. equation (
6.7
)).
Then, we can estimate this integral from below as follows:

|I|>\frac{1}{4}(|n-1||\tau_{p}|-nR_{p})=\frac{|\tau_{p}|}{4}\big(|n-1|-n|\tau_{p}|^{\delta_{d}}\big).

(6.17)

In the event that we continue to follow the scheme delineated in
[
19
]
, we have to estimate

I
from above by using the correction term for the function
\tau\big(u(\tau)-u_{as}(\tau)\big)
. According to
equations (
A.12
) and (
A.17
) in
Appendix
A.2
, this term equals
yA_{1}(x)
(cf. equation (
A.22
)),
where
y=\tau^{-\sigma}
and
x=\tau^{2+\sigma}
, and, as a consequence of the symmetry
\sigma\to-\sigma
,
we can take
\sigma=-4\varrho=-2-4\mathrm{i}\varkappa
.
The function
A_{1}(x)
has a third-order pole; therefore, expanding it in a neigbourhood of
\tau=\tau_{p}\to 0
,
one finds that

yA_{1}(x)\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau,\tau_{p}\in\mathcal{D}_{p}\\
\tau\to 0\end{subarray}}{=}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\tau_{p}^{5}}{(\tau-\tau_{p})^{3}}\right),\quad y=\tau^{2+4\mathrm{i}\varkappa},\quad x=\tau^{-4\mathrm{i}\varkappa},\quad\varkappa\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.

(6.18)

Using the estimate (
6.18
) and the fact that
\tau\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{p}
, so that

|\tau-\tau_{p}|=R_{p}
, one finds

|I|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau,\tau_{p}\in\mathcal{D}_{p}\\
\tau\to 0\end{subarray}}{<}\left|\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|\tau_{p}|^{5}}{R_{p}^{2}}\right)\right|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau,\tau_{p}\in\mathcal{D}_{p}\\
\tau\to 0\end{subarray}}{=}\left|\mathcal{O}\left(|\tau_{p}|^{3-2\delta_{d}}\right)\right|.

(6.19)

Comparing the inequalities (
6.17
) and (
6.19
), we see that, in
order to prove that
n=1
, we must impose the condition
1<3-2\delta_{d}
, i.e.,
\delta_{d}<1
, rather than
\delta_{d}<2
!
To reconcile this situation, we have to take for
u_{as}(\tau)
the sequence of the first
m-1
terms of the
asymptotic expansion (cf. Appendix
A.2
,
equations (
A.12
) and (
A.17
)). In this case,
the last omitted term is of the order

y^{m}A_{m}(x)\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau,\tau_{p}\in\mathcal{D}_{p}\\
\tau\to 0\end{subarray}}{=}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\tau_{p}^{3m+2}}{(\tau-\tau_{p})^{m+2}}\right),

(6.20)

so that, repeating
verbatim
the estimate of
I
for the case
m=1
, one arrives at

|I|<\left|\mathcal{O}\big(|\tau_{p}|^{2m+1-(m+1)\delta_{d}}\big)\right|\quad\Rightarrow\quad\delta_{d}<\frac{2m}{m+1}.

(6.21)

Thus, for the poles,
\delta_{d}
can be taken equal to any positive number less than
2
. The last fact implies that
the pole of the function
u(\tau)
is located in the closed disc with radius

R_{p}=J_{p}|\tau_{p}|^{1+\delta_{d}}
, with
\delta_{d}=2
; increasing, if necessary,
J_{p}
, we can assume that the pole of

u(\tau)
is located in some open disc
\mathcal{D}_{p}
.

The absence of zeros in
\mathcal{D}_{p}
can be proved by using the Argument Principle; here, we should exploit
the fact that the leading term of asymptotics does not have zeros in some small enough cut neighbourhood of the
origin.

\blacksquare

There are special cases of the parameter of formal monodromy
a
which are excluded in the formulation of
Theorem
6.1
, and, subsequently, Theorem
6.2

(cf. condition (
6.4
)); however,
for these values of
a
, equation (
1.1
) still possesses solutions with poles accumulating at the origin.

The analogue of the asymptotic results stated in Theorem
6.1
for the
condition (
6.4
) is formulated in Theorems
6.3
and

6.4
below, because the monodromy data of the
corresponding solutions are different. At the same time, it is possible to combine both cases and formulate the
analogue of—the “disc”—Theorem
6.2
as a single theorem, namely,
Theorem
6.5
(see below).

The problem with the vanishing denominators that occurs in the leading terms of the asymptotics
derived in Theorems
3.1
and
3.1
′
,
the consideration of which is the starting point of this section, also takes place with the denominators of the
leading terms of the asymptotics presented in the respective items
(1)
of
Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
. The analysis of the vanishing
denominators in the latter theorems completes the description of solutions with the poles accumulating at the origin,
and corresponds to the values of the parameter of formal monodromy excluded in Theorem
6.1
.

Define the poles (cf. equation (
6.1
) for
\tau_{p}
with
k=1
)

\hat{\tau}_{p}=\exp\left(-\frac{\pi p}{2|\varkappa|}+\frac{\pi}{4\varkappa}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4\varkappa}\ln\frac{\hat{\omega}_{1}}{\hat{\omega}_{2}}\right),\qquad\hat{\omega}_{j}=\hat{w}_{j}|_{\varrho=1/2+\mathrm{i}\varkappa},\quad j=1,2,

(6.22)

where
\hat{w}_{1}
and
\hat{w}_{2}
are defined by equations (
4.5
) and (
4.6
), respectively.
If we introduce “hats”, that is,
\tau_{p}\rightarrow\hat{\tau}_{p}
,
\mathcal{D}_{p}\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{p}
,
and
\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{S}}
, then the entire discussion subsequent to
equation (
6.1
) until Theorem
6.1
applies without change to the

6.1
.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
(u(\tau),\varphi(\tau))
be a solution of the system (
1.1
), (
2.1
) corresponding to the
monodromy data
(a,s_{0}^{0},s_{0}^{\infty},s_{1}^{\infty},g_{11},g_{12},g_{21},g_{22})
.
Suppose that

a=2\varkappa+\mathrm{i}(2n+1),\;\;\mathrm{where}\;\varkappa\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},\;n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0},\quad s_{0}^{\infty}=0,\quad\mathrm{and}\quad s_{1}^{\infty}\neq 0.

(6.23)

Then,
g_{21}\neq 0
, and the remaining monodromy data are given by the following equations:

s_{0}^{0}=-2\mathrm{i}\cosh(2\pi\varkappa),\quad g_{11}=-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\varkappa}g_{21},\quad g_{12}=-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\varkappa}-\mathrm{i}s_{1}^{\infty}g_{21}^{2}}{2\sinh(2\pi\varkappa)g_{21}},\quad g_{22}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}+\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\varkappa}s_{1}^{\infty}g_{21}^{2}}{2\sinh(2\pi\varkappa)g_{21}}.

(6.24)

Let

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}_{1}=

\displaystyle\left(\tfrac{\varepsilon b}{2}\right)^{-\mathrm{i}\varkappa}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}+\pi\mathrm{i}(n+1)}\,\frac{2\pi}{n!}\,\frac{\Gamma(1+2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}{\Gamma(1-2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-5\pi\varkappa/2}}{s_{1}^{\infty}g_{21}},

(6.25)

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}_{2}=

\displaystyle\left(\tfrac{\varepsilon b}{2}\right)^{\mathrm{i}\varkappa}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}-\pi\mathrm{i}(n+1)}\,\frac{2\pi}{\Gamma(n+1-2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}\frac{\Gamma(1-2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}{\Gamma(1+2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-3\pi\varkappa/2}g_{21}.

(6.26)

Assume that
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
is defined as
\mathcal{S}
in (
6.2
) with

\tau_{p}\rightarrow\hat{\tau}_{p}
,
\mathcal{D}_{p}\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{p}
, and
\delta_{d}\in[0,2);

then,

\displaystyle u(\tau)\underset{\scriptscriptstyle\hat{\mathcal{S}}\ni\tau\to 0}{=}

\displaystyle-\frac{4\varepsilon\varkappa^{2}\hat{\omega}_{1}\hat{\omega}_{2}\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2-\delta_{d}}\big)\Big)}{\tau\big(\hat{\omega}_{1}\tau^{-2\mathrm{i}\varkappa}+\hat{\omega}_{2}\tau^{2\mathrm{i}\varkappa}\big)^{2}},

(6.27)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}\underset{\scriptscriptstyle\hat{\mathcal{S}}\ni\tau\to 0}{=}

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\varkappa-\pi\mathrm{i}(n+1)}\frac{2\pi}{\hat{\omega}_{1}\hat{\omega}_{2}}\big(2\tau^{2}\big)^{-2n-1+2\mathrm{i}\varkappa}\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2-\delta_{d}}\big)\Big).

(6.28)

Proof.

This theorem is a refined formulation of the results presented in item
(1)
of
Theorem
4.1
for a specific choice of the formal monodromy
a
given
in (
6.23
). This formulation is required because in this, and only this, case
the denominator of the corresponding leading term of asymptotics of
u(\tau)
(cf. equation (
4.3
))
vanishes at the sequence of points
\hat{\tau}_{p}\to 0
.
To see this, note that the denominator in equation (
4.3
) vanishes at an infinite number of
points iff
\varrho=1/2+\mathrm{i}\varkappa
,
\varkappa\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. On the other hand,
in item
(1)
of Theorem
4.1
, the parameter
\varrho=1+n+\mathrm{i}a/2
,

n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. Solving the equation
\varrho=1+n+\mathrm{i}a/2=1/2+\mathrm{i}\varkappa
, one arrives at
the formula for the formal monodromy
a
given in (
6.23
). The formulae for the remaining
monodromy data given in the list (
6.24
) are obtained from the corresponding
formulae (
4.2
) for this choice of
a
.

The formulae for the asymptotics (
6.27
) and (
6.28
),
respectively, coincide with the corresponding asymptotics (
4.3
) and
(
4.4
) for
\varrho=1/2+\mathrm{i}\varkappa
, where, for brevity, we introduced the notation

\hat{\omega}_{j}=\hat{w}_{j}|_{\varrho=1/2+\mathrm{i}\varkappa}
,
j=1,2
.

The solution of the problem for the vanishing denominators is similar to that presented in
Theorem
6.1
, and is solved by restricting the asymptotic formulae to the
multiply-connected domain
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
defined in the theorem.
∎

To remove the restriction (
6.4
) for non-positive values of

, we can refer to
Theorem

. In order to formulate the corresponding result

Define the poles (cf. equation (

\tilde{\tau}_{p}=\exp\left(-\frac{\pi p}{2|\varkappa|}+\frac{\pi}{4\varkappa}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4\varkappa}\ln\frac{\tilde{\omega}_{3}}{\tilde{\omega}_{4}}\right),\qquad\tilde{\omega}_{j}=\tilde{w}_{j}|_{\varrho=1/2-\mathrm{i}\varkappa},\quad j=3,4,

(6.29)

where

If we introduce “tilde” variables, that is,

,

\mathcal{D}_{p}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{p}
, and

)
until Theorem

to formulate an analogue of Theorem

.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
(u(\tau),\varphi(\tau))
be a solution of the system (
1.1
), (
2.1
) corresponding to the
monodromy data
(a,s_{0}^{0},s_{0}^{\infty},s_{1}^{\infty},g_{11},g_{12},g_{21},g_{22})
.
Suppose that

a=2\varkappa-\mathrm{i}(2n+1),\;\;\mathrm{where}\;\varkappa\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},\;n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0},\quad s_{1}^{\infty}=0,\quad\mathrm{and}\quad s_{0}^{\infty}\neq 0.

(6.30)

Then,
g_{12}\neq 0
, and the remaining monodromy data are given by the following equations:

s_{0}^{0}=-2\mathrm{i}\cosh(2\pi\varkappa),\quad g_{11}=\frac{s_{0}^{\infty}g_{12}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\varkappa}+\mathrm{i}}{2\sinh(2\pi\varkappa)g_{12}},\quad g_{21}=\frac{\mathrm{i}s_{0}^{\infty}g_{12}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi\varkappa}-\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\varkappa}}{2\sinh(\pi a)g_{12}},\quad g_{22}=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\varkappa}g_{12}.

(6.31)

Let

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}_{3}=

\displaystyle\left(\tfrac{\varepsilon b}{2}\right)^{\mathrm{i}\varkappa}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}-\pi\mathrm{i}n}\,\frac{2\pi}{n!}\,\frac{\Gamma(1-2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}{\Gamma(1+2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{3\pi\varkappa/2}}{s_{0}^{\infty}g_{12}},

(6.32)

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}_{4}=

\displaystyle\left(\tfrac{\varepsilon b}{2}\right)^{-\mathrm{i}\varkappa}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}+\pi\mathrm{i}(n+1)}\frac{2\pi}{\Gamma(n+1+2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}\frac{\Gamma(1+2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}{\Gamma(1-2\mathrm{i}\varkappa)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-3\pi\varkappa/2}g_{12}.

(6.33)

Assume that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is defined as
\mathcal{S}
in (
6.2
) with

\tau_{p}\rightarrow\tilde{\tau}_{p}
,
\mathcal{D}_{p}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{p}
, and
\delta_{d}\in[0,2);

then,

\displaystyle u(\tau)\underset{\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}\ni\tau\to 0}{=}

\displaystyle-\frac{4\varepsilon\varkappa^{2}\tilde{\omega}_{3}\tilde{\omega}_{4}\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2-\delta_{d}}\big)\Big)}{\tau\big(\tilde{\omega}_{3}\tau^{2\mathrm{i}\varkappa}+\tilde{\omega}_{4}\tau^{-2\mathrm{i}\varkappa}\big)^{2}},

(6.34)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}\underset{\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}\ni\tau\to 0}{=}

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\pi\varkappa+\pi\mathrm{i}(n+1)}\frac{\tilde{\omega}_{3}\tilde{\omega}_{4}}{2\pi}\big(2\tau^{2}\big)^{2n+1+2\mathrm{i}\varkappa}\Big(1+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2-\delta_{d}}\big)\Big).

(6.35)

Proof.

The proof of this theorem is similar to the proof of Theorem
6.3
; more precisely,
it is a refined formulation of the results presented in item
(1)
of
Theorem
4.2
for the formal monodromy
a
given in (
6.30
).
In this case, the parameter
\varkappa
is defined via the relation

\varrho=1+n-\mathrm{i}a/2=1/2-\mathrm{i}\varkappa
,
\varkappa\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.

The formulae for the asymptotics (
6.34
) and (
6.35
), respectively,
coincide with the corresponding asymptotics (
4.22
) and
(
4.23
) for
\varrho=1/2-\mathrm{i}\varkappa
, where, for brevity, we introduced the notation

\tilde{\omega}_{j}=\tilde{w}_{j}|_{\varrho=1/2-\mathrm{i}\varkappa}
,
j=3,4
.

The solution of the problem for the vanishing denominators is similar to
that presented in Theorem
6.1
, and is solved by restricting the asymptotic formulae to the
multiply-connected domain
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
defined in the theorem.
∎

We conclude this section by formulating two statements regarding the properties of the zeros and poles of the
solutions considered in Theorems
6.3
and
6.4
.
These properties are precisely the same as those formulated in Corollary
6.1
and
Theorem
6.2
for the solutions presented in Theorem
6.1
.
The proofs for the statements formulated below do not rely on any particular parametrization(s) for the zeros and poles
in terms of the monodromy data, and, therefore, coincide with the proofs of the corresponding statements given above.

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
(u(\tau),\varphi(\tau))
be a solution of the system (
1.1
), (
2.1
)
corresponding to the monodromy data
(a,s_{0}^{0},s_{0}^{\infty},s_{1}^{\infty},g_{11},g_{12},g_{21},g_{22})
.
Suppose that the conditions (
6.23
) of Theorem
6.3
or
the conditions (
6.30
) of Theorem
6.4
are satisfied,
which imply the equations (
6.24
) or
(
6.31
), respectively.

For
\epsilon>0
, define

\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\epsilon}:=\left\{\tau\in\hat{\mathcal{S}}:|\tau|<\epsilon\right\}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\epsilon}:=\left\{\tau\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}:|\tau|<\epsilon\right\}.

Then, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
u(\tau)
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
have neither zeros nor poles
in
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\epsilon}
or
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\epsilon}
, respectively.

Theorem 6.5
.

For large enough
p\in\mathbb{N}
, each disc
\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{p}
or
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{p}
contains one,
and only one, pole and no zeros of the solution
u(\tau)
corresponding to the monodromy data defined in
Theorem
6.3
or Theorem
6.4
, respectively.

7
Meromorphic Solutions

Substituting the pole-like expansion for
u(\tau)
into equation (
1.1
), one immediately observes that
solutions of this equation cannot have a pole at the origin; thus, all meromorphic solutions admit a Taylor-series
expansion centred at
\tau=0
.
The first three theorems of this section concern the parametrization via the monodromy data of meromorphic solutions
vanishing at the origin, while the fourth theorem deals with non-vanishing meromorphic solutions.

Theorem 7.1
.

Assume that
s_{0}^{\infty}=s_{1}^{\infty}=0;
then,
a\neq\mathrm{i}k
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and

s_{0}^{0}=2\mathrm{i}\cosh(\pi a),\quad g_{11}=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}g_{21},\quad g_{22}=-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi a}g_{12},\quad g_{12}g_{21}=-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\pi a}}{2\sinh(\pi a)}.

(7.1)

The corresponding functions
u(\tau)
and
\varphi(\tau)
are meromorphic with the following Taylor-series expansions
centred at
\tau=0
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon u(\tau)=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\tilde{b}_{2k-1,0}\tau^{2k-1},

(7.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{\pi a}\Gamma^{2}(1-\mathrm{i}a)}{2\pi ag_{12}^{2}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon b}{4}\right)^{\mathrm{i}a}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\tilde{p}_{N}\tau^{2N}\right),

(7.3)

where
\tilde{b}_{2k-1,0}=b_{2k-1,0}|_{\sigma=-2\mathrm{i}a}
and
\tilde{p}_{N}=p_{N}|_{\sigma=-2\mathrm{i}a}
, with the coefficients

b_{2k-1,0}
and
p_{N}
defined in Appendix
A
and
equations (
4.13
), (
4.14
), respectively.

Proof.

Substituting the conditions
s_{0}^{\infty}=s_{1}^{\infty}=0
into
equations (
2.2
)–(
2.6
), one finds that
a\neq\mathrm{i}k
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
shows that the monodromy data satisfy the conditions (
7.1
).
We now address the expansion (
A.1
) and equations (
A.11
) for
b_{1,\pm 1}
.
Using the explicit expressions for
\varpi_{n}(\pm\rho)
,
n=1,2
, given in
[
15
]

(see, also,
[
17
]
), we get
b_{1,\pm 1}=0
; thus, we arrive at the expansion (
7.2
).
The value for
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi(0)}
in equation (
7.3
) is obtained from the general asymptotics
as
\tau\to 0
for
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
given in Appendix B, Theorem B.1 of
[
17
]
, and the
Taylor series in the argument of the exponential function in equation (
7.3
) is derived with
the aid of equation (
2.1
).
∎

Theorem 7.2
.

Assume that
s_{1}^{\infty}=0
,
a=\mathrm{i}(n-1/2)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
s_{0}^{\infty}\in\mathbb{C}
, and

g_{12}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\};
then,

s_{0}^{0}=0,\quad g_{11}=\frac{(-1)^{n}-s_{0}^{\infty}g_{12}^{2}}{2g_{12}},\quad g_{21}=-\frac{1+(-1)^{n}s_{0}^{\infty}g_{12}^{2}}{2g_{12}},\quad g_{22}=(-1)^{n}g_{12}\neq 0.

(7.4)

The corresponding functions
u(\tau)
and
\varphi(\tau)
are meromorphic with the following Taylor-series
expansions centred at
\tau=0
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon u(\tau)

\displaystyle\underset{\tau\to 0}{=}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}c_{m}\tau^{m}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\hat{b}_{2k-1,0}\tau^{2k-1}+\hat{b}_{1,1}\tau^{2n}+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2n+1}\big),

(7.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}

\displaystyle\underset{\tau\to 0}{=}\frac{(-1)^{n}\,\mathrm{i}\,\big((2n-1)!!\big)^{2}}{2(\varepsilon b)^{n-1/2}g_{12}^{2}(2n-1)}\exp\left(-(2n-1)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\xi_{n}\frac{\tau^{n}}{n}\right),

(7.6)

where

\displaystyle c_{m}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}2k-1+l(2n-1)=m\\
k\geqslant 1,\;0\leqslant l\leqslant k\end{subarray}}\hat{b}_{2k-1,l},\qquad\quad\left.\hat{b}_{2k-1,l}=b_{2k-1,l}\vphantom{M^{M^{M^{M}}}}\right|_{\begin{subarray}{l}\scriptscriptstyle{a=\mathrm{i}(n-1/2)}\\
\scriptscriptstyle{\sigma=2n-1}\end{subarray}}\,\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\frac{2a}{\varepsilon b}\right)^{k}\sum_{m_{i}\in M_{k,n}}\frac{(m_{1}+\dotsb+m_{n})!}{m_{1}!\,\dotsb\,m_{n}!}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i+1})^{m_{i}},

where the summation set
M_{k,n}
coincides with
M_{k,N}
for
N=n

(
cf. equations (
4.14
)
)
,
and the coefficients
b_{2k-1,l}
are defined in Appendix
A.1
. For

1\leqslant l\leqslant k
, these coefficients depend on
b
and
b_{1,1};
the parameter
b
and the coefficient

b_{1,1}
must be modified as follows:
b\to\varepsilon b
and
b_{1,1}\to\hat{b}_{1,1}
, where

\hat{b}_{1,1}=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}n}{2}}\big(\varepsilon b\big)^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2^{2n}s_{0}^{\infty}g_{12}^{2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\big((2n-1)!!\big)^{3}}.

(7.7)

In fact,
\hat{b}_{1,1}
is the first coefficient in the Taylor series that depends on the monodromy data.

Proof.

This is a special case of Theorem
4.2
, item
(3)
for
\sigma=-2\mathrm{i}a
and
a=\mathrm{i}(n-1/2)
.
∎

Theorem 7.3
.

Assume that
s_{0}^{\infty}=0
,
a=-\mathrm{i}(n-1/2)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
s_{1}^{\infty}\in\mathbb{C}
, and

g_{21}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\};
then,

s_{0}^{0}=0,\quad g_{22}=\frac{(-1)^{n}-s_{1}^{\infty}g_{21}^{2}}{2g_{21}},\quad g_{12}=-\frac{1+(-1)^{n}s_{1}^{\infty}g_{21}^{2}}{2g_{21}},\quad g_{11}=(-1)^{n}g_{21}\neq 0.

(7.8)

The corresponding functions
u(\tau)
and
\varphi(\tau)
are meromorphic with the following Taylor-series
expansions centred at
\tau=0
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon u(\tau)

\displaystyle\underset{\tau\to 0}{=}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}d_{m}\tau^{m}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\check{b}_{2k-1,0}\tau^{2k-1}+\check{b}_{1,-1}\tau^{2n}+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{2n+1}\big),

(7.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}

\displaystyle\underset{\tau\to 0}{=}\frac{(-1)^{n}\,\mathrm{i}\,\big((2n-1)!!\big)^{2}}{2(\varepsilon b)^{n-1/2}g_{21}^{2}(2n-1)}\exp\left(-(2n-1)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\nu_{n}\frac{\tau^{n}}{n}\right),

(7.10)

where

\displaystyle d_{m}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}2k-1+l(2n-1)=m\\
k\geqslant 1,\;0\leqslant l\leqslant k\end{subarray}}\check{b}_{2k-1,-l},\qquad\quad\left.\check{b}_{2k-1,l}=b_{2k-1,l}\vphantom{M^{M^{M^{M}}}}\right|_{\begin{subarray}{l}\scriptscriptstyle{a=-\mathrm{i}(n-1/2)}\\
\scriptscriptstyle{\sigma=-(2n-1)}\end{subarray}}\,\,,

\displaystyle\nu_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\frac{2a}{\varepsilon b}\right)^{k}\sum_{m_{i}\in M_{k,n}}\frac{(m_{1}+\ldots+m_{n})!}{m_{1}!\cdot\ldots\cdot m_{n}!}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(d_{i+1})^{m_{i}},

where the summation set
M_{k,n}
coincides with
M_{k,N}
for
N=n

(
cf. equations (
4.14
)
)
,
and the coefficients
b_{2k-1,-l}
are defined in Appendix
A.1
.
For
1\leqslant l\leqslant k
, these coefficients depend on
b
and
b_{1,-1};
the parameter
b
and
the coefficient
b_{1,-1}
must be modified as follows:
b\to\varepsilon b
and
b_{1,-1}\to\check{b}_{1,-1}
,
where

\check{b}_{1,-1}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}n}{2}}\big(\varepsilon b\big)^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2^{2n}s_{1}^{\infty}g_{21}^{2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\big((2n-1)!!\big)^{3}}.

(7.11)

In fact,
\check{b}_{1,-1}
is the first coefficient in the Taylor series that depends on the monodromy data.

Proof.

This is a special case of Theorem
4.1
, item
(3)
for
\sigma=-2\mathrm{i}a
and
a=-\mathrm{i}(n-1/2)
.
∎

Remark 7.1
.

The solutions presented in Theorem
7.1
were considered in
[
11
]
.
Theorems
7.1
–
7.3
describe all
meromorphic solutions of equation (
1.1
) for
a\in\mathbb{C}
that vanish at the origin.
This fact follows from the local analysis
presented in Lemma 2.1 of
[
14
]
. For the case
a\neq\mathrm{i}k
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, this can be
deduced independently from the results presented in Sections
3
–
5
.
The monodromy data for the vanishing solutions presented in
Theorem
7.1
were calculated by another method—based on the odd symmetry of
the solutions—in
[
14
]
(see Proposition 7.1 in
[
14
]
); however, the corresponding
asymptotic formula for the function
\varphi(\tau)
was not obtained in
[
14
]
.

For
n=1
, the monodromy data for the one-parameter family of solutions
u(\tau)
vanishing at the origin presented
in Theorems
7.2
and
7.3
were calculated by an alternative
method (via Bäcklund transformations applied to non-vanishing meromorphic solutions) in
[
17
]
; however,
the corresponding asymptotics for
\varphi(\tau)
was not obtained in
[
17
]
.

\blacksquare

Theorem 7.4
.

If
u(\tau)
is a meromorphic solution of equation (
1.1
) with
u(0)\neq 0
, then

\sigma=4\rho=1
, and thus
s_{0}^{0}=0
.
Depending on the values of the Stokes multipliers at the point at infinity, the Taylor-series expansion centred at

\tau=0
of the function
u(\tau)
and the corresponding asymptotics of
\varphi(\tau)
can be parametrized in terms
of the monodromy data in one of the following ways:

(1)

s_{0}^{\infty}s_{1}^{\infty}\neq 0\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;g_{11}\neq\pm g_{21}
,
g_{22}\neq\pm g_{12}
, and

a\neq\mathrm{i}(k+1/2)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z};
thus,

\displaystyle\varepsilon u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0}{=}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}f_{m}\tau^{m}=\tilde{b}_{1,-1}+(\tilde{b}_{1,0}+\tilde{b}_{3,-2})\tau+(\tilde{b}_{1,1}+\tilde{b}_{3,-1}+\tilde{b}_{5,-3})\tau^{2}+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{3}\big),

(7.12)

\displaystyle f_{m}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}2k-1+l=m\\
-k\leqslant l\leqslant k\end{subarray}}\tilde{b}_{2k-1,l},\qquad\tilde{b}_{2k-1,l}=b_{2k-1,l}\lvert_{\sigma=1,b\to\varepsilon b},\quad k\geqslant 1,\;\;|l|\leqslant k,

(7.13)

where the coefficients
b_{2k-1,l}
are defined in Appendix
A
;
in particular,
one finds that

\tilde{b}_{1,0}=2a\varepsilon b\;\;\mathrm{and}\;\;\tilde{b}_{1,1}=b^{2}(a^{2}+1/4)/\tilde{b}_{1,-1}.

(7.14)

The monodromy parametrization of the leading coefficient reads:

\tilde{b}_{1,-1}=\left(\frac{\varepsilon b}{2}\right)^{1/2}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi a}{2}}}{2\pi}\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{4}-\mathrm{i}\frac{a}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{4}+\mathrm{i}\frac{a}{2}\right)(g_{11}+g_{21})(g_{12}+g_{22}),

(7.15)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}\underset{\tau\to 0}{=}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{4}-\frac{\mathrm{i}a}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\mathrm{i}a}{2}\right)}\left(\frac{g_{12}+g_{22}}{g_{11}+g_{21}}\right)(2\tau^{2})^{\mathrm{i}a}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\frac{\varepsilon b}{f_{0}}\left(\tau+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\eta_{n}\frac{\tau^{n+1}}{n+1}\right)\right),

(7.16)

\displaystyle\eta_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{f_{0}^{k}}\sum_{m_{i}\in M_{k,n}}\frac{(m_{1}+\ldots+m_{n})!}{m_{1}!\cdot\ldots\cdot m_{n}!}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(f_{i})^{m_{i}},

(7.17)

where the summation set
M_{k,n}
coincides with
M_{k,N}
for
N=n

(
cf. equations (
4.14
)
)
, and
the numbers
f_{i}
are defined by the second equality in equation (
7.12
)
;

in particular,
f_{0}=\tilde{b}_{1,-1}
.

(2)

s_{0}^{\infty}=0
,
a=\mathrm{i}(m-1/2)
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

(2.1)
a=\mathrm{i}(2n+3/2)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
,
s_{1}^{\infty},g_{21}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
,

g_{11}=-g_{21},\quad g_{12}=\frac{s_{1}^{\infty}g_{21}^{2}-1}{2g_{21}},\quad g_{22}=-\frac{s_{1}^{\infty}g_{21}^{2}+1}{2g_{21}}.

The function
\varepsilon u(\tau)
is given by—the same—equations (
7.12
),
(
7.13
), and (
7.14
), with

.
Equations (
7.15
) and (
7.16
) should be
changed to

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{1,-1}=-\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{4}\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon b}}{s_{1}^{\infty}g_{21}^{2}}\frac{(2n+1)!!}{(2n)!!},

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\frac{(-1)^{n}(2n+1)!s_{1}^{\infty}}{\sqrt{2\pi}(2\tau)^{4n+3}}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\frac{\varepsilon b}{f_{0}}\left(\tau+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\eta_{n}\frac{\tau^{n+1}}{n+1}\right)\right),

where the coefficients

are given by equation (

) accompanied with the same specialization for

.

n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}

(7.19)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}(2\tau)^{4n+1}}{(-1)^{n}(2n)!s_{0}^{\infty}}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\frac{\varepsilon b}{f_{0}}\left(\tau+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\eta_{n}\frac{\tau^{n+1}}{n+1}\right)\right),

Proof.

Begin with the proof of the asymptotics for the meromorphic solutions presented in item
(1)
of the theorem.
Assume that
u(\tau)
has a Taylor-series expansion centred at

with

; this expansion should
