A.1
) for
\sigma=\pm 1
.
Due to the symmetry
\sigma\to-\sigma
discussed in Subsection
A.1
, the
expansions with
\sigma=\pm 1
correspond to the same monodromy data; thus, we can put
\sigma=4\rho=1
, so that

s_{0}^{0}=0
(cf. equation (
3.1
)). Equation (
3.1
)
also implies that, if we require
s_{0}^{\infty}s_{1}^{\infty}\neq 0
, then
a\neq\mathrm{i}(k+1/2)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. The other
conditions on the monodromy data are a consequence of equations (
2.4
) and (
2.5
).

The series (
7.12
), with the coefficients (
7.13
) and
(
7.14
), is a rearrangement of the series (
A.1
) for

\sigma=1
. The formula (
7.15
) for

\tilde{b}_{1,-1}
is calculated via equation (
A.11
), where
\beta_{1,-1}=\tilde{b}_{1,-1}
, and
explicit expressions for
\varpi_{n}(\pm\rho)
,
n=1,2
, are given in Appendix B, Theorem B.1 of
[
17
]
;
the latter theorem also allows one to calculate the leading term of asymptotics as
\tau\to 0
of the function

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
presented in equation (
7.16
). The Taylor series
in the argument of the exponential function
in equation (
7.16
) is obtained with the help of equation (
2.1
).

The proofs of the results presented in items
(2)
and
(3)
are similar; item
(2)
is a
special case of Theorem
4.1
(cf. subitem
(2.1)
for
\varrho=1/4
and
subitem
(2.2)
for
\varrho=3/4
), and item
(3)
is a special case of
Theorem
4.2
(cf. subitem
(3.1)
for
\varrho=1/4
and
subitem
(3.2)
for
\varrho=3/4
).
∎

Appendix A
Appendix. The Complete Small-
\tau
Asymptotic Expansion of the General Solution
u(\tau)

A.1
Error Correction Term of the Power-Like Isomonodromy Asymptotics as
\tau\to 0

The local expansion of the general solution
u(\tau)
of equation (
1.1
) with

\varepsilon=1

in a neighbourhood of
\tau=0
can be presented in the form of the following convergent (for small enough
\tau
) series:

u(\tau)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\tau^{2k-1}\sum_{m=-k}^{k}b_{2k-1,m}\tau^{m\sigma},

(A.1)

where
\sigma\neq 0
and the coefficients
b_{2k-1,m}
are
\tau
-independent complex
numbers.
31
31

31

To get the
\varepsilon
-dependent variant of this expansion, one has to introduce
\varepsilon
on both sides of all the
equations in this appendix according to the rule
u\to\varepsilon u
and
b\to\varepsilon b
.

The parameters
\sigma
and
b_{1,\pm 1}
satisfy the
following conditions:

|\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma|<2,\qquad b_{1,0}=\frac{2ab}{\sigma^{2}},\qquad b_{1,1}b_{1,-1}=\frac{b^{2}(4a^{2}+\sigma^{2})}{4\sigma^{4}};

(A.2)

otherwise, they can be taken arbitrarily.
The remaining coefficients
b_{2k-1,m}
,
k\geqslant 2
,
|m|\leqslant k
, can be determined uniquely in terms of

\sigma
and one of the parameters
b_{1,\pm 1}
upon substitution of the series (
A.1
) into
equation (
1.1
).

There are several methodologies for proving the existence of such local expansions; in the context of the Painlevé
equations, we refer to the papers
[
22
,
23
,
12
,
25
,
5
]
.
In this appendix, we do not consider the formal proof of the expansion (
A.1
), but, rather,
focus our attention on its computational aspects.

To determine the coefficients
b_{2k-1,m}
, substitute the expansion (
A.1
) into
equation (
1.1
), and find that, for
k=1,2,3,\ldots
,

\displaystyle b_{2k+1,k+1}=(-1)^{k}\,\frac{2^{2k}(k+1)b_{1,1}^{k+1}}{(\sigma+2)^{2k}},\quad b_{2k+1,k}=(-1)^{k}\,\frac{2^{2k+2}(2k+2+k\sigma)^{2}ab\,b_{1,1}^{k}}{\sigma^{2}(\sigma+4)^{2}(\sigma+2)^{2k-1}},

(A.3)

\displaystyle b_{2k+1,-k-1}=(-1)^{k}\,\frac{2^{2k}(k+1)b_{1,-1}^{k+1}}{(\sigma-2)^{2k}},\quad b_{2k+1,-k}=(-1)^{k-1}\,\frac{2^{2k+2}(2k+2-k\sigma)^{2}ab\,b_{1,-1}^{k}}{\sigma^{2}(\sigma-4)^{2}(\sigma-2)^{2k-1}}.

(A.4)

We define the
level
of the coefficient
b_{2k-1,m}
to be the number
k
in its first subscript; thus,
the total number of coefficients at level
k
is
2k+1
.
As an example, we present the remaining coefficients for the levels 2 and 3:

b_{3,0}=4b^{2}\frac{(20a^{2}\sigma^{2}+3\sigma^{4}-48a^{2}-4\sigma^{2})}{\sigma^{4}(\sigma+2)^{2}(\sigma-2)^{2}}=-\frac{12a^{2}b^{2}}{\sigma^{4}}+(a^{2}+1)b^{2}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}+4}{(\sigma^{2}-4)^{2}}-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\right),

(A.5)

\begin{gathered}b_{5,1}=4b^{2}b_{1,1}((32\sigma^{5}+8\sigma^{4}-748\sigma^{3}-1120\sigma^{2}+1680\sigma+2880)a^{2}-12\sigma^{6}-71\sigma^{5}\\
-80\sigma^{4}+84\sigma^{3}+144\sigma^{2})/((\sigma-2)^{2}(\sigma+4)(\sigma+2)^{4}\sigma^{4}),\end{gathered}

(A.6)

\begin{gathered}b_{5,-1}=4b^{2}b_{1,-1}((32\sigma^{5}-8\sigma^{4}-748\sigma^{3}+1120\sigma^{2}+1680\sigma-2880)a^{2}+12\sigma^{6}-71\sigma^{5}\\
+80\sigma^{4}+84\sigma^{3}-144\sigma^{2})/((\sigma+2)^{2}(\sigma-4)(\sigma-2)^{4}\sigma^{4}),\end{gathered}

(A.7)

b_{5,0}=\frac{192ab^{3}(7\sigma^{6}+36a^{2}\sigma^{4}-100\sigma^{4}-560a^{2}\sigma^{2}+192\sigma^{2}+1280a^{2})}{\sigma^{6}(\sigma-4)^{2}(\sigma+4)^{2}(\sigma-2)^{2}(\sigma+2)^{2}}.

(A.8)

The expansion (
A.1
) is symmetric with respect to the change
\sigma\to-\sigma
and

b_{2k-1,m}\to b_{2k-1,-m}
. Note that equation (
1.1
) depends neither on the parameter
\sigma
nor on any
of the coefficients
b_{2k-1,m}
, that is, it is also symmetric with respect to the change of variables
indicated above. This means that the coefficients
b_{2k-1,\pm m}
are related to each other
by the change
\sigma\to-\sigma
and
b_{1,1}\to b_{1,-1}
. This property can be observed upon comparing the
left- and right-most equations, respectively, in (
A.3
) and (
A.4
),
and (
A.6
) with (
A.7
).

We computed the coefficients
b_{2k-1,m}
up to the level
9
; therefore,
equations (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) are verified only
for
k=1,2,\ldots,9
.
32
32

32

On an eleven-year-old notebook (4Gb RAM, i7 processor, 4th generation),
Maple
15 computed the coefficients
of four levels, from 2 to 5, in approximately 17 seconds; in contrast, on a notebook with 16Gb RAM, i7 processor,
12th generation,
Maple
17 executes the same computation in roughly 2.5 seconds. The latter notebook computed
the coefficients of the subsequent 4 levels, from 6 to 9, in 333 seconds.
These calculations were carried out without taking into account the symmetry between the coefficients discussed above:
by employing the stated symmetry, the computational time could be reduced by
30
to
35
percent.
In order to illustrate the increase in complexity of the calculation, we refer, say, to equation (
A.6
),
which takes less than two lines of text to display; in contrast, the coefficient
b_{17,1}
, being presented in the
same, explicit way, would require at least 132 lines of text to display!

In Subsection
A.2
below, these formulae are proved for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
The complexity of the coefficients, together with their number, increases quickly with the growth of the level;
therefore, the reader should not be overly optimistic about the prospect of obtaining as many of the coefficients as
necessary for the achievement of the required degree of accuracy.

The simplest application of the expansion (
A.1
) is the calculation of the error-correction term
for the small-
\tau
asymptotics obtained in
[
15
]
. Recall that the small-
\tau
asymptotic formula obtained
in
[
15
]
reads

u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}\,\frac{\tau b\mathrm{e}^{\pi a/2}}{16\pi}\!\left(\varpi_{1}(\rho)\tau^{2\rho}+\varpi_{1}(-\rho)\tau^{-2\rho}\right)\!\left(\varpi_{2}(\rho)\tau^{2\rho}+\varpi_{2}(-\rho)\tau^{-2\rho}\right)\!\left(1+o\big(\tau^{\delta})\right),

(A.9)

where
\delta>0
, and the
\tau
-independent coefficients
\varpi_{n}(\rho)
,
n=1,2
, depend on the branching parameter,

\rho
, and the monodromy data: their explicit formulae are given in
[
15
,
17
]
.
Since equation (
A.9
) is symmetric with respect to the reflection
\rho\to-\rho
, we assume that

\mathrm{Re}\,\rho\geqslant 0
; then, expanding the brackets on the right-hand side of (
A.9
), one arrives at

u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}\beta_{1,-1}\tau^{1-4\rho}+\beta_{1,0}\tau+\beta_{1,1}\tau^{1+4\rho}+o\big(\tau^{1-4\rho+\delta}\big),

(A.10)

where

\beta_{1,\pm 1}=\frac{b\mathrm{e}^{\pi a/2}}{16\pi}\varpi_{1}(\pm\rho)\varpi_{2}(\pm\rho),\qquad\beta_{1,0}=\frac{b\mathrm{e}^{\pi a/2}}{16\pi}\big(\varpi_{1}(\rho)\varpi_{2}(-\rho)+\varpi_{1}(-\rho)\varpi_{2}(\rho)\big).

(A.11)

Now, using the explicit expressions for
\varpi_{n}(\pm\rho)
, which can be taken from either one of the works

[
15
]
or
[
17
]
, one proves that the coefficients
\beta_{1,m}
,
m=-1,0,1
, satisfy the same
equations (
A.2
) (with
\sigma^{2}=(4\rho)^{2}
) as the coefficients
b_{1,m}
with the
corresponding subscripts. Comparing the expansions (
A.10
) and
(
A.1
) and taking into account the symmetry
\sigma\to-\sigma
, we can set
\sigma=4\rho
and

\beta_{k,m}=b_{k,m}
,
m=-1,0,1
. This comparison allows us to derive a more precise evaluation for the parameter

\delta
in the correction term of equation (
A.9
).

The error estimate to the level 1 terms in the expansion (
A.1
) can be written as
\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{\delta_{1}}\big)
, where
\delta_{1}=3-2\mathrm{Re}\,\rho
. If we assume that
1-4\mathrm{Re}\,\rho+\delta\geqslant\delta_{1}
,
then the first three explicit terms in the asymptotics (
A.10
) are larger than the corresponding
error estimate. The solution of equation (
1.1
) with such asymptotics does not exist because substituting this
expansion into (
1.1
) we get a term which cannot be cancelled by the term generated from the correction

o(\tau^{1-4\rho+\delta})
.
33
33

33

The substitution is simpler if the multiplicative
form of the asymptotics (
A.9
) is exploited, and equation (
1.1
) should be integrated from
some finite value
\tau_{0}
to
\tau\to 0
. The small-
\tau
isomonodromy asymptotics is differentiable with respect to
\tau
,
that is, the asymptotics of the derivative
u^{\prime}(\tau)
is the formal derivative of the asymptotics of
u(\tau)
.
There is an
alternative argument which does not require direct substituton; in case
1-4\mathrm{Re}\,\rho+\delta\geqslant\delta_{1}
, then, we
have two different solutions: one defined by the isomonodromy asymptotics, and the other by the full asymptotic expansion; however,
all points of the monodromy manifold are already assigned to the “isomonodromy” solutions via their asymptotics, so the monodromy
manifold does not have any “space” for the solutions defined via the asymptotic expansion (
A.1
).

We now assume that
1-4\mathrm{Re}\,\rho+\delta<\delta_{1}
; then, the asymptotics defined by the expansion (
A.1
)
satisfies the condition for the isomonodromy asymptotics (
A.10
). If we equate the largest terms of these
asymptotics, then, for both solutions defined by these asymptotics, we get, via a direct solution of the monodromy problem
[
15
]
,
the same monodromy data, which means that they coincide, and we see that the correction
o\big(\tau^{1-4\rho+\delta}\big)
in the expansion
(
A.10
) can, in fact, be strengthened to
\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{1-4\rho+\delta}\big)
since
\delta>0
is not
fixed; subsequently, comparing the latter estimate with the largest term of the second level in the expansion (
A.1
), we
obtain
1-4\mathrm{Re}\,\rho+\delta=3-2\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma
, where
\sigma=4\rho
, which implies
\delta=2-4\mathrm{Re}\,\rho
. If one does
not assume that
\mathrm{Re}\,\rho>0
and reverts back to the symmetric form of the asymptotics (
A.9
), then one gets

\delta=2-4|\mathrm{Re}\,\rho|
.

In certain special cases, the evaluation of
\delta
can be improved; assume, say, that
\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma<0
, and consider the degenerate
case
b_{1,1}=0
. (Note that this does not necessarily imply that
b_{1,-1}=0
.) In this case, however, the coefficients
b_{2k-1,m}=0
for all

m\in\mathbb{N}
; it is enough, in fact, to know that
b_{3,2}=b_{3,1}=0
(cf. equations (
A.3
) and (
A.4
)),
so that the largest non-vanishing correction term is
b_{3,0}\tau^{3}
, hence
\delta=2
.

A.2
Super-Generating Function

The formal construction of the
super-generating function for the coefficients of the expansion (
A.1
) is defined as

A(x,y)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}y^{n}A_{n}(x),

(A.12)

where the coefficients
A_{n}(x)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, are generating functions for the coefficients

b_{2k-1,k-n}
,

\displaystyle A_{0}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}b_{2k-1,k}x^{k},

(A.13)

\displaystyle A_{n}(x)=\sum_{k=\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor+1}^{\infty}b_{2k-1,k-n}x^{k},\qquad n\geqslant 1,

(A.14)

where
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
denotes the floor of the real number.
It is easy to see that each coefficient
b_{2k-1,m}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
m=-k,\ldots,k
, belongs to one, and only
one, function
A_{n}(x)
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
.

Define the linear differential operator
D
acting in the space of formal power series of two variables
x
and
y
as

D:=(2+\sigma)x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}-\sigma y\frac{\partial}{\partial y};

(A.15)

then, the function
A\equiv A(x,y)
solves the PDE

D^{2}(\ln A)=-8A+2a\frac{bxy}{A}+\left(\frac{bxy}{A}\right)^{2}.

(A.16)

Note that equation (
A.16
) has the symmetry
x\leftrightarrow y
,

-\sigma\leftrightarrow 2+\sigma
. This symmetry, however, cannot be interpreted in terms of the coefficients

b_{2k-1,m}
because the symmetry has “renotational sense”, that is,
x
plays the role of
y
and

vice versa
(see equation (
A.17
) below).
To justify equation (
A.16
), one has to use the following relation between the super-generating function

A(x,y)
(cf. equation (
A.12
)) and the solution
u(\tau)
(cf.
equation (
A.1
)):

u(\tau)=\frac{1}{\tau}A(\tau^{2+\sigma},\tau^{-\sigma}).

(A.17)

Now, we apply equation (
A.16
) for the calculation of the generating functions
A_{n}(x)
: substitute
the series (
A.12
) into equation (
A.16
), take the numerator of the resulting
equation, and equate to zero the coefficients of powers of
y^{n}
. For
n=0
, we get the following second-order ODE,

D_{x}^{2}\ln A_{0}(x)=-8A_{0}(x),

(A.18)

where

D_{x}:=(2+\sigma)\,x\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}

(A.19)

is the
x
-part of the operator
D
(cf. equation (
A.15
)).
Equation (
A.18
) has the following solutions:

A_{0,gen}(x)=\frac{(2+\sigma)^{2}\,C_{1}^{2}\,C_{2}x^{C_{1}}}{4(x^{C_{1}}+C_{2})^{2}},\qquad A_{0,spec}(x)=-\frac{(\sigma+2)^{2}}{4\ln^{2}(C_{2}x)},

(A.20)

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are constants of integration. To get the solution that is consistent with the definition of

A_{0}(x)
, one must set
C_{1}=1
and
C_{2}=(\sigma+2)^{2}/(4b_{1,1})
, so that

A_{0}(x)=\frac{b_{1,1}x}{(1+4b_{1,1}x/(\sigma+2)^{2})^{2}}=\frac{(\sigma+2)^{2}z}{4(1+z)^{2}},\qquad z=\frac{4b_{1,1}x}{(\sigma+2)^{2}}.

(A.21)

Expanding the function
A_{0}(x)
in equation (
A.21
) into a power series in
x
and comparing this
expansion with equation (
A.13
), one proves the left-most equation in (
A.3
)
and, due to the symmetry
\sigma\to-\sigma
and
b_{1,1}\to b_{1,-1}
(cf.
Subsection
A.1
), also the left-most equation in (
A.4
).

By continuing this process of determining the generating functions described above, one obtains the following
ODE for the function
A_{1}(x)
:

\left(\left(D_{x}-\sigma\right)^{2}+8A_{0}(x)\right)A_{1,0}(x)=\frac{2abx}{A_{0}(x)},\qquad A_{1,0}(x):=\frac{A_{1}(x)}{A_{0}(x)}.

(A.22)

The homogeneous part of equation (
A.22
) is a degenerate hypergeometric equation, all of whose solutions
have, for
\sigma\neq-2+2/n_{1}
,
n_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
, a branching point at
x=0
. Since the right-hand side of
equation (
A.22
) is a rational function of
x
, it follows that, for generic values of
\sigma
and

b_{11}\neq 0
, there exists a unique rational solution
A_{1}(x)
of this equation which, in terms of the variable
z

(cf. equation (
A.21
)), reads

A_{1}(x)=\frac{ab(2+\sigma)^{2}z(z\sigma-\sigma-4)(z^{2}\sigma+2z(\sigma^{2}+4\sigma+2)-\sigma-4)}{2\sigma^{2}(4+\sigma)^{2}b_{1,1}(z+1)^{3}}.

(A.23)

For the special values of
\sigma
mentioned above, we also have the same solution (
A.23
),
because adding to
A_{1}(x)
the rational part of the solution of the homogenous equation invalidates the
expansion (
A.14
), that is, it leads to the appearence of non-positive powers of
x
in the
expansion.
Reverting back to the original variable
x
and developing
A_{1}(x)
into a power series in
x
, one finds that

A_{1}(x)=\frac{2abx}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{16abb_{1,1}x^{2}}{\sigma^{2}(2+\sigma)}+\frac{256ab(3+\sigma)^{2}b_{1,1}^{2}x^{3}}{\sigma^{2}(4+\sigma)^{2}(2+\sigma)^{3}}-\frac{256ab(8+3\sigma)^{2}b_{1,1}^{3}x^{4}}{\sigma^{2}(4+\sigma)^{2}(2+\sigma)^{5}}+\mathcal{O}(x^{5}).

(A.24)

Comparing the expansions (
A.24
) and (
A.14
) term-by-term, one verifies the second
relation given in (
A.2
) and the right-most formula in (
A.3
) for

k=1,2,3
; the formula for arbitrary
k\in\mathbb{N}
given there can be proven by decomposing
A_{1}(x)
into a
sum of partial fractions.

Even though the procedure for the construction of the generating functions
A_{n}(x)
is straightforward, it requires
rather cumbersome calculations, if done by hand. We checked that
Maple
was able to handle
these calculations for
n=2,3,4
in a few seconds; but thus far we haven’t found a compact presentation for
the corresponding results.

Consider, for example, the calculation of the generating function
A_{2}(x)
. This calculation shows, on the one hand,
the increased complexity of the coefficients, and, on the other hand, that it is general enough to estimate the
complexity of successive calculations for the generating functions
A_{n}(x)
.
The function
A_{2}(x)
solves the following ODE:

\left(\left(D_{x}-2\sigma\right)^{2}+8A_{0}\right)A_{2,0}=\frac{1}{2}\left(D_{x}-2\sigma\right)^{2}A_{1,0}^{2}-2abx\frac{A_{1,0}}{A_{0}}+\left(\frac{bx}{A_{0}}\right)^{2},\quad A_{2,0}:=\frac{A_{2}(x)}{A_{0}(x)}.

(A.25)

This equation is similar to equation (
A.18
); however, its right-hand side is more complicated, thus
resulting in a substantially more involved explicit formula for the solution. Every solution that is single-valued
at
x=0
is a rational function of
x
. If one takes into account that the solution should have a first-order zero at

x=0
(cf. equation (
A.14
) for
n=2
), then one arrives at the following partial-fraction
decomposition of
A_{2}(x)
, presented, again, in terms of the variable
z
(cf. the right-most equation
in (
A.21
)):

A_{2}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{3}\xi_{k}z^{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{4}\frac{\xi_{-k}}{(z+1)^{k}},

(A.26)

where the coefficients
\xi_{k}
,
k=-4,-3,-2,-1,0,1,2,3
, satisfy the condition

\xi_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{4}\xi_{-k}=0,

(A.27)

and depend only on
a
and
\sigma
. Explicit formulae for the coefficients
\xi_{k}
read:

\displaystyle\xi_{3}=

\displaystyle\frac{b^{2}(2+\sigma)^{2}((4+\sigma)^{2}+4a^{2})}{16b_{1,1}^{2}(4+\sigma)^{4}},\quad

\displaystyle\xi_{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{b^{2}\left(2+\sigma)^{2}(4(5\sigma^{2}+40\sigma+68)a^{2}+(3\sigma^{2}+24\sigma+44\right)(4+\sigma)^{2})}{4b_{1,1}^{2}(4+\sigma)^{4}(6+\sigma)^{2}},\quad

\displaystyle\xi_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{b^{2}(2+\sigma)^{2}}{16b_{1,1}^{2}\sigma(4+\sigma)^{4}(6+\sigma)^{2}}\left(4(8\sigma^{5}+158\sigma^{4}+1061\sigma^{3}+2964\sigma^{2}+3412\sigma+1152)a^{2}\right.

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left.\sigma(12\sigma^{3}+121\sigma^{2}+380\sigma+388)(4+\sigma)^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\xi_{0}=

\displaystyle-\frac{b^{2}(2+\sigma)^{5}}{b_{1,1}^{2}\sigma^{2}(4+\sigma)^{4}(6+\sigma)^{2}(2-\sigma)^{2}}\left(2(8\sigma^{5}+95\sigma^{4}+184\sigma^{3}-584\sigma^{2}-96\sigma+576)a^{2}\right.

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left.3\sigma^{2}(\sigma^{2}+4\sigma-6)(4+\sigma)^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\xi_{-1}=

\displaystyle\frac{3b^{2}(2+\sigma)^{5}(2+3\sigma)^{2}}{4b_{1,1}^{2}\sigma^{4}(4+\sigma)^{4}(6+\sigma)^{2}(2-\sigma)^{2}}\left(2(4\sigma^{5}+45\sigma^{4}+72\sigma^{3}-344\sigma^{2}-96\sigma+576)a^{2}\right.

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left.\sigma^{2}(\sigma^{2}+4\sigma-6)(4+\sigma)^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\xi_{-2}=

\displaystyle-\frac{b^{2}(2+\sigma)^{6}}{4b_{1,1}^{2}\sigma^{4}(4+\sigma)^{4}(6+\sigma)^{2}(2-\sigma)^{2}}\left(2(148\sigma^{6}+1657\sigma^{5}+2898\sigma^{4}-12584\sigma^{3}\right.

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left.11792\sigma^{2}+22656\sigma+19584)a^{2}+3\sigma^{2}(6+7\sigma)(\sigma^{2}+4\sigma-6)(4+\sigma)^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\xi_{-3}=

\displaystyle\frac{b^{2}(2+\sigma)^{7}}{2b_{1,1}^{2}\sigma^{4}(4+\sigma)^{4}(6+\sigma)^{2}(2-\sigma)^{2}}\left(2(48\sigma^{5}+463\sigma^{4}+248\sigma^{3}\right.

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left.4808\sigma^{2}+1056\sigma+7488)a^{2}+3\sigma^{2}(\sigma^{2}+4\sigma-6)(4+\sigma)^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\xi_{-4}=

\displaystyle-\frac{12b^{2}(2+\sigma)^{8}a^{2}}{b_{1,1}^{2}\sigma^{4}(4+\sigma)^{4}}.

One now verifies that equation (
A.27
) is satisfied. Expanding the function
A_{2}(x)

(cf. equation (
A.26
))
into a power series in
z
, taking into account the relation between
z
and
x
,
and comparing this with the expansion (
A.14
),
one obtains an explicit formula for the coefficients
b_{2k-1,k-2}
,

b_{2k-1,k-2}=\frac{(-4)^{k}b_{1,1}^{k}}{(2+\sigma)^{2k}}\sum_{p=1}^{4}\binom{k+p-1}{p-1}\xi_{-p},\qquad k=4,5,\ldots,

(A.28)

where
\tbinom{m}{k}=\tfrac{m!}{k!(m-k)!}
is the binomial coefficient, and, the first three off-set coefficients,

b_{2k-1,k-2}=\frac{(-4)^{k}b_{1,1}^{k}}{(2+\sigma)^{2k}}\left((-1)^{k}\xi_{k}+\sum_{p=1}^{4}\binom{k+p-1}{p-1}\xi_{-p}\right),\qquad k=1,2,3.

(A.29)

Substituting
k=1,2,3
into equation (
A.29
), one reproduces
b_{1,-1}
,
b_{3,0}
, and
b_{5,1}

given, respectively, by the third equation in (
A.2
), and equations (
A.5
) and
(
A.6
).

The calculation of the generating functions
A_{0}(x)
,
A_{1}(x)
, and
A_{2}(x)
with the help of
Maple
on
a generic laptop takes but a few seconds; therefore, from the practical point of view, one can continue such
calculations to obtain successive generating functions.
These functions for
n\geqslant 3
satisfy the following inhomogeneous degenerate hypergeometric equation,

\displaystyle\left(\left(D_{x}-n\sigma\right)^{2}+8A_{0}\right)A_{n,0}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k}=n\\
i_{j}\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\left(D_{x}-n\sigma\right)^{2}A_{i_{1},0}A_{i_{2},0}\ldots A_{i_{k},0}

(A.30)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{2abx}{A_{0}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k}=n-1\\
i_{j}\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}A_{i_{1},0}A_{i_{2},0}\ldots A_{i_{k},0}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(\frac{8bx}{A_{0}}\right)^{2}\,\sum_{k=1}^{n-2}(-1)^{k}(k+1)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k}=n-2\\
i_{j}\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}A_{i_{1},0}A_{i_{2},0}\ldots A_{i_{k},0},

where, for any natural
k
,
A_{k,0}:=A_{k}(x)/A_{0}(x)
. Equation (
A.30
) is, in fact, valid starting from

n=1
, provided one starts the summations in the last two sums from
k=0
and agrees to abide by the standard rules
for interpreting the
\sum
-operator in such degenerate situations, e.g.,
\sum^{-1}_{k=0}:=0
. To complete the
definition of
A_{n}(x)
, we have to state that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
A_{n}(x)
is the unique rational solution of
equation (
A.30
) with the first term of its Taylor expansion at
x=0
of the order
x^{N}
, where

N=\lfloor\tfrac{n-1}{2}\rfloor+1
(cf. equation (
A.14
)). The last condition is necessary in order to
remove an ambiguity of the rational solution for some special values of
\sigma
: for generic values of
\sigma
,
this condition is satisfied automatically.

The right-hand side of equation (
A.30
) becomes progressively more complicated for increasing values of
n
.
This fact, however, does not encumber the procedure for finding explicitly the generating functions
A_{n}(x)
:
we have performed calculations for
n=3
and
4
without encountering a visible increase of the time of calculations.
The main problem is the presentation of the results in an observable form: the partial-fraction decomposition
of
A_{n}(x)
helps, to some extent, because, structurally,
A_{n}(x)
in terms of
z
has poles only at
z=-1

and
z=\infty
; however, the number and complexity of the corresponding coefficients is incresing rapidly.
Computationally, the procedure is quite simple for
Maple
to execute, so that, ultimately, it is the
lack of available memory that halts the calculations.

A.3
A Symmetric Power-Like Asymptotic Expansion as
\tau\to 0

As mentioned in Subsection
A.1
, the original
expansion (
A.1
) possesses the symmetry
\sigma\to-\sigma
,
b_{1,1}\leftrightarrow b_{1,-1}
.
In the construction of the super-generating function
A(x,y)
in
Subsection
A.2
, this symmetry is lost because of the “non-symmetric”
definition of
A(x,y)
. We can, however, using the symmetry, define a symmetric reflection of the function
A(x,y)
,
namely, the function
\bar{A}(\bar{x},\bar{y})
, by making in the definitions (
A.12
),
(
A.13
), and (
A.14
) the changes
A_{n}(x)\to\bar{A}_{n}(\bar{x})
for

n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
,
b_{2k-1,m}\to b_{2k-1,-m}
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,

x\to\bar{x}
, and
y\to\bar{y}
. In the subsequent constructions of
Subsection
A.2
, we have to change, additionally,
\sigma\to-\sigma
,
so that, in particular, the operator
D\to\bar{D}
, where

\bar{D}=(2-\sigma)\bar{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{x}}+\sigma\bar{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{y}}.

In the formula expressing
u(\tau)
in terms of
A(x,y)
, we must substitute
x=\tau^{2+\sigma}
and

y=\tau^{-\sigma}
, and, in the corresponding formula in terms of
\bar{A}(\bar{x},\bar{y})
, the conjugated
variables
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
should be replaced by

\bar{x}=\tau^{2-\sigma}
and
\bar{y}=\tau^{\sigma}
, so that this formula reads

u(\tau)=\frac{1}{\tau}\bar{A}(\tau^{2-\sigma},\tau^{\sigma}).

(A.31)

Adding equations (
A.17
) and (
A.31
), we arrive at the symmetized
form for the function
u(\tau)
:

u(\tau)=\frac{1}{2\tau}\big(A(\tau^{2+\sigma},\tau^{-\sigma})+\bar{A}(\tau^{2-\sigma},\tau^{\sigma})\big).

(A.32)

As an application of the ideas considered herein, we obtain the uniform
(with respect to
\sigma
) leading term of the power-like small-
\tau
asymptotics of the function
u(\tau)
.
Using the definition of
A_{0}(x)
(cf. equation (
B.2
)) and its symmetry conjugate

\bar{A}_{0}(\bar{x})
, we can rewrite the expansion (
A.1
) as follows:

u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}\frac{1}{\tau}\big(A_{0}(x)+\bar{A}_{0}(\bar{x})+b_{1,0}\tau^{2}\big)+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{3-|\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma|}\big),\quad x=\tau^{2+\sigma},\;\;\bar{x}=\tau^{2-\sigma}.

(A.33)

Taking into account the explicit expression for
A_{0}(x)
(cf. equation (
A.21
)), and the
corresponding expression for
\bar{A}_{0}(\bar{x})
obtained via the symmetry described above, one finds that

u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}\tau\left(\frac{b_{1,1}\tau^{\sigma}}{\left(1+\frac{4b_{1,1}\tau^{2+\sigma}}{(\sigma+2)^{2}}\right)^{2}}+\frac{b_{1,-1}\tau^{-\sigma}}{\left(1+\frac{4b_{1,-1}\tau^{2-\sigma}}{(\sigma-2)^{2}}\right)^{2}}+\frac{2ab}{\sigma^{2}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\tau^{3-|\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma|}\right).

(A.34)

The asymptotics (
A.34
) is valid for all values of the parameter
\sigma
such that

\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma\in[-2,2]
, excluding the values
\sigma=0,\pm 2
.
34
34

34

As a matter of fact, this formula also works for
\sigma=0,\pm 2
in the sense of the proper limiting procedures

\sigma\to 0
or
\sigma\to\pm 2
. To evaluate the limits, one must use the monodromy parametrizations of
b_{1,\pm 1}
.

For the latter values of
\sigma
, the asymptotics is constructed in terms of logarithmic functions: these cases
are considered in Appendices
B
and
C
below.
Of course, it is assumed that the asymptotics (
A.34
) is valid for those solutions
u(\tau)

corresponding to monodromy data for which the parametrizations of
b_{1,\pm 1}
via these data make
sense (see the definition of this parametrization in the paragraph following equation (
A.11
)).
Clearly, in some domains of
\sigma
, the asymptotics (
A.34
) can be simplified
(some terms can be omitted); in Section
3
, say, we derived asymptotics for
u(\tau)

in terms of the parameter
\varrho
, with
\sigma=4\varrho
: this formula is, in fact, valid in the strip

0<\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma<4
punctured at
\sigma=2
. For
\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma>1
, the first term of the
asymptotics (
A.34
) is smaller than the correction term, and can, therefore, be omitted,
and, if
\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma>2
, the third term of the asymptotics (
A.34
) is smaller
than the correction term, and can also be neglected, thus the leading terms in the asymptotic
formulae (
3.7
) and (
A.34
) coincide, even though they are obtained
by different methods! Note, however, that for
0<\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma<2
, the asymptotic
formula (
A.34
) provides a more accurate approximation for
u(\tau)
than the
asymptotic results presented in Section
3
; furthermore, for
\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma\in(-2,2)
, the
asymptotics (
A.34
) better approximates the function
u(\tau)
than does the
asymptotics (
A.10
) obtained in
[
15
]
(see the discussion of the
error estimation in the paragraph subsequent to equation (
A.11
)).

In light of the discussion above, it is interesting to see how one can go beyond the
leading term of the uniform asymptotics and obtain explicitly at least the first correction term. In this respect,
our strategy is the same as for the derivation of the uniform asymptotic expansion (
A.34
):
rewrite the original asymptotic expansion (
A.1
) with the help of the functions

A_{0}(x)
and
A_{1}(x)
(cf. equations (
A.13
) and (
A.14
) for
n=1
) and their
symmetry conjugates
\bar{A}_{0}(\bar{x})
and
\bar{A}_{1}(\bar{x})
keeping all terms up to the level
k=3
:

u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}\frac{1}{\tau}\big(A_{0}(x)+\bar{A}_{0}(\bar{x})+yA_{1}(x)+\bar{y}\bar{A}_{1}(\bar{x})-b_{1,0}\tau^{2}+b_{3,0}\tau^{4}\big)+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{5-|\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma|}\big),

(A.35)

where we subtracted
b_{1,0}\tau^{2}
, because it is counted twice, once in each of the terms
yA_{1}(x)

and
\bar{y}\bar{A}_{1}(\bar{x})
. Taking note of the leading term of the expansion as presented in
equation (
A.33
), we next rewrite the expansion (
A.35
)
using the relations
yx=\bar{y}\bar{x}=\tau^{2}
:

u(\tau)\underset{\tau\to 0^{+}}{=}\frac{1}{\tau}\big(A_{0}(x)+\bar{A}_{0}(\bar{x})+b_{1,0}\tau^{2}\big)+\tau\big(A_{1}(x)/x+\bar{A}_{1}(\bar{x})/\bar{x}-2b_{1,0}\big)+b_{3,0}\tau^{3}+\mathcal{O}\big(\tau^{5-|\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma|}\big).

(A.36)

Using equation (
A.23
), where
z
is defined in equation (
A.21
), and
recalling that
b_{1,0}=2ab/\sigma^{2}
(cf. equation (
A.2
)), one finds that

\frac{A_{1}(x)}{x}-b_{1,0}=-\frac{4abz(\sigma+2)}{\sigma^{2}(\sigma+4)^{2}(z+1)}\left(\frac{2(\sigma+2)^{2}}{(z+1)^{2}}-\frac{\sigma^{2}-4}{z+1}+4\right),

(A.37)

and its conjugate reads

\frac{\bar{A}_{1}(\bar{x})}{\bar{x}}-b_{1,0}=\frac{4ab\bar{z}(\sigma-2)}{\sigma^{2}(\sigma-4)^{2}(\bar{z}+1)}\left(\frac{2(\sigma-2)^{2}}{(\bar{z}+1)^{2}}-\frac{\sigma^{2}-4}{\bar{z}+1}+4\right),\qquad\bar{z}=\frac{4b_{1,-1}\bar{x}}{(\sigma-2)^{2}}.

(A.38)

Remark A.1
.

The asymptotic formula (
A.36
) allows us to justify the error estimate for the asymptotics
obtained in Section
3
(cf. equations (
3.7
) and (
3.13
)).
If
\mathrm{Re}\,\varrho\in(0,1/2)
, these asymptotic formulae correspond to the parameter

\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma\in(0,2)
; then, referring
to the asymptotic expansion (
A.1
) and comparing these expansions, one finds that the term

\mathcal{O}(\tau)
as
\tau\to 0
is absent in the expansions (
3.7
) and (
3.13
).
The leading term of asymptotics in Section
3
is of the order
\tau^{1-4\varrho}
; denoting
the correction term as
\mathcal{O}(\tau^{\delta})
, we get the condition

1-4\mathrm{Re}\,\varrho+\delta=1
, so that
\delta=4\mathrm{Re}\,\varrho
.

If
\mathrm{Re}\,\varrho\in[1/2,1)
, then
\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma\in[2,4)
, so that, as
\tau\to 0
,

\bar{z}=\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2-\sigma})\to\infty
and
z=\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2+\sigma})\to 0
.
Then, from the asymptotic expansion (
A.36
), we see again that, in the
expansions (
3.7
) and (
3.13
), the
\mathcal{O}(\tau)
term is omitted. The order of
the leading term in the asymptotics (
3.7
) and (
3.13
) is
\tau^{-3+4\varrho}
;
denoting the correction term as
\mathcal{O}(\tau^{\delta})
, we arrive at the condition

-3+4\mathrm{Re}\,\varrho+\delta=1
, so that
\delta=4(1-\mathrm{Re}\,\varrho)
.

\blacksquare

Appendix B
Appendix. The Complete Small-
\tau
Regular Logarithmic

Asymptotic Expansion of
u(\tau)
:
Theorem
5.1

B.1
Error Correction Term of the Isomonodromy Logarithmic Asymptotics As
\tau\to 0

The generic logarithmic asymptotic expansion as
\tau\to 0
can be written as
35
35

35

This expansion can be obtained by considering a formal
\sigma\to 0
limit of the general power-like asymptotic
expansion (
A.1
).

u(\tau)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\tau^{2k-1}\sum_{m=0}^{2k}c_{2k-1,m}(\ln\tau)^{m}.

(B.1)

This expansion is convergent in a neighborhood of
\tau=0
.
We assume that
|\arg\,\tau|<\pi
and the principal branch of
\ln
-function is taken. In this subsection, we study
the expansion (
B.1
) under the assumption that
a\neq 0
. This expansion is also applicable
for
a=\mathrm{i}k
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
; however, for
a=\mathrm{i}k\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{Z}
, there are special variants of
the expansion (
B.1
) which we will discuss in a follow-up paper.

Substituting the expansion (
B.1
) into equation (
1.1
), one finds:

c_{1,2}=-ab,\qquad c_{1,1}=-abc,\qquad c_{1,0}=-\frac{b(a^{2}c^{2}+1)}{4a},

(B.2)

where
c\in\mathbb{C}
is a parameter. We call the coefficients
c_{2k-1,m}
,
m=0,1\ldots,2k
, the

coefficients of level
k
. The coefficients of level
k=1
are given by equations (
B.2
).
Below, we present the coefficients of levels
k=2
and
3
:

\displaystyle c_{3,4}=-2a^{2}b^{2},\quad c_{3,3}=-4a^{2}b^{2}(c-1),\quad c_{3,2}=-b^{2}(3a^{2}c^{2}-6a^{2}c+4a^{2}+1),

\displaystyle c_{3,1}=-b^{2}(c-1)(a^{2}c^{2}-2a^{2}c+2a^{2}+1),

\displaystyle c_{3,0}=-\frac{b^{2}}{8a^{2}}(a^{4}c^{4}-4a^{4}c^{3}+2a^{2}(4a^{2}+1)c^{2}-4a^{2}(2a^{2}+1)c+1),

\displaystyle c_{5,6}=-3a^{3}b^{3},\quad c_{5,5}=-3a^{3}b^{3}(3c-4),\quad c_{5,4}=-\frac{ab^{3}}{8}(90a^{2}c^{2}-240a^{2}c+185a^{2}+18),

\displaystyle c_{5,3}=-\frac{ab^{3}}{8}(60a^{2}c^{3}-240a^{2}c^{2}+(370a^{2}+36)c-209a^{2}-48),

\displaystyle c_{5,2}=-\frac{3b^{3}}{16a}(15a^{4}c^{4}-80a^{4}c^{3}+(185a^{4}+18a^{2})c^{2}-(209a^{4}+48a^{2})c+91a^{4}+35a^{2}+3),

\displaystyle c_{5,1}=-\frac{b^{3}}{64a}(36a^{4}c^{5}-240a^{4}c^{4}+(740a^{4}+72a^{2})c^{3}-(1254a^{4}+288a^{2})c^{2}

\displaystyle+(1092a^{4}+420a^{2}+36)c-401a^{4}-258a^{2}-48),

\displaystyle c_{5,0}=-\frac{b^{3}}{128a^{3}}(6a^{6}c^{6}-48a^{6}c^{5}+(185a^{6}+18a^{4})c^{4}-(418a^{6}+96a^{4})c^{3}

\displaystyle+(546a^{6}+210a^{4}+18a^{2})c^{2}-(401a^{6}+258a^{4}+48a^{2})c+128a^{6}+128a^{4}+25a^{2}+6)

Although the number of parameters on which the coefficients
c_{2k-1,m}

depend is one less than the number of parameters in the coefficients
b_{2k-1,m}
studied in
Appendix
A
(
\sigma
is absent), they look even more
complicated.
36
36

36

We measured the amount of time that was required in order to compute the coefficients
c_{2k-1,m}
for the
first 9 levels, as done for the coefficients
b_{2k-1,m}
(see footnote
32
), and
the results were quite surprising! On the old notebook,
Maple
15 showed the quickest computation time was
about 105 seconds, while on the new notebook,
Maple
2017 executed the same calculation in approximately
470 seconds! We also ran the same calculation on the new notebook using
Maple
2022, and its fastest
computation time was roughly 137 seconds, whilst for the calculation discussed in
footnote
32
,
Maple
2022 executed it nearly 15 seconds slower than

Maple
2017! The only thing we can confirm is the fact that the length of the
Maple
output for
the coefficients
b_{17,0}
and
c_{17,0}
, when compared side-by-side, look very similar; both coefficients require
about 36 lines to display.

As a result of the computation of the coefficients
c_{2k-1,m}
, one can formulate the following conjecture.

Conjecture B.1
.

c_{2k-1,m}=b^{k}P_{k}(a,c)a^{2\lfloor\frac{m+1}{2}\rfloor-k},

where
P_{k}(a,c)
is a polynomial in the two variables
a
and
c
such that

P_{k}(0,c)
is a non-vanishing polynomial in
c
,
\deg_{c}P_{k}(a,c)=2k-m
, and

\deg_{a}P_{k}(a,c)=2k-2\lfloor\frac{m+1}{2}\rfloor
.

Of course, in order to justify the existence of the expansion (
B.1
), one has to derive
and study the recursion relation for the coefficients; this relation establishes the existence of the
expansion (
B.1
) for all
a\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
and

c\in\mathbb{C}
.
37
37

37

The logarithmic asymptotics as
\tau\to 0
for
a=0
are obtained
in
[
15
]
and simplified in
[
19
]
. The full asymptotic expansion, together with the
corresponding super-generating function, will appear in a follow-up paper.

In Appendix
C.1
below, we show how one can corroborate
a similar statement for the other logarithmic expansion (
C.1
) without having to write
an explicit formula for the recursion relation; however, the proof of Conjecture
B.1
may turn out to
be more complicated despite the fact that the recursion relation would be presented explicitly.

Remark B.1
.

We now verify the error-correction term for the asymptotics of the functions

u(\tau)
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
given in Theorem
5.1
. Note that the error-correction
term originally obtained for these asymptotics as
\tau\to 0
was
\mathcal{O}(\tau^{\delta})

[
15
,
19
]
. The expansion (
B.1
) allows one to obtain a more precise
estimate for the error presented in Theorem
5.1
.
Consider the solution
u(\tau)
defined by the expansion (
B.1
) with the same parameter
c

(cf. equation (
5.3
)) as in the asymptotics for
u(\tau)
given in Theorem
5.1
;
both asymptotics, therefore, have the same leading-order behaviour.
The function
u(\tau)
defined by the asymptotic expansion (
B.1
) is a solution of
equation (
1.1
) and thus corresponds to some point on the monodromy manifold: the solution
u(\tau)
in
Theorem
5.1
corresponds to the same point since the remaining points
on the monodromy manifold correspond to solutions with different asymptotic behaviours, as follows from the
results of this paper. The coincidence of the solutions implies that the correction term in the
asymptotics (
5.4
) can be determined by referring to expansion (
B.1
).
The error estimate in the asymptotics (
5.6
) for
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(\tau)}
is obtained by
integrating equation (
2.1
).

\blacksquare

B.2
Super-Generating Function for the Regular Logarithmic Asymptotics

The formal construction of the super-generating function for the coefficients of the
expansion (
B.1
) is similar to the one presented in
Subsection
A.2
, namely,

\hat{A}(x,y)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}y^{n}\hat{A}_{n}(x),

(B.3)

where the coefficient functions
\hat{A}_{n}(x)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, are generating functions for
the coefficients
c_{2k-1,2k-n}
:

\displaystyle\hat{A}_{0}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_{2k-1,2k}x^{k},

(B.4)

\displaystyle\hat{A}_{n}(x)=\sum_{k=\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor+1}^{\infty}c_{2k-1,2k-n}x^{k},\qquad n\geqslant 1.

(B.5)

Define the linear differential operator
\hat{D}
acting in the space of formal power
series of two variables
x
and
y
as follows:

\hat{D}:=2x(1+y)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}-y^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial y};

(B.6)

then, the function
\hat{A}\equiv\hat{A}(x,y)
solves the PDE

\hat{D}^{2}(\ln\hat{A})=-8\hat{A}+2a\frac{bxy^{2}}{\hat{A}}+\left(\frac{bxy^{2}}{\hat{A}}\right)^{2}.

(B.7)

Assuming that a proper solution of the PDE (
B.7
) is constructed, one can obtain the solution of
equation (
1.1
) via the relation

u(\tau)=\frac{1}{\tau}\hat{A}\big(\tau^{2}\ln^{2}\tau,(\ln\tau)^{-1}\big).

(B.8)

Now, in a manner similar to the one taken for the functions
A_{n}(x)
in
Subsection
A.2
, we show that this construction leads to explicit formulae
for the functions
\hat{A}_{n}(x)
.

For
n=0
, we get exactly the same equation (cf. equation (
A.18
)) for
\hat{A}_{0}(x)
that we got for
the function
A_{0}(x)
, but with
\sigma=0
,

\hat{D}_{x}^{2}\ln\hat{A}_{0}(x)=-8\hat{A}_{0}(x),

(B.9)

where

\hat{D}_{x}:=2x\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}

(B.10)

is the
x
-part of the operator
\hat{D}
. The analysis of this equation is even simpler
than the one for equation (
A.18
), where, for equation (
B.9
), we have to choose the rational
solution

\hat{A}_{0}(x)=-\frac{Cx}{(1-Cx)^{2}},

(B.11)

where
C
is the constant of integration. To determine
C
, one expands the right-hand side of
equation (
B.11
) into a Taylor series centred at
x=0
,

\hat{A}_{0}(x)=-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k(Cx)^{k},

and compares it with the series (
B.4
); then,

C=-c_{1,2}=ab,

(B.12)

where, for the latter equation, we used the first relation in (
B.2
); thus, we’ve calculated all
the coefficients

c_{2k-1,2k}=-k(ab)^{k},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

To calculate the next generating function
\hat{A}_{1}(x)
, substitute the expansion (
B.3
)
into equation (
B.7
) and equate the terms that are linear in
y
on both sides of the resulting
equation,

\hat{D}_{x}^{2}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{1}(x)}{\hat{A}_{0}(x)}\right)+8\hat{A}_{1}(x)+2\hat{D}_{x}^{2}\ln\hat{A}_{0}(x)=0,

(B.13)

where
\hat{A}_{0}(x)
is given in equation (
B.11
); thus, equation (
B.13
) is the
inhomogeneous degenerate hypergeometric equation with general solution

\hat{A}_{1}(x)=-\frac{x(1+Cx)C_{1}}{(1-Cx)^{3}}-\frac{x(4+\ln(x)+Cx\ln(x))C_{0}}{(1-Cx)^{3}}-\frac{4Cx}{(1-Cx)^{3}},

where
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
are constants of integration. One sets
C_{0}=0
because the expansion (
B.5
)
for
n=1
does not contain any logarithmic terms; therefore,

\hat{A}_{1}(x)=-\frac{x(1+Cx)C_{1}}{(1-Cx)^{3}}-\frac{4Cx}{(1-Cx)^{3}}\underset{x\to 0}{=}-x(C_{1}+4C)+\mathcal{O}\big(x^{2}\big).

Comparing the latter expansion with the definition of
\hat{A}_{1}(x)
(cf. equation (
B.5
) for

n=1
and the second equation in (
B.2
)), we find
-C_{1}-4C=c_{1,1}=-abc=-Cc
, so that
C_{1}=C(c-4)
.
It is convenient to decompose
\hat{A}_{1}(x)
into partial fractions:

\hat{A}_{1}(x)=-\frac{c-4}{1-Cx}+\frac{3c-8}{(1-Cx)^{2}}-\frac{2(c-2)}{(1-Cx)^{3}}.

(B.14)

Expanding each fraction in equation (
B.14
) into Taylor series centred at
x=0
,
one finds that

\hat{A}_{1}(x)=-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k(ck-2(k-1))(Cx)^{k}.

(B.15)

Comparing, now, the expansions (
B.5
) and (
B.15
), and taking into account
(
B.12
), we get

c_{2k-1,2k-1}=-k(k(c-2)+2)(ab)^{k},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

We present below, without detailed explanations, the construction for the functions
\hat{A}_{2}(x)
and
\hat{A}_{3}(x)
.
The inhomogeneous degenerate hypergeometric equation for the function
\hat{A}_{2}(x)
is

\begin{gathered}\hat{D}_{x}^{2}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{2}(x)}{\hat{A}_{0}(x)}\right)+8\hat{A}_{2}(x)-\frac{2abx}{\hat{A}_{0}(x)}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{D}_{x}^{2}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{1}(x)}{\hat{A}_{0}(x)}\right)^{2}+2\hat{D}_{x}^{2}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{1}(x)}{\hat{A}_{0}(x)}\right)-2\hat{D}_{x}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{1}(x)}{\hat{A}_{0}(x)}\right)\\
+\hat{D}_{x}^{2}\ln\hat{A}_{0}(x)-\hat{D}_{x}\ln\hat{A}_{0}(x)=0.\end{gathered}

(B.16)

Remark B.2
.

A rational solution of equation (
B.16
) exists if and only if the parameter
C
in
\hat{A}_{0}(x)
is
chosen as per equation (
B.12
); therefore, it was not necessary to calculate
c_{1,2}
in order to determine
the value of the parameter
C
, and the possibility of the continuation of our construction dictates the correct
value of
C
. Henceforth, we assume that
C=ab
.

\blacksquare

With reference to Remark
B.2
, one finds the one-parameter (
C_{2}
) rational solution of
equation (
B.16
) which, when decomposed into partial fractions, reads

\begin{gathered}\hat{A}_{2}(x)=-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{abx}{8}-\frac{4C_{2}-11}{4(1-abx)}+\frac{12C_{2}-8c^{2}+24c-35}{4(1-abx)^{2}}\\
-\frac{4C_{2}-10c^{2}+36c-37}{2(1-abx)^{3}}-\frac{3(c-2)^{2}}{(1-abx)^{4}}.\end{gathered}

(B.17)

Expand
\hat{A}_{2}(x)
into a Taylor series centred at
x=0
:

\hat{A}_{2}(x)=-\frac{ab}{8}(8C_{2}+8c^{2}-48c+57)x+\mathcal{O}\big(x^{2}\big).

(B.18)

Comparing the leading coefficient of the expansion (
B.18
) with the analogous one in
the expansion (
B.5
), one finds

-\frac{ab}{8}(8C_{2}+8c^{2}-48c+57)=c_{1,0}=-\frac{b(a^{2}c^{2}+1)}{4a};

(B.19)

thus, solving equation (
B.19
) for
C_{2}
, we obtain

C_{2}=-\frac{1}{8a^{2}}(6a^{2}c^{2}-48a^{2}c+57a^{2}-2).

(B.20)

Now, using the partial fraction expansion (
B.17
) for the function
\hat{A}_{2}(x)
,
we find, after a straightforward calculation, that, for
k\geqslant 2
,

c_{2k-1,2k-2}=-\frac{(ab)^{k}}{8}\left(2k^{2}(2k-1)(c-2)^{2}+8k(2k-1)(c-2)+(k+2)(5k-2)+\frac{2k^{2}}{a^{2}}\right).

(B.21)

Remark B.3
.

The initial coefficient
c_{1,0}
has the off-set value (cf. equation (
B.2
)); however, its value
allows us to determine the remaining “regular” coefficients of the series (
B.21
). It seems
that there should be some other idea that would allow one to fix the coefficients without having to appeal to

a priori
calculated coefficients for small values of
k
.
This idea has already been demonstrated in Remark
B.2
, where it was explained that the
determination of the value of
C
is the crucial issue for the existence of the
expansion (
B.3
) with rational coefficient functions. To check whether or not this idea
works, we continue with the calculation of the function
\hat{A}_{3}(x)
, but without reference to the particular value
of
C_{2}
obtained above (cf. equation (
B.20
)).

\blacksquare

To simplify the notation in some of the formulae below, we write
\hat{A}_{k}(x)=\hat{A}_{k}
for
k=0,1,2,3,4
.
With this notation, the equation for the determination of the function
\hat{A}_{3}(x)
reads:

\begin{gathered}\hat{D}_{x}^{2}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{3}}{\hat{A}_{0}}\right)+8\hat{A}_{3}+2\frac{\hat{A}_{1}}{\hat{A}_{0}}+2abx\frac{\hat{A}_{1}}{\hat{A}_{0}^{2}}+\hat{D}_{x}^{2}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{1}}{\hat{A}_{0}}\right)-3\hat{D}_{x}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{1}}{\hat{A}_{0}}\right)\\
+\hat{D}_{x}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{1}}{\hat{A}_{0}}\right)^{3}-\frac{\hat{A}_{1}\hat{A}_{2}}{\hat{A}_{0}^{2}}\right)+2\hat{D}_{x}^{2}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{2}}{\hat{A}_{0}}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{1}}{\hat{A}_{0}}\right)^{2}\right)-4\hat{D}_{x}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{2}}{\hat{A}_{0}}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\hat{A}_{1}}{\hat{A}_{0}}\right)^{2}\right)=0.\end{gathered}

(B.22)

Equation (
B.22
) has, for any value of the parameter
C_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
, a rational solution that
depends on
a parameter
C_{3}
. The partial fraction decomposition of
\hat{A}_{3}(x)
is

\begin{gathered}\hat{A}_{3}(x)=-\frac{abx}{8}+\frac{c}{2}-1-\frac{4c^{3}-24c^{2}+48c-32}{(1-abx)^{5}}\\
+\frac{14c^{3}-60c^{2}+87c-46-12cC_{2}+24C_{2}}{2(1-abx)^{4}}-\frac{6c^{3}-21c+9+4C_{3}-20cC_{2}+32C_{2}}{2(1-abx)^{3}}\\
+\frac{24c^{2}-16c-29+12C_{3}-16cC_{2}+16C_{2}}{4(1-abx)^{2}}-\frac{10c-15+4C_{3}}{4(1-abx)}.\end{gathered}

(B.23)

Consider the first terms of the Taylor expansion at
x=0
of the function
\hat{A}_{3}(x)
,

\displaystyle\hat{A}_{3}(x)=

\displaystyle-\frac{ab}{8}(8C_{3}+8c^{3}-96c^{2}+360c-349+16cC_{2}-64C_{2})x

(B.24)

\displaystyle-\frac{(ab)^{2}}{2}(8C_{3}+16c^{3}-156c^{2}+473c-410+24cC_{2}-72C_{2})x^{2}+\mathcal{O}\big(x^{3}\big),

and compare it with its definition (
B.5
) for
n=3
; the term proportional to
x

vanishes, which allows us to get the constant
C_{3}
in terms of
C_{2}
:

C_{3}=(8-2c)C_{2}+349/8-45c+12c^{2}-c^{3}.

(B.25)

At this stage of the calculation, we continue to assume that
C_{2}
has not been determined. We carry forward with our
construction and try to get a rational function
\hat{A}_{4}(x)
which solves equation (
B.31
)
for
n=4
(see below). In case one uses either
Maple
or
Mathematica
, finding a general solution
to this equation is not problematic, and the solution for
n=4
is obtained almost immediately; however, we see
that the rational solution of this equation exists iff the constant of integration
C_{2}
is fixed according to
equation (
B.20
), in which case, the final form of the function
\hat{A}_{3}(x)
reads

\begin{gathered}\hat{A}_{3}(x)=-\frac{abx}{8}+\frac{c}{2}-1-\frac{4(c-2)^{3}}{(1-abx)^{5}}+\frac{(c-2)\big((46c^{2}-208c+217)a^{2}-6\big)}{4a^{2}(1-abx)^{4}}\\
-\frac{(46c^{3}-336c^{2}+765c-545)a^{2}-14c+32}{4a^{2}(1-abx)^{3}}+\frac{(36c^{3}-312c^{2}+802c-607)a^{2}-20c+56}{8a^{2}(1-abx)^{2}}\\
-\frac{(4c^{3}-48c^{2}+158c-137)a^{2}-4c+16}{8a^{2}(1-abx)}.\end{gathered}

(B.26)

Using the representation (
B.26
) for the function
\hat{A}_{3}(x)
, we obtain the general formula
for the coefficients
c_{2k-1,2k-3}
:

\begin{gathered}c_{2k-1,2k-3}=-\frac{(ab)^{k}}{2}\left(\frac{k^{2}(2k-1)(k-1)}{6}(c-2)^{3}+k(2k-1)(k-1)(c-2)^{2}\right.\\
\left.+(k-1)\left(\frac{5k^{2}}{4}+2k-1+\frac{k^{2}}{2a^{2}}\right)(c-2)+k\left(\frac{k-1}{a^{2}}+\frac{9k-10}{4}\right)\right),\qquad k\geqslant 2.\end{gathered}

(B.27)

As stated above, only if the constant of integration
C_{2}
in the rational function
\hat{A}_{3}(x)
is chosen as per
equation (
B.20
) can one find a rational solution of equation (
B.31
) for
n=4
:

\begin{gathered}\hat{A}_{4}(x)=-\left(1+\frac{4}{a^{2}}\right)\frac{(abx)^{3}}{256}+\left(17+\frac{44}{a^{2}}\right)\frac{(abx)^{2}}{576}+\left(144c-469-\frac{388}{a^{2}}\right)\frac{abx}{2304}\\
-\frac{3}{16}\left(2c^{2}-8c+3-\frac{2}{a^{2}}\right)-\frac{5(c-2)^{4}}{(1-abx)^{6}}+\frac{(c-2)^{2}\big((36c^{2}-160c+161)a^{2}-6\big)}{2a^{2}(1-abx)^{5}}\\
-\frac{(1564c^{4}-14176c^{3}+46212c^{2}-64352c+32315)a^{4}-12(50c^{2}-216c+227)a^{2}+12}{64a^{4}(1-abx)^{4}}\\
+\frac{\big(72(243c^{4}-2432c^{3}+8489c^{2}-12238c)+441091\big)a^{4}-4(2988c^{2}-14400c+16265)a^{2}+504}{1152a^{4}(1-abx)^{3}}\\
-\frac{\big(48(65c^{4}-760c^{3}+2965c^{2}-4523c)+107041\big)a^{4}-4(888c^{2}-5088c+6491)a^{2}+240}{768a^{4}(1-abx)^{2}}\\
+\frac{(720c^{4}-11520c^{3}+56880c^{2}-98640c+46009)a^{4}-4(360c^{2}-2880c+4763)a^{2}+144}{2304a^{4}(1-abx)}.\end{gathered}

(B.28)

Developing, with the help of equation (
B.28
), the function
\hat{A}_{4}(x)
into a Taylor
series about
x=0
and comparing the resulting expansion with the definition of the function
\hat{A}_{n}(x)

for
n=4
(cf. equation (
B.5
)), one finds, for
k=2
and
k=3
, two off-set coefficients,

\begin{gathered}c_{3,0}=-\frac{(ab)^{2}}{8}\left(\frac{1}{a^{4}}+\frac{2c(c-2)}{a^{2}}+c(c-2)(c^{2}-2c+4)\right),\\
c_{5,2}=-\frac{3(ab)^{3}}{16}\left(\frac{3}{a^{4}}+\frac{18c^{2}-48c+35}{a^{2}}+15c^{4}-80c^{3}+185c^{2}-209c+91\right),\end{gathered}

(B.29)

and the general formula

\begin{gathered}c_{2k-1,2k-4}=-\frac{(abx)^{k}}{96}\bigg(k^{2}(k-1)(2k-1)(2k-3)(c-2)^{4}+8k(k-1)(2k-1)(2k-3)(c-2)^{3}\\
+3(k-1)(2k-3)\left(5k^{2}+8k-4+\frac{2k^{2}}{a^{2}}\right)(c-2)^{2}+6k(2k-3)\left(\frac{4(k-1)}{a^{2}}+9k-10\right)(c-2)\\
+3\left(\frac{5}{2}+\frac{1}{a^{2}}\right)^{2}k^{3}-\left(\frac{3}{a^{4}}+\frac{43}{6a^{2}}+\frac{55}{24}\right)k^{2}-6\left(19+\frac{10}{a^{2}}\right)k+9\left(10+\frac{4}{a^{2}}\right)\!\!\bigg),\quad k=4,5,6,\dotsc.\end{gathered}

(B.30)

In general, for
n=0,1,2,\ldots
, the functions
\hat{A}_{n}(x)\equiv\hat{A}_{n}
are defined as rational solutions of
the following degenerate hypergeometric equation,

\begin{gathered}\hat{D}_{x}^{2}(f_{n}+2f_{n-1}+f_{n-2})-\hat{D}_{x}\big(2(n-1)f_{n-1}+(2n-3)f_{n-2}\big)+(n-1)(n-2)f_{n-2}+8\hat{A}_{n}\\
=\frac{2abx}{\hat{A}_{0}}\sum_{l=0}^{n-2}(-1)^{l}\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+\ldots+p_{l}=n-2\\
p_{i}\geqslant 1,\;i=1,\ldots,l\end{subarray}}\hat{A}_{p_{1}0}\cdot\ldots\cdot\hat{A}_{p_{l}0}+\frac{b^{2}x^{2}}{\hat{A}_{0}^{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{n-4}(-1)^{l}(l+1)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+\ldots+p_{l}=n-4\\
p_{i}\geqslant 1,i=1,\ldots,l\end{subarray}}\tilde{A}_{p_{1}0}\cdot\ldots\cdot\tilde{A}_{p_{l}0},\end{gathered}

(B.31)

whose Taylor-series expansions start with the term
x^{\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor+1}
, and where,
in equation (
B.31
), we adopted the notations

\hat{A}_{p0}:=\frac{\hat{A}_{p}}{\hat{A}_{0}},\quad p\in\mathbb{N},\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad f_{n}=\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{l-1}}{l}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+\ldots+p_{l}=n\\
p_{i}\geqslant 1,\;i=1,\ldots,l\end{subarray}}\hat{A}_{p_{1}0}\cdot\ldots\cdot\hat{A}_{p_{l}0}.

(B.32)

Since, for
n>4
, the value of the inner sums in both of the double sums vanish for
l=0
, it follows that the
summation in both of the double sums actually begins at
l=1
. For
n=0,1,2,3,4
, equation (
B.31
)
remains valid. In this case, though, one has to assume that
f_{-2}=f_{-1}=0
and
f_{0}=\ln\hat{A}_{0}
; moreover,
the following—natural—conventions regarding the summation in the double sums are made: (i) if the upper limit
of the external sum of a double sum in equation (
B.31
) is negative, then the double sum equals
zero; (ii) if the upper limit of the external sum is zero, then the corresponding double sum equals
1
; and (iii)
if the upper limit of the external sum is positive, then the value of the inner sum corresponding to
l=0
vanishes,
so that the summation starts at
l=1
.

Remark B.4
.

The explicit formula for the function
\hat{A}_{4}(x)
(cf. equation (
B.28
)) is rather
cumbersome, so that the question arises as to whether or not the procedure can effectively be continued to construct
the functions
\hat{A}_{n}(x)
for larger values of
n
, especially when the growth in size with respect to
n

of the inhomogeneous part of the ODE (
B.31
) is taken into account. Inherent in such calculations is
the substantial problem of having to store into the memory of the program all of the previously obtained results,
that is, the functions
\hat{A}_{p}(x)
for
p<n
. We calculated
\hat{A}_{n}(x)
for
n=5
and
6
: the calculation of
each function, modulo the previously obtained functions, was completed within 2 seconds. We assume that such
straightforward calculations on a standard, modern laptop can be performed in a reasonable time frame (a few hours),
at least for values of
n
up to
15
.

\blacksquare

Remark B.5
.

The partial fraction decomposition of the generating function
\hat{A}_{n}(x)
consists of fractions with
denominators of the form
(1-abx)^{k}
with
k\leqslant n+2
; this leads to the fact that, for generic values of
the parameters, the coefficients
c_{2k-1,2k-n}
are polynomials in
k
of degree
n+1
.
It is easy to see that the residues of the partial fractions beginning with the fraction with highest order

k=n+2
in the denominator are successively proportional to
(c-2)^{n}
,
(c-2)^{n-2}
, etc., up to powers
of
(c-2)
that remain positive.
For
c=2
, therefore, the partial fractions corresponding to the first
\lfloor\tfrac{n+1}{2}\rfloor
members of the
pole expansion of
\hat{A}_{n}(x)
vanish. Thus, for
c=2
, the order of the pole of
\hat{A}_{n}(x)

is
n+2-\lfloor\tfrac{n+1}{2}\rfloor
, which means that the coefficients
c_{2k-1,2k-n}
are polynomials in
k

of
\deg_{k}c_{2k-1,2k-n}=n+1-\lfloor\tfrac{n+1}{2}\rfloor
.

An interesting observation is the fact that
c
depends on the scaling parameter
b

(cf. equation (
5.3
)), which means that, for any solution having regular logarithmic behaviour as

\tau\to 0
, one can choose a proper scaling for which the growth of the coefficients of the
corresponding asymptotic expansion achieves its minimal possible value.

\blacksquare

Appendix C
Appendix. The Complete Small-
\tau
Irregular Logarithmic

Asymptotic Expansion of
u(\tau)
:
Theorems
5.2
and
5.2
′

C.1
Asymptotic Expansion and Properties of its Coefficients

Bearing in mind the application of Bäcklund transformations to the expansion (
B.1
),
the complete logarithmic asymptotic expansion related with the leading term of asymptotics given in
Theorems
5.2
and
5.2
′
is

u(\tau)=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\tau^{2k-1}\sum_{m=-2\lfloor k/2\rfloor}^{+\infty}\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}(\ln\tau)^{-m},\qquad\tilde{c}_{-1,0}=\tilde{c}_{-1,1}=0,\quad\tilde{c}_{-1,2}=-\frac{1}{4}.

(C.1)

The expansion (
C.1
) depends on the single parameter
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\in\mathbb{C}
and
is convergent in a neighbourhood of
\tau=0
; furthermore, as in the expansion (
A.1
),
we assume that
|\arg\,\tau|<\pi
and the principle branch of
\ln
-function is chosen.
The expansion (
C.1
) is valid for all values of the parameter of formal monodromy

a\in\mathbb{C}
, although for
a=\mathrm{i}k
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, there are some simplifications that we
address in an upcoming paper.
We say that the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}
are the
coefficients of level
k
. In contrast to the cases
considered in Appendices
A
and
B
, for the present case, the
number of coefficients that belong to each level in the generic situation is infinite; there is, however, one
interesting special case,
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}=0
, for which all the levels are finite.

Below, we present explicit expressions for the coefficients of the first few levels that were calculated by
directly substituting the expansion (
C.1
) into equation (
1.1
) with

\varepsilon=1
:
31

\boldsymbol{\text{Level}\;k=0:}\quad\tilde{c}_{-1,m}=(-1)^{m-1}2^{m-4}(m-1)\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{m-2},\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}.

(C.2)

Numerically, we obtained only the first few members of this sequence: a generic formula can be verified with the
help of the generating function studied in the next subsection.

\begin{gathered}\boldsymbol{\text{Level}\;k=1:}\quad\tilde{c}_{1,0}=\frac{ab}{2},\quad\tilde{c}_{1,1}=-ab,\quad\tilde{c}_{1,2}=ab\,(2\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+1),\\
\tilde{c}_{1,3}=-\frac{ab}{2}(8\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}+8\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+1),\quad\tilde{c}_{1,4}=ab\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}(8\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}+12\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+3),\\
\tilde{c}_{1,5}=-4ab\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}(2\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+3)(2\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+1),\quad\tilde{c}_{1,6}=8ab\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}(4\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}+10\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+5),\\
\tilde{c}_{1,7}=-8ab\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{4}(8\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}+24\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+15),\quad\tilde{c}_{1,8}=16ab\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{5}(8\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}+28\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+21),\\
\tilde{c}_{1,9}=-128ab\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{6}(2\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}+8\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+7),\quad\tilde{c}_{1,10}=256ab\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{7}(\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+3)(2\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+3);\end{gathered}

(C.3)

\begin{gathered}\boldsymbol{\text{Level}\;k=2:}\quad\tilde{c}_{3,-2}=-\frac{b^{2}(a^{2}+1)}{4},\quad\tilde{c}_{3,-1}=-b^{2}\big((a^{2}+1)\tilde{c}_{-1,3}-a^{2}-1/2\big),\\
\tilde{c}_{3,0}=-b^{2}\Big((a^{2}+1)\big(\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}-2\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+71/32\big)+\tilde{c}_{-1,3}-13/8\Big),\quad\tilde{c}_{3,1}=\frac{3b^{2}(37a^{2}+5)}{32},\\
\tilde{c}_{3,2}=-\frac{b^{2}}{64}(444a^{2}\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+239a^{2}+60\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+15),\\
\tilde{c}_{3,3}=\frac{b^{2}}{256}(3552a^{2}\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}+3824a^{2}\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+623a^{2}+480\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}+240\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+15),\\
\tilde{c}_{3,4}=-\frac{3b^{2}}{128}\big((1184\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}+1912\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}+623\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+32)a^{2}+160\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}+120\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}+15\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\big),\\
\tilde{c}_{3,5}=\frac{b^{2}\tilde{c}_{-1,3}}{32}\big((1776\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+3824\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+1869\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+192)a^{2}+240\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+240\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+45\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\big),\\
\tilde{c}_{3,6}=-\frac{b^{2}\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}}{16}\big((1776\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+4780\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+3115\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+480)a^{2}\!+240\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+300\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+75\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\big),\\
\tilde{c}_{3,7}=\frac{3b^{2}\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}}{16}\big((1184\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+3824\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+3115\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+640)a^{2}\!+160\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+240\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+75\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\big),\\
\tilde{c}_{3,8}=-\frac{b^{2}\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{4}}{8}\big((3552\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+13384\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+13083\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+3360)a^{2}\\
+480\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+840\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+315\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\big);\end{gathered}

(C.4)

\begin{gathered}\boldsymbol{\text{Level}\;k=3:}\quad\tilde{c}_{5,-2}=\frac{ab^{3}(a^{2}+1)}{4},\quad\tilde{c}_{5,-1}=ab^{3}\big((a^{2}+1)\tilde{c}_{-1,3}-13a^{2}/8-9/8\big),\\
\tilde{c}_{5,0}=\frac{ab^{3}}{36}\big((36\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!-117\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+176)a^{2}\!+36\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}-81\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+83\big),\\
\end{gathered}

(C.5)

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{5,1}=-\frac{ab^{3}}{864}(7685a^{2}+2309),\quad\tilde{c}_{5,2}=\frac{ab^{3}}{10368}\big((184440\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+111659)a^{2}\!+55416\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+20171\big),

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{5,3}=-\frac{ab^{3}}{7776}\big((276660\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+334977\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+67630)a^{2}\!+83124\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+60513\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+6622\big),

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{5,4}=\frac{ab^{3}}{20736}\big((1475520\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+2679816\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+1082080\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+89181)a^{2}\!

\displaystyle+443328\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+484104\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+105952\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+3645\big),

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{5,5}=-\frac{ab^{3}}{2592}\big((368880\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{4}\!+893272\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+541040\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+89181\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+2592)a^{2}

\displaystyle+110832\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{4}\!+161368\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+52976\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+3645\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\big),

\displaystyle+664992\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{4}\!+1210260\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+529760\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+54675\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\big),

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{5,7}=-\frac{ab^{3}\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}}{648}\big((368880\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{4}\!+1339908\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+1352600\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+445905\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+38880)a^{2}

\displaystyle+110832\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{4}\!+242052\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+132440\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+18225\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\big),

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{5,8}=\frac{ab^{3}\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}}{1296}\big((1475520\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{4}\!+6252904\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+7574560\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+3121335\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\!+362880)a^{2}

\displaystyle+443328\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{4}\!+1129576\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}\!+741664\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}\!+127575\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\big),\dotsc.

Perusing these formulae, one can deduce several interesting properties of the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}
;
for example,

\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}=b^{k}P_{k,m}(a,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}),

(C.6)

where
P_{k,m}(a,\tilde{c}_{-1,3})
is a polynomial with rational coefficients; moreover, the following properties
can be conjectured.

Conjecture C.1
.

For
k,m,n\in\mathbb{N}:

(1)

a=0
is a first-order zero of the polynomials
P_{2n-1,m}(a,\tilde{c}_{-1,3})
and

P_{2n,m}(0,\tilde{c}_{-1,3})\neq 0;

(2)

for
m\geqslant k+3
,
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}=0
is a zero of order
m-k-2
for the polynomials

P_{k,m}(a,\tilde{c}_{-1,3});

(3)

\deg P_{k,m}=k+m-1
,
\deg_{a}P_{k,m}=k
, and
\deg_{\tilde{c}_{-1,3}}P_{k,m}=m-1
.

We, in fact, calculated the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{k,m}
for levels
k=4
and
5
up to
m=9
and
7
,
respectively; these calculations support the conjectures (1)--(3) made above.
38
38

38

The time required for the calculation of all the coefficients mentioned in this sentence on the new notebook
computer is approximately 2 minutes and 10 seconds. Our attempt to calculate the coefficients of
level
k=8
up to
m=10
, however, failed, so we halted the calculation after roughly 1 hour and 30 minutes,
when almost 4Gb of RAM was used. Note that, to
calculate
\tilde{c}_{15,10}
, one has to calculate the coefficients of the previous level
7
up to
m=12
,
of the level
6
up to
m=14
, etc.

The formulae presented above allow the reader to posit further conjectures regarding the properties of the
polynomials
P_{k,m}
.

We would like to draw the reader’s attention to conjecture (2) above for the polynomials

P_{k,m}(a,\tilde{c}_{-1,3})
, because it is the only—proposed—case for which all the levels
k
in the
expansion (
C.1
) become finite! In Section
5

(cf. Corollary
5.1
), we found the monodromy data of the solution corresponding to
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}=0
.
At the end of Subsection
C.2
below, we explain how the hypothesis
of the finiteness of the levels follows from the construction of the super-generating function; see the discussion
below Conjecture
C.2
.

At the very least, we expect that some of the properties mentioned above could be proved with the help of the
recurrence relation for the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}
via mathematical induction; this recurrence relation,
however, is quite cumbersome, and such considerations would lead us far too astray from our current goals.
In particular, it is important to prove that the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{k,m}
are defined for all values of the
parameters
a,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}\in\mathbb{C}
, and
b>0
. For this purpose, it is sufficient to prove the
property (
C.6
). First, we establish the
b
-dependence
of the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}
. Recall that
b>0
is a parameter that can be removed from
equation (
1.1
) via scaling, which, for
\varepsilon=1
, reads

u(\tau)=\sqrt{b}\,u_{1}(\tau_{1}),\qquad\tau_{1}=\sqrt{b}\,\tau,

(C.7)

where
u_{1}(\tau_{1})
is a solution of equation (
1.1
) for
b=1
and
u(\tau)
solves equation (
1.1
)
for arbitrary
b>0
.
To make the distinction between the coefficients that correspond to
b=1
and those that correspond to generic values
of
b
, we denote them, respectively, as
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{1}
and
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{b}
. Now, we write the
asymptotics (
C.1
) for the function
u_{1}(\tau_{1})
, and then perform a re-scaling of the
function
u(\tau)
(cf. equation (
C.7
)); then,
expanding as
\tau\to 0
,

(\ln\sqrt{b}+\ln\tau)^{-k}=(\ln\tau)^{-k}-k\ln\sqrt{b}\,(\ln\tau)^{-k-1}+\frac{k(k+1)}{2}\ln^{2}\sqrt{b}\,(\ln\tau)^{-k-2}-\ldots,\quad k\in\mathbb{Z},

and comparing the coefficients, we get

\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}^{b}=b^{k}\big(\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}^{1}-(m-1)\ln\sqrt{b}\,\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m-1}^{1}+\frac{(m-2)(m-1)}{2}\ln^{2}\sqrt{b}\,\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m-2}^{1}+\ldots\big).

(C.8)

At first glance, it appears that there is a contradiction with equation (
C.6
), because
we get a polynomial depending not only on
a
and
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}
, but also on (
\ln\sqrt{b}
)! This, surely,
seems wrong, because equation (
1.1
) depends quadratically on
b
, and thus, by substituting into it the
ansatz (
C.1
), we get that, in the worst case, the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}
are
rational functions of
b
. The resolution of this apparent visual contradiction is to express all of our coefficients
in terms of
a
and
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{b}
(note that, in equation (
C.6
), we have, in fact,

\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{b}
, and not
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{1}
), because
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{1}
is independent of
b
while

\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{b}
may be
b
-dependent.
This observation proves the
b
-dependence of equation (
C.6
), because it shows that
the polynomial of
\ln\sqrt{b}
inside the parentheses in equation (
C.8
) is,
in fact, of order zero, and we can rewrite it in the form
\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}=b^{k}P_{k,m}(a,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{b})
,
where, at this juncture, we cannot conclude that the function
P_{k,m}
is necessarily a polynomial, but can claim
that it is some rational function of its variables.

For the reader who is perplexed by this proof, we present a couple of examples showing how the mechanism of the
ln-cancellation works. A surprise appears at the stage that, by definition, both
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{1}
and

\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{b}
are defined in the same way, namely, as
\mathbb{C}
-valued parameters, that is,
they coincide at first glance. Recall that
\tilde{c}_{-1,2}^{b}=\tilde{c}_{-1,2}^{1}=-1/4
: they coincide because

\tilde{c}_{-1,0}=\tilde{c}_{-1,1}=0
, independent of the value of
b
.
To find
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{b}
, we refer to equation (
C.8
) for
k=0
and
m=3
:

\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{b}=\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{1}-2\ln(\sqrt{b})\,\tilde{c}_{-1,2}^{1}=\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{1}+\frac{1}{2}\ln\sqrt{b}.

(C.9)

Now, let’s check equation (
C.8
) for the next value,
m=4
:

\tilde{c}_{-1,4}^{b}=\tilde{c}_{-1,4}^{1}-3\ln\sqrt{b}\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{1}+3\ln^{2}\sqrt{b}\,\tilde{c}_{-1,2}=-3(\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{1})^{2}-3\ln\sqrt{b}\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{1}-\frac{3}{4}\ln^{2}\sqrt{b}=-3(\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{b})^{2},

where, in the last calculation, we used equation (
C.2
) for
b=1
and
m=4
. Consider one more
example for
k=1
: setting
m=0
,
m=1
, and
m=2
successively in equation (
C.8
),
we get

\tilde{c}_{1,0}^{b}=b\,\tilde{c}_{1,0}^{1},\quad\tilde{c}_{1,1}^{b}=b\,\tilde{c}_{1,1}^{1},\;\;\mathrm{and}\;\;\tilde{c}_{1,2}^{b}=b(\tilde{c}_{1,2}^{1}-\ln\sqrt{b}\,c_{1,1}^{1});

since the first two equations demonstrate the correct dependence of the coefficients on
b
, let us consider the third
one, namely,

\tilde{c}_{1,2}^{b}=b(a(2\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{1}+1)-\ln\sqrt{b}\,\tilde{c}_{1,1}^{1})=b\big(2a(\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{b}-\frac{1}{2}\ln\sqrt{b})+a-\ln\sqrt{b}\,\tilde{c}_{1,1}^{1}\big)=ab(2\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{b}+1),

where, to obtain the first equation, we used the third equation in the first line of the list (
C.3
)
for
b=1
, to get the second equation, we employed the relation (
C.9
), and, finally, the second
equation in the first line of the list (
C.3
), that is,
\tilde{c}_{1,1}^{1}=-a
, provided the
cancellation of the
\ln
-terms.

We revert back to our original notation and prove that the function
P_{k,m}(a,\tilde{c}_{-1,3})
is a polynomial
of two variables. In principle, this fact can be established by appealing to the recurrence relation for the
coefficients
\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}
; but, because this relation is complicated, we are not going to use its explicit
form in this work, and will, therefore, exploit only those properties of this relation that are pertinent to
the current proof.

To derive the recurrence relation, multiply both sides of equation (
1.1
) by
\tau^{3}(u(\tau))^{2}
and
substitute for
u(\tau)
its asymptotic expansion (
C.1
); then, for
k=0,1,2,\ldots
,
collect, successively, the “coefficients” of like powers of
\tau^{2k}
. These “coefficients” are, in fact,
series of powers of
\ln\tau
, the coefficients of which are
\tau
-independent polynomials of the

\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}
’s.

Consider what happens for
k=0
. The first non-trivial coefficient corresponds to the term
(\ln\tau)^{-6}
, and
equals
8\tilde{c}_{-1,2}^{3}+2\tilde{c}_{-1,2}^{2}
, which consists of two contributions: the first one stems from the
term
8\tau^{3}(u(\tau))^{3}
on the right-hand side of this equation, and the second one originates from its
differential part. Setting
8\tilde{c}_{-1,2}^{3}+2\tilde{c}_{-1,2}^{2}=0
, it follows that the only solution allowing
one to develop a non-trivial expansion (
C.1
) is
\tilde{c}_{-1,2}=-1/4
. Then, proceeding
to the subsequent power of
\ln\tau
, we get
(24\tilde{c}_{-1,2}^{2}+6\tilde{c}_{-1,2})\tilde{c}_{-1,3}=0
, which
implies that
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}
is a complex parameter. The following terms, for
m>3
, read:

(24\tilde{c}_{-1,2}^{2}+(m(m-3)+6)\tilde{c}_{-1,2})\tilde{c}_{-1,m}\equiv m(m-3)\tilde{c}_{-1,2}\tilde{c}_{-1,m}=f_{-1,m},

(C.10)

where

f_{-1,m}
is a polynomial with integer coefficients of the variables
\tilde{c}_{-1,m^{\prime}}
for
m^{\prime}<m
and the
parameters
a
and
b
. The last statement is apparent because the transformed equation (
1.1
) is a
polynomial with integer coefficients in terms of
u(\tau)
, its derivatives, and the parameters
a
and
b
, and,
at the same time, the ansatz (
C.1
) does not have any coefficients
\tilde{c}_{-1,m}

in the denominator.
Solving equation (
C.10
) successively for
m=4,5,\ldots
, one arrives at the
formulae (
C.2
). Then, we continue this procedure for the higher levels
k=1,2,\ldots
, and,
in this way, obtain equations of the form

(2k)^{2}\tilde{c}_{-1,2}\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}=f_{k,m},

(C.11)

where
f_{k,m}
is a polynomial of the “lower-order” coefficients
\tilde{c}_{2k^{\prime}-1,m^{\prime}}
,
k^{\prime}<k
and

m^{\prime}\leqslant m+2(k-k^{\prime})
, and the parameters
a
and
b
. Note that,
in equation (
C.11
), since the coefficient
(2k)^{2}c_{-1,2}=-k^{2}\neq 0
for all
m
,
it is clear that all the
\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}
’s are polynomials with rational coefficients of the
variables
a
,
b
, and
\tilde{c}_{-1,3}
.

C.2
Super-Generating Function for the Irregular Logarithmic Asymptotics

In contrast to the expansions (
A.1
) and (
B.1
) studied in the
previous appendices, the asymptotic expansion (
C.1
) has infinite levels, as a result of
which, we present in this subsection the super-generating function that computes the coefficients of the levels.
The construction of the super-generating function for the coefficients of the
expansion (
C.1
), though similar to those presented in
Subsections
A.2
and
B.2
, is simpler:

\tilde{A}(x,y)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}y^{k}\tilde{A}_{k}(x),

(C.12)

where the coefficient functions
\tilde{A}_{k}(x)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
, are generating functions for
the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}
:

\tilde{A}_{k}(x)=\sum_{m=-2\lfloor k/2\rfloor}^{\infty}\tilde{c}_{2k-1,m}x^{k},\qquad k\geqslant 0.

(C.13)

Define the linear differential operator
\tilde{D}
acting in the space of formal power series of two variables

x
and
y
as follows:

\tilde{D}=-x^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+2y\frac{\partial}{\partial y};

(C.14)

then, the function
\tilde{A}\equiv\tilde{A}(x,y)
solves the PDE

\tilde{D}^{2}(\ln\tilde{A})=-8\tilde{A}+2a\frac{by}{\tilde{A}}+\left(\frac{by}{\tilde{A}}\right)^{2}.

(C.15)

Assuming that a proper solution of the PDE (
C.15
) is constructed, one can obtain the solution of
the ODE (
1.1
) with the help of the relation

u(\tau)=\frac{1}{\tau}\tilde{A}\big(1/\ln\tau,\tau^{2}\big).

(C.16)

What, then, is the proper solution? The function
\tilde{A}(x,y)
(cf. expansion (
C.12
))
is a formal solution of equation (
C.15
) with generating functions
\tilde{A}_{k}(x)
that are
rational functions of
x
, and the function
\tilde{A}_{0}(x)
is normalized by the small-
x

expansion (
C.13
) with
k=0
,
\tilde{c}_{-1,0}=\tilde{c}_{-1,1}=0
, and
\tilde{c}_{-1,2}=-1/4
.

Define the
x
-part of the operator
\tilde{D}
as

\tilde{D}_{x}:=-x^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x},

(C.17)

and construct the first few generating functions
\tilde{A}_{k}(x)
,
k=0,1,2,3
.

Substituting the expansion (
C.12
) into equation (
C.15
) and equating to
zero the coefficients of the monomials
y^{k}
for
k=0,1,2,\ldots
, one obtains ODEs defining the generating functions

\tilde{A}_{k}(x)
. The function
\tilde{A}_{0}(x)
satisfies the ODE

\tilde{D}_{x}^{2}\ln\tilde{A}_{0}(x)+8\tilde{A}_{0}(x)=0.

(C.18)

The general solution of this ODE reads:

\tilde{A}_{0}(x)=-\frac{C_{1}^{2}}{16\cos^{2}(C_{1}(C_{2}x-1)/(2x))},

(C.19)

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are constants of integration. To achieve our goal, we need a solution
\tilde{A}_{0}(x)
that is
a rational function of
x
. This is a special solution of the ODE (
C.18
) that can be obtained
from the general one by making the scaling limit
C_{1}\to 0
,
C_{2}=C+\pi/C_{1}
in equation (
C.19
),
where
C
is a complex parameter; then, we find that

\tilde{A}_{0}(x)=-\frac{1}{4(1/x-C)^{2}}.

(C.20)

Comparing the expansion (
C.12
) with the definition (
C.16
) for

u(\tau)
in terms of
\tilde{A}(x,y)
, we see that
\tilde{A}_{0}(x)/\tau

(cf. equation (
C.20
)) coincides with the leading term of asymptotics of the function
u(\tau)

obtained in Theorems
5.2
and
5.2
′
; moreover, the
following conditions hold:

-\frac{C}{2}=c_{+}=c_{-}=c_{-1,3},

(C.21)

where
c_{+}
and
c_{-}
are defined in terms of the monodromy data in
Theorems
5.2
and
5.2
′
. Expanding, now, the
function
\tilde{A}_{0}(x)
(cf. equation (
C.20
)) into a Taylor series about
x=0
, one proves
the general formula for the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{-1,m}
given in equation (
C.2
).

The equation for the generating function
\tilde{A}_{1}(x)
reads:

\big((\tilde{D}_{x}+2)^{2}+8\tilde{A}_{0}(x)\big)\tilde{A}_{10}=\frac{2ab}{\tilde{A}_{0}(x)},\qquad\tilde{A}_{10}:=\frac{\tilde{A}_{1}(x)}{\tilde{A}_{0}(x)}.

(C.22)

The general solution of equation (
C.22
) is

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{10}\,\tilde{A}_{0}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{\big(C_{1}x^{3}+C_{2}(3C^{2}x^{2}-3Cx+1)\big)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{2}{x}}}{(Cx-1)^{3}}

(C.23)

\displaystyle+\frac{ab}{2}\,\frac{((C^{2}+C+1)x^{2}-(2C+1)x+1)((C+1)x-1)}{(Cx-1)^{3}},

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are constants of integration, and
C
is given in equation (
C.21
).
Since we need a rational solution, we set
C_{1}=C_{2}=0
, and obtain, finally,

\tilde{A}_{1}(x)=\frac{ab}{2}\,\frac{((C^{2}+C+1)x^{2}-(2C+1)x+1)((C+1)x-1)}{(Cx-1)^{3}}.

(C.24)

Expanding the function
\tilde{A}_{1}(x)
given in equation (
C.24
) into a Taylor series about

x=0
, one shows that

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{1}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{ab}{2C^{3}}\left(C^{3}+2C^{2}+2C+1-\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{(m+2)(m+1)}{2}-(2C+3)(m+1)\right.\right.

(C.25)

\displaystyle+2C^{2}+4C+3\Bigg)C^{m}x^{m}\Bigg).

Comparing expansion (
C.13
) for
k=1
with the expansion (
C.25
),
we get that
\tilde{c}_{1,0}=ab/2
, and

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{1,m}

\displaystyle=-abC^{m-3}\left(C^{2}-(m-1)C+\frac{(m-1)(m-2)}{4}\right)

(C.26)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}2^{m-5}ab\,\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{m-3}\big(16\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}+8(m-1)\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+(m-1)(m-2)\big),\quad m\in\mathbb{N}.

The formula (
C.26
) should be compared with the coefficients
(cf. equations (
C.3
))
\tilde{c}_{1,m}
,
m=1,2\ldots 10
, that were calculated by directly
substituting the expansion (
C.1
) into equation (
1.1
).

The equation for the determination of the generating function for level
2
can be written as follows:

\big((\tilde{D}_{x}+4)^{2}+8\tilde{A}_{0}(x)\big)\tilde{A}_{20}=\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{D}_{x}+4)^{2}\tilde{A}_{10}^{2}-2ab\frac{\tilde{A}_{10}}{\tilde{A}_{0}(x)}+\frac{b^{2}}{\tilde{A}_{0}(x)^{2}},\qquad\tilde{A}_{20}:=\frac{\tilde{A}_{2}(x)}{\tilde{A}_{0}(x)},

(C.27)

where the function
\tilde{A}_{10}
is defined by equation (

). The general solution of
equation (

) can be presented as

\tilde{A}_{2}^{gen}(x)=\frac{\big(C_{1}x^{3}+C_{2}(3C^{2}x^{2}-3Cx+1)\big)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{4}{x}}}{(Cx-1)^{3}}+\tilde{A}_{2}(x),

(C.28)

where, with slight abuse of notation, we denote by
\tilde{A}_{2}(x)
a special rational solution of
equation (
C.27
) corresponding to vanishing values of the constants of integration,
C_{1}=C_{2}=0
.
This special solution coincides with the generating function for level 2. It is convenient to present
it via a partial-fraction decomposition:

\begin{gathered}\tilde{A}_{2}(x)=-\frac{b^{2}(a^{2}+1)}{4x^{2}}+\frac{b^{2}\big((a^{2}+1)C+2a^{2}+1\big)}{2x}-\frac{b^{2}}{256C^{4}}\Bigg(\big(64(a^{2}+1)C^{6}+128(2a^{2}+1)C^{5}\\
+8(71a^{2}+19)C^{4}+24(37a^{2}+5)C^{3}+4(239a^{2}+15)C^{2}+(623a^{2}+15)C+192a^{2}\big)\\
-\frac{192a^{2}}{(Cx-1)^{4}}-\frac{(623a^{2}\!+15)C+768a^{2}}{(Cx-1)^{3}}-\frac{4(239a^{2}\!+15)C^{2}+3(623a^{2}\!+15)C+1152a^{2}}{(Cx-1)^{2}}\\
-\frac{24(37a^{2}+5)C^{3}+8(239a^{2}+15)C^{2}+3(623a^{2}+15)C+768a^{2}}{Cx-1}\Bigg).\end{gathered}

(C.29)

Equation (
C.29
) allows one to derive a general formula for the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{3,m}

(cf. equations (
C.4
)). The first term in equation (
C.29
) immediately
provides us with the formula for
\tilde{c}_{3,-2}
, while the second term, after substituting
C=-2\tilde{c}_{-1,3}
,
coincides with
\tilde{c}_{3,-1}
, and, finally, setting
x=0
in the denominators of the terms in the
third and fourth lines, we find that

\tilde{c}_{3,0}=b^{2}\left(\frac{C^{2}}{4}(a^{2}+1)+C(a^{2}+1/2)+\frac{71}{32}a^{2}+\frac{19}{32}\right).

(C.30)

Substituting
C=-2\tilde{c}_{-1,3}
into equation (
C.30
), we arrive at the formula for

\tilde{c}_{3,0}
that is equivalent to the one written in the list of equations (
C.4
).
Expanding
\tilde{A}_{2}(x)
into a Taylor series about
x=0
, we find that

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{3,m}=

\displaystyle\frac{b^{2}C^{m-4}}{512}\Big(48(37a^{2}+5)C^{3}-8(239a^{2}+15)(m-1)C^{2}

(C.31)

\displaystyle+(623a^{2}+15)(m-1)(m-2)C-64a^{2}(m-1)(m-2)(m-3)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{m-1}2^{m-12}b^{2}\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{m-4}\Big(192(37a^{2}+5)\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{3}+16(239a^{2}+15)(m-1)\tilde{c}_{-1,3}^{2}

\displaystyle+(623a^{2}+15)(m-1)(m-2)\tilde{c}_{-1,3}+32a^{2}(m-1)(m-2)(m-3)\Big),\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}.

The generating function
\tilde{A}_{3}(x)
is the rational solution of the ODE

\Big(\big(\tilde{D}_{x}+6\big)^{2}+8\tilde{A}_{0}\Big)\tilde{A}_{30}=\big(\tilde{D}_{x}+6\big)^{2}\big(\tilde{A}_{10}\tilde{A}_{20}-\frac{1}{3}\tilde{A}_{10}^{3}\big)+\frac{2ab}{\tilde{A}_{0}}\big(\tilde{A}_{10}^{2}-\tilde{A}_{20}\big)-\frac{2b^{2}}{\tilde{A}_{0}^{2}}\tilde{A}_{10},

(C.32)

where, for
k=1,2,3
,
\tilde{A}_{k0}:=\frac{\tilde{A}_{k}(x)}{\tilde{A}_{0}(x)}
, with the rational functions

\tilde{A}_{k-1}(x)
obtained in the previous steps (cf. equations (
C.20
),
(

), and (

)). The form of the general solution of
equation (
C.32
) is similar to the one in equation (
